Ill-Typed Programs Don’t Evaluate DOI: XXXXXXX.XXXXXXX Conference: Make sure to enter the correct conference title from your rights confirmation emai; June 03–05, 2018; Woodstock, NY Price: 15.00 ISBN: 978-1-4503-XXXX-X/18/06
Steven Ramsay, Charlie Walpole

Abstract
. We introduce two-sided type systems, which are sequent calculi for typing formulas. Two-sided type systems allow for hypothetical reasoning over the typing of compound program expressions, and the refutation of typing formulas. By incorporating a type of all values, these type systems support more refined notions of well-typing and ill-typing, guaranteeing both that well-typed programs don’t go wrong and that ill-typed programs don’t evaluate - that is, reach a value. This makes two-sided type systems suitable for incorrectness reasoning in higher-order program verification, which we illustrate through an application to precise data-flow typing in a language with constructors and pattern matching. Finally, we investigate the internalisation of the meta-level negation in the system as a complement operator on types. This motivates an alternative semantics for the typing judgement, which guarantees that ill-typed programs don’t evaluate, but in which well-typed programs may yet go wrong.



1.
Introduction

It is natural to think of a type system as a kind of proof system, whose purpose is reasoning about the behaviour of program expressions.
In this view, the atomic formulas of the system are typings
M:A
, where
M
is a term and
A
a type and, in building a proof of a judgement
\Gamma\vdash M:A
, we aim to conclude an atomic formula
M:A
under some assumptions
\Gamma
on the types of its free variables.
Therefore, built into traditional type systems, but absent from most general proof systems, is a fundamental asymmetry: although we can conclude arbitrary typing formulas
M:A
, we may only make assumptions on variable typing formulas
x:B
.
In this paper, we argue that type theory is enriched by removing this restriction, and there are interesting practical applications.
Our
two-sided type systems
allow for making assumptions on the typing of arbitrary program expressions. In fact, they are particular kinds of sequent calculi over formulas of shape
M:A
.

The ability to reason hypothetically about the behaviour of compound terms gives us a new way to express how programs depend on their inputs.
For example, we can prove that, if
x+1
evaluates to a natural number and
(\lambda fz.\,f\,(f\,z))\,(\lambda x.\,x)\,y
evaluates to a natural number, then
(x,y)
is necessarily a pair of natural numbers:

x+1:\mathsf{Nat},\,(\lambda fz.\,f\,(f\,z))\,(\lambda x.\,x)\,y:\mathsf{Nat}\vdash(x,y):\mathsf{Nat}\times\mathsf{Nat}

We also remove the restriction that one must conclude exactly one typing formula on the right of the turnstile, with multiple formulas understood disjunctively (as in most sequent calculi). In particular, one is allowed to derive an empty conclusion
\Gamma\vdash
, meaning that the assumed typing formulas
\Gamma
are inconsistent (the unit of disjunction is absurdity). For example, one can prove:

(\lambda fz.\,f\,(f\,z))\,(\lambda x.\,x)\,(y,\,y):\mathsf{Nat}\vdash

stating that, no matter the value of
y
, the subject on the left
doesn’t
evaluate to a natural number.

Despite this, the term above
does
evaluate, i.e. reduce to a value. By introducing a type
\mathsf{Ok}
to characterise all values, we can use hypothetical judgements like the above to
prove
that terms
do not evaluate
(at all), that is they either diverge or go wrong. For example, we can prove:

(\lambda fz.\,f\,(f\,z))\,(\lambda x.\,\mathsf{pred}(x))\,(y,\,y):\mathsf{Ok}\vdash

stating that the program that applies the predecessor function twice in succession to a pair will fail to evaluate. We say that such a term, to which we can assign the type
\mathsf{Ok}
on the left, is
ill-typed
. In this paper, ill-typed is not merely a synonym for
untypable
: an ill-typed term can be typed (as one might expect from the suffix), but the type assignment is on the left of the turnstile.

As well as the familiar rules for concluding typings on the right of the turnstile, our systems also have rules for refuting assumed typings on the left.
The key to the whole enterprise is a new kind of function type, the necessity arrow
A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B
, pronounced ‘
A

only to

B
’.
The necessity arrow describes functions that produce a
B

only if
they are given an
A
as input.
It is, in a sense that we make precise, the contrapositive of the usual (sufficiency) arrow
A\to B
, which guarantees that a
B
is produced
if
an
A
is given as input.
The rule,
(AbnR)
, for deducing that a function
\lambda x.\,M
is of type
A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B
, has a premise that is symmetrical to the usual rule for
A\to B
:

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash M:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B,\,\Delta}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 17.22217pt{\Gamma,\,N:A\vdash\Delta}\mathstrut

(AppL)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,(M\,N):B\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,M:B\vdash x:A,\,\Delta}\mathstrut

(AbnR)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash(\lambda x.\,M):A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B,\,\Delta\mathstrut

On the other hand, the rule
(AppL)
shows us how to decompose an application on the left, using necessity. As is typical in sequent calculi, it is instructive to think of a left rule in terms of refuting its principal formula. To
refute
that an application
M\,N
evaluates to an
B
, it suffices to
affirm
that
M
is a function that produces a
B
only if given an
A
, and then to
refute
that
N
is an
A
.

The ability to refute that terms evaluate becomes very useful when we consider more sophisticated type systems, designed for verifying stronger, behavioural properties of terms. In such systems, one faces the same difficulties as in general program verification, namely that proofs are difficult to construct automatically and, as a consequence, false positives -- that is, where a perfectly good program is not well-typed
1
1

1

A simple example that is rejected by many systems is:
\mathsf{if}\,\mathsf{true}\,\mathsf{then}\,1\,\mathsf{else}\,(1,1)
.
– are common. This is problematic because, in practice, a lot of the value of program verification tools derives from their use as a means to find bugs, i.e. true positives.

One might argue that false positives are already a feature of the mainstream type systems, as e.g. used in compilers, and are thus evidently not too onerous (though proponents of untyped programming may forcefully disagree). However, a significant difference is that, by and large, programmers understand enough of those systems to be able to predict false positives, or at least quickly diagnose and program around them. By contrast, type systems for program verification are often significantly more complex and the success of automated tools is bound up with the efficacy of
ad hoc
heuristics.

With two-sided typing, we can obtain a ‘true positives’ theorem:
ill-typed programs don’t evaluate
in addition to the usual ‘true negatives’ theorem:
well-typed programs don’t go wrong
.
Thus, like O’Hearn’s Incorrectness Logic for imperative programs
(
O’Hearn 2019
)
, our two-sided typing provides some foundation for the use of type systems to predict erroneous program behaviours. Indeed, one of the original motivations for this work was to understand the basis for Erlang’s highly effective Success Typing
(
Lindahl and Sagonas 2006
)
.

We illustrate this in a two-sided, constrained type system designed for reasoning precisely about the shape of data structures. Following
Aiken et al. 1994
, our system has a type constructor for every datatype constructor, so that, for example,
[]
is both the term constructor for the empty list and the type of the empty list, and
A::B
is the type of all non-empty (cons
::
) lists whose head element has type
A
and whose tail is of type
B
. Using the system we can prove, for example, that the head function requires a cons with head element of type
A
in order to return an
A
, and that the map function requires non-emptiness of its list argument in order to provide a non-empty output:

\mathsf{head}:\forall a.\,(a::\mathsf{Ok})\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}a\qquad\mathsf{map}:\forall a\,b.\,(a\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}b)\to(a::\mathsf{Ok})\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}(b::\mathsf{Ok})

More precisely, the combination of
to
and
only to
arrows in
\mathsf{map}
’s type says that when given an
a
only to
b
function, it is guaranteed to behave like a function that necessarily requires a cons with element type
a
in order to produce a cons with element type
b
. Since our system can refute that the empty list is non-empty, taking the head of a list obtained by mapping the empty list is
ill-typed
:

\mathsf{head}\,(\mathsf{map}\,(\lambda x.\,x)\,[]):\mathsf{Ok}\vdash

This program, which typically shows as a
false negative
in mainstream type systems for functional programming (i.e. is reported as well-typed but actually goes wrong), is proven by our system to be a true positive: the term is guaranteed to either diverge or go wrong.

Pure Calculus Rules

(Id)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,x:A\vdash x:A,\,\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash M:B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A,\,\Delta}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 17.22217pt{\Gamma,\,N:B\vdash\Delta}\mathstrut

(AppL)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,M\,N:A\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash M:B\to A,\,\Delta}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 17.22217pt{\Gamma\vdash N:B,\,\Delta}\mathstrut

(AppR)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash M\,N:A,\,\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,f:A\to B,\,x:A\vdash M:B,\,\Delta}\mathstrut

(FixsR)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M:A\to B,\,\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,f:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B,\,M:B\vdash x:A,\,\Delta}\mathstrut

(FixnR)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B,\,\Delta\mathstrut

.
Typing rules for the pure calculus.

1.1.
Contributions

We introduce the notion of
two-sided type systems
, which are sequent calculi whose only formulas are typings
M:A
. We are particularly interested in two-sided systems for call-by-value
\lambda
-calculi with fixpoints, for which we also introduce the necessity function type.

Two two-sided type systems.

To show that the idea has both theoretical and practical interest, we formulate two distinct two-sided type systems. Both systems have at their core the two-sided rules of Figure
1
for call-by-value (CBV), pure
\lambda
-calculus with fixpoints, but we add constants according to the topic of interest. Note: throughout this paper, we will take fixpoint abstractions
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M
as basic, and define
\lambda x.\,M
as an abbreviation for the former in case
f
does not occur free in
M
2
2

2

We are grateful to one of the anonymous reviewers for this suggestion.
.

•

In Sections
2
-
5
our goal is to illustrate the new kind of type-theoretic reasoning that two-sided systems enable, and to develop some of the metatheory. To this end, we take the rules for typing pure terms with fixpoints and add constants amounting to a simple, PCF-like language.

•

In Section
6
, our goal is to show the practical value of two-sided systems, and type inference. To this end, we introduce a two-sided
constrained
type system, which can be understood as a two-sided variant of certain systems in the literature that enable the automatic verification of pattern-match safety
3
3

3

That is, that no execution of a given term can trigger a pattern match non-exhaustiveness exception at runtime.
. Starting from the typing rules for pure terms with fixpoints, we add top-level definitions, algebraic data types, and pattern matching. To make type inference as simple as possible, we formulate this system as an
intuitionistic
sequent calculus, in the sense that we restrict the consequent
\Delta
in the rules to have size at most
1
.

The meaning of the necessity function type, and semantic soundness

We introduce a CBV semantics for the two-sided type system for PCF. This semantics highlights an important difference between the meaning of the necessity arrow and all the other type operators in the system. In the absence of the necessity arrow, all of the types of the system can be thought of as defining safety properties (in a sense we make precise). However, there are choices of
A
and
B
for which
A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B
is not a safety property (Theorem
4.5
). By restricting the use of function types on the right of the necessity arrow, we obtain a fragment in which all types again describe safety properties (Theorem
4.7
). For this fragment we can prove
semantic soundness
: every provable judgement is true (Theorem
4.8
). As a corollary, for this fragment we obtain both that
well-typed programs don’t go wrong
and also that
ill-typed programs don’t evaluate
.

Complements, negation, and the success semantics

A natural question is the extent to which a two-sided judgement can be simulated using a one-sided judgement with a type-level negation. To answer this, we introduce a complement operator on types,
\_^{c}
, and show that it is inadequate, in the sense that
M:A\vdash
and
\vdash M:A^{c}
are not equivalent. This motivates an alternative,
success semantics
for two-sided judgements, in which showing that a term is typable does not preclude that the term goes wrong. Under the success semantics, from our two-sided system with complements, we can derive a one-sided (traditional) type system, which actually subsumes the original (Theorem
5.5
). A preservation argument gives
syntactic soundness
for the one-sided system, which implies that ill-typed programs don’t evaluate, though well-typed programs may yet go wrong (Theorem
5.7
).

Application to pattern-match safety, type inference, and syntactic soundness.

The second of our two-sided systems allows for precise and automatic reasoning about the shape of data structures through expressive type inclusion constraints. For this two-sided system, we describe a fully automatic type inference algorithm which, given a term and two environments as input, computes a type assignment that is principal in a suitable sense (Theorem
6.8
). This algorithm exploits the fact that our two-sided system has similar characteristics to traditional one-sided systems that admit computable type inference. By adapting the technique of
Wright and Felleisen 1994
to two-sided systems, we prove
syntactic soundness
, from which we obtain that well-typed programs don’t go wrong and ill-typed programs don’t evaluate (Theorem
6.12
).

1.2.
Outline

The paper continues by introducing the basic ideas of two-sided typing through a simple, PCF-like programming language in Sections
2
and
3
, before considering the semantics of such systems in Section
4
. The relationship between the two-sided judgement and complement types is dealt with in Section
5
, which introduces the success semantics and derives a one-sided system with a true positives theorem. Then, in Section
6
, we then turn to an application of two-sided typing, namely automatic and precise reasoning in a functional programming language with constructors and pattern matching. Finally, Section
7
concludes with a review of related work.

2.
A Language and its Two-sided Typing

We start by introducing a PCF-like, call-by-value programming language.

Definition 2.1 (Terms).

Assume a denumerable set of term variables
x
,
y
,
z
.
We consider a simple functional programming language, whose
values
, typically
V
,
W
, and whose
terms
, typically
M
,
N
,
P
,
Q
, are defined by the following grammar:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}V,\,W&\Coloneqq&x\mid\texttt{succ}^{n}(\texttt{zero})\mid(V,\,W)\mid\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M\\[2.84526pt]
M,N,P,Q&\Coloneqq&\texttt{zero}\mid\texttt{succ}(M)\mid\mathsf{pred}(M)\mid\mathsf{ifz}\,M\,\mathsf{then}\,N\,\mathsf{else}\,P\mid\\[2.13394pt]
&&\qquad x\mid M\,N\mid\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M\mid(M,N)\mid\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=M\,\mathsf{in}\,N\end{array}

We consider terms identified up to renaming of bound variables and adopt the usual conventions regarding their treatment. A term with no free variables is said to be
closed
.
A
term substitution
, typically
\theta
, is finite map from term variables to terms. We say that a substitution is
closed
just if the terms in its range are closed. We will often write concrete substitutions explicitly as a list of maplets as in
[M_{1}/x_{1},\ldots,M_{n}/x_{n}]
, or
[\vv{M_{i}/x_{i}}]
when indices are clear.

Abbreviations

It will be helpful to define some abbreviations. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we write
\underline{n}
for the
numeral

\texttt{succ}^{n}\,(\texttt{zero})
. We write
\lambda x.\,M
for
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M
when
f
does not occur free in
M
. The let construct is used to obtain the two components of a pair by matching but, in examples, we will typically always use it on the argument of an abstraction. Hence, it is helpful to define
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x,y).\,M
as an abbreviation for
\mathsf{fix}\,f(z).\,\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=z\,\mathsf{in}\,M
. We write
div
as an abbreviation for
(\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,f\,x)\,\underline{0}
, and
id
for
\lambda x.\,x
.

The language is essentially an applied CBV
\lambda
-calculus with fixpoints. Terms
M
can be tested for equality with
zero
using the if-zero expression
\mathsf{ifz}\,M\,\mathsf{then}\,N\,\mathsf{else}\,P
, and can be deconstructed as a pair using the let expression
\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=M\,\mathsf{in}\,N
. Closed values are numerals, pairs, and (fixpoint) abstractions.

Definition 2.2 (Reduction).

The
evaluation contexts
are defined by the following grammar:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathcal{E},\,\mathcal{F}&\Coloneqq&\Box\mid(\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M)\,\mathcal{E}\mid\mathcal{E}\,N\mid\texttt{succ}(\mathcal{E})\mid\mathsf{pred}(\mathcal{E})\mid\\[2.84526pt]
&&\qquad(\mathcal{E},M)\mid(V,\mathcal{E})\mid\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=\mathcal{E}\,\mathsf{in}\,N\mid\mathsf{ifz}\,\mathcal{E}\,\mathsf{then}\,N\,\mathsf{else}\,P\end{array}

Given a context
\mathcal{E}
, we write
\mathcal{E}[M]
for the term obtained by replacing the hole
\Box
by
M
. The
one-step reduction relation
, written
M\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ N
, is the binary relation on (possibly open) terms obtained as the closure of the following schema under evaluation contexts.

\begin{array}[]{ccrcl}\textsf{\footnotesize(IfZ)}&&\mathsf{ifz}\,\underline{0}\,\mathsf{then}\,N\,\mathsf{else}\,P&\,\ \mathord{\rhd}&N\\[5.69054pt]
\textsf{\footnotesize(IfS)}&&\mathsf{ifz}\,\texttt{succ}(\underline{n})\,\mathsf{then}\,N\,\mathsf{else}\,P&\,\ \mathord{\rhd}&P\\[5.69054pt]
\textsf{\footnotesize(Let)}&&\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=(V,W)\,\mathsf{in}\,M&\,\ \mathord{\rhd}&M[V/x,W/y]\\[5.69054pt]
\textsf{\footnotesize(PredZ)}&&\mathsf{pred}(\underline{0})&\,\ \mathord{\rhd}&\underline{0}\\[5.69054pt]
\textsf{\footnotesize(PredS)}&&\mathsf{pred}(\underline{n+1})&\,\ \mathord{\rhd}&\underline{n}\\[5.69054pt]
\textsf{\footnotesize(Fix$\beta$)}&&(\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M)\,V&\,\ \mathord{\rhd}&M[V/x,\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M/f]\end{array}

The terms on the left-hand side of the above schema are said to be
redexes
.
We write
M\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,N
for the reflexive transitive closure of the one-step relation.
A term
M
that cannot make a step is said to be in
normal form
. A term with no normal form is said to
diverge
.
We write
M\Downarrow V
just if closed term
M\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,V
and we say that
M

evaluates
.
A term is said to be
stuck
just if it is a normal form that is not a value. A term is said to
get stuck
(or
go wrong
) just if it reduces to a stuck term.

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\texttt{zero}:\mathsf{Nat},\,\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash M:\mathsf{Nat},\,\Delta}\mathstrut

(cR)

\mathsf{pred}\end{array}\!\!\right\}

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash c(M):\mathsf{Nat},\,\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,f:A\to B,\,x:A\vdash M:B,\,\Delta}\mathstrut

(FixsR)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M:A\to B,\,\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,f:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B,\,M:B\vdash x:A,\,\Delta}\mathstrut

(FixnR)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B,\,\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash M:B\to A,\,\Delta}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 17.22217pt{\Gamma\vdash N:B,\,\Delta}\mathstrut

(AppR)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash M\,N:A,\,\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash M:A,\,\Delta}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 17.22217pt{\Gamma\vdash N:B,\,\Delta}\mathstrut

(PairR)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash(M,\,N):A\times B,\,\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\begin{array}[]{c}\\
\Gamma\vdash M:B\times C,\,\Delta\\

(LetR)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=M\,\mathsf{in}\,N:A,\,\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\begin{array}[]{c}\\
\Gamma\vdash M:\mathsf{Nat},\,\Delta\end{array}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 17.22217pt{\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash P:A,\,\Delta\\

(IfzR)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{ifz}\,M\,\mathsf{then}\,N\,\mathsf{else}\,P:A,\,\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash M:B,\,\Delta}\mathstrut

(Dis)

A\mathrel{||}B

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,M:A\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,M_{i}:A_{i}\vdash\Delta}\mathstrut

(PairL)

i\in\{1,2\}

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,(M_{1},\,M_{2}):A_{1}\times A_{2}\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash M:B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A,\,\Delta}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 17.22217pt{\Gamma,\,N:B\vdash\Delta}\mathstrut

(AppL)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,M\,N:A\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,M:\mathsf{Nat}\vdash\Delta}\mathstrut

(cL)

c\in\left\{\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}\texttt{succ}\\
\mathsf{pred}\end{array}\!\!\right\}

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,c(M):\mathsf{Nat}\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,M:\mathsf{Nat}\vdash\Delta}\mathstrut

(IfzL1)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,\mathsf{ifz}\,M\,\mathsf{then}\,N\,\mathsf{else}\,P:A\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,N:A\vdash\Delta}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 17.22217pt{\Gamma,\,P:A\vdash\Delta}\mathstrut

(IfzL2)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,\mathsf{ifz}\,M\,\mathsf{then}\,N\,\mathsf{else}\,P:A\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,N:A\vdash\Delta}\mathstrut

(LetL1)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=M\,\mathsf{in}\,N:A\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma,\,M:B_{1}\times B_{2}\vdash\Delta\\
\Gamma,\,N:A\vdash x_{i}:B_{i},\,\Delta\;(\forall i)\end{array}}\mathstrut

(LetL2)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,\mathsf{let}\,(x_{1},x_{2})=M\,\mathsf{in}\,N:A\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

.
Two-sided type assignment.

2.1.
Two-Sided Type System

We introduce a simple two-sided type system for this language. Starting from the rules for pure terms of Figure
1
, we add rules for assigning types to terms headed by the constants of our language, and a rule allowing for reasoning by disjointness (which is discussed further in Section
2.2
). The types of the system are either the base type
\mathsf{Nat}
, or constructed using product or the two arrows.

Definition 2.3 (Types).

The
types
, typically
A
,
B
and so on, are defined by the following grammar:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}A,B&\Coloneqq&\mathsf{Nat}\mid A\times B\mid A\to B\mid A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B\end{array}

We will label the usual arrow type,
A\to B
, the
sufficiency arrow
to distinguish it from the
necessity arrow
,
A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B
. We pronounce the first of these types as ‘
A
to
B
’ and the second as ‘
A
only to
B
’
4
4

4

Strictly speaking, for the English to be correct, one should pronounce it as ‘
A
only, to
B
’, but we do not recommend it.
. We assume all arrows associate to the right and products bind tighter than the other operators.

The idea of
A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B
, which will be made precise in Section
4
, is to classify those functions which return a
B

only if
an
A
was supplied as input (hence the pronunciation). An example is the function
\lambda x.\,(x,\underline{0})
which can be assigned the type
\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Nat}\times\mathsf{Nat}
. If this function returns a pair of numerals, its input must have been a numeral. On the other hand,
\lambda x.\,(\underline{0},\underline{1})
cannot be assigned this type, because if it returns a pair of numerals, it is
not necessary
that a numeral was input.

We shall not consider subtyping for this system, but it is useful to define a notion of type
disjointness
. We will discuss the motivation for this further in Section
2.2
but, intuitively, it is a weak axiomatisation of the assertion that two types do not have any values in common.

Definition 2.4 (Disjointness).

We say that two types
A
and
B
are
disjoint
, and write
A\mathrel{||}B
, just if either (i) one is
\mathsf{Nat}
and the other is not, or (ii) one is an arrow and the other is not, or (iii) one is a product and the other is not.

Now, we come to define the two-sided type system. The idea is that the system is a simple kind of sequent calculus whose only formulas are typings
M:A
.

Definition 2.5 (Type Assignment).

A
typing formula
, or just
typing
, is a pair
M:A
of a term
M
and type
A
. The term
M
is said to be the
subject
of the formula.
A
typing judgement
is a pair of finite sets of typings, written
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
.
A judgement is said to be
provable
(or
derivable
) according to the rules in Figure
2
. We make some additional requirements on the occurrences of free variables that are omitted from the rules for typesetting reasons: in every use of
(AbsR)
,
(AbnR)
,
(LetR)
,
(LetL1)
,
(LetL2)
and
(FixR)
, we require that the bound variables displayed do not occur freely in
\Gamma
or
\Delta
.

In a traditional, one-sided type system, a judgement like
x:A,\,y:B\vdash P:C
is conventionally understood as ‘if x has type
A
and
y
has type
B
, then
P
has type
C
’. Here, we generalise this in two ways. First, we allow arbitrary terms on the left and not only variables. However, the judgement can be read in the same way. So
M:A,\,N:B\vdash P:C
should be understood as:

‘if
M
has type
A
and
N
has type
B
, then
P
has type
C
’.

Second, we allow for multiple typings (including none) on the right of the turnstile. These are understood disjunctively, so
M:A\vdash P:C,\,Q:D
should be read as:

‘if
M
has type
A
, then
either

P
has type
C

or

Q
has type
D
’.

In particular,
M:A,\,N:B\vdash
, which has an empty conclusion, should be understood as ‘if
M
has type
A
and
N
has type
B
then false’, i.e. either
M
cannot have type
A
or
N
cannot have type
B
.

However, we must take care to say something about the meaning of ‘
M
has type
A
’ within a judgement. Since our language is call-by-value, there is an asymmetry between the left- and right-hand side. On the left-hand side of the turnstile ‘
M
has type
A
’ means
M
evaluates to an
A
, but on the right-hand side, it means
either
M
diverges or
M
evaluates to an
A
. This is made precise in Section
4
, but it is useful to have some intuitions already.

For example, suppose we have some term
add
and we know that
\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{add}}}:\mathsf{Nat}\times\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{Nat}
. Then, we should expect that the following judgement is provable:

\texttt{succ}(\texttt{succ}(z)):\mathsf{Nat},\;\mathsf{ifz}\,y\,\mathsf{then}\,\underline{0}\,\mathsf{else}\,\underline{1}:\mathsf{Nat}\vdash\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{add}}}\,(y,z):\mathsf{Nat}

Intuitively, if
\texttt{succ}(\texttt{succ}(z))
is a numeral, it must be that
z
was a numeral, and if the term
\mathsf{ifz}\,y\,\mathsf{then}\,\underline{0}\,\mathsf{else}\,\underline{1}
evaluates to a numeral, it must be that
y
is a numeral too (recall that the conditional of the language requires the guard to be a numeral). Therefore,
\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{add}}}\,(y,z)
should evaluate to a numeral. This is the kind of reasoning that we are trying to capture with the rules of Figure
2
.

The rules have been divided into three kinds,
structural rules
,
right rules
and
left rules
. Typically, the rules are used bottom-to-top in order to construct a derivation. The right rules should be familiar from simple type systems, and are for concluding typings on the right of the turnstile; they only differ in that they allow for multiple conclusions. One can think of the purpose of left rules as for refuting typings on the left of the turnstile. However, in practice, they are more often used as a means to extract information from assumptions.

Structural Rules

The rule
(Id)
should be familiar. The only difference is that now we allow for multiple formulas in the conclusion, but since they are understood disjunctively, all is well.

Left Rules

The
(Dis)
rule expresses a key mechanism for refuting that a term
M
evaluates to an
A
, namely by affirming that
M
either diverges or evaluates to a value of some disjoint type
B
. The motivation for this rule will be further discussed in Section
2.2
.

The rule
(SuccL)
allows for refuting that a term of shape
\texttt{succ}(M)
has type
\mathsf{Nat}
. The only way that
\texttt{succ}(M)
may
not
evaluate to a numeral is if
M
does not evaluate to a numeral, so refuting
\texttt{succ}(M):\mathsf{Nat}
reduces to refuting
M:\mathsf{Nat}
. The rule
(PredL)
is similar.

The rule
(AppL)
says that, to refute that
M\,N
evaluates to an
A
, we can do the following. First show that
M
either diverges or requires an input of type
B
in order to produce an
A
, then refute that
N
evaluates to a
B
. Notice the combination of affirmation and refutation required in this rule.

The rule
(PairL)
says that to refute that
(M_{1},M_{2})
evaluates to a value of type
A_{1}\times A_{2}
we need only refute that
one
component of the pair evaluates accordingly.

The rules
(LetL1)
and
(LetL2)
allow for reasoning about let expressions on the left. To refute that
\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=M\,\mathsf{in}\,N
evaluates to an
A
, one can refute that the body evaluates to an
A
(independently of the values of
x
and
y
); this is
(LetL1)
. Alternatively, one can refute that
M
evaluates to a pair
(V,W)
of the appropriate kind to allow
N[V/x,W/y]
to evaluate to an
A
. We use the ‘if … then …’ reading of the judgement, in order to explain how the rule implements this strategy. The first two premises of
(LetL2)
say that, if
N
evaluates to an
A
then it must be that
x
was a
B
and that
y
was a
C
. In other words, in showing these two premises, we are showing that for
N
to evaluate to an
A
as in the conclusion,
(x,y)
is necessarily a value of
B\times C
. Then in the third premise we refute that
M
evaluates to a pair of this form.

Finally, the rules
(IfZL1)
and
(IfZL2)
are used for reasoning about the if-zero expression on the left. To refute that
\mathsf{ifz}\,M\,\mathsf{then}\,N\,\mathsf{else}\,P
evaluates to an
A
, we can refute that
M
evaluates to a numeral, as in
(IfZL1)
. Alternatively, we can show that neither branch evaluates to an
A
, which is
(IfZL2)
.

Right Rules.

The rule
(FixnR)
allows for affirming the necessity function type of a fixpoint abstraction. Otherwise, the right rules are mostly standard, excepting that they allow for the more general kind of judgement.

In sequent calculi defined over sequences or multisets of formulas, it is sometimes necessary to add structural rules such as exchange, weakening and contraction. However, since our sequents consist of two
sets
of formulas, only weakening is relevant.
We do not have explicit weakening rules, but since all the rules allow for arbitrary contexts
\Gamma
and
\Delta
on the left and right respectively, weakening is admissible.

Lemma 2.6 (Weakening).

If
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
, then
\Gamma,\,M:A\vdash\Delta
and
\Gamma\vdash M:A,\,\Delta
.

Abstractions

Recall that we treat
\lambda x.\,M
as an abbreviation for
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M
, when
f
does not occur free in
M
. In light of Lemma
2.6
, it is easy to see that the following typing rules are admissible:

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,x:B\vdash M:A,\,\Delta}\mathstrut

(AbsR)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.\,M:B\to A,\,\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,M:A\vdash x:B,\,\Delta}\mathstrut

(AbnR)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.\,M:B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A,\,\Delta\mathstrut

Each requires that
x
does not occur free in
\Gamma
or
\Delta
.
Recall that we use
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x,y).\,M
as an abbreviation for
\mathsf{fix}\,f(z).\,\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=z\,\mathsf{in}\,M
. In the following it will be efficient to use the derived typing rule:

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,f:\,B\times C\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A,\,M:A\vdash x:B,\,\Delta}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 17.22217pt{\Gamma,\,f:B\times C\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A,\,M:A\vdash y:C,\,\Delta}\mathstrut

(PFixnR)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{fix}\,f(x,y).\,M:B\times C\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A,\,\Delta\mathstrut

Example 2.7.

We can define addition
\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{add}}}=\mathsf{fix}\,f(y,z).\,\mathsf{ifz}\,y\,\mathsf{then}\,z\,\mathsf{else}\,\texttt{succ}\,(f\,(\mathsf{pred}(y),z))
.
To return a numeral, both its arguments must be numerals. First observe that, in the ‘else’ branch of the conditional, if we know that the recursive call requires a pair of numerals in order to produce a numeral, then
z
must be a numeral, we use
\Gamma
to abbreviate
\{\,f:\mathsf{Nat}\times\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Nat}\,\}
in:

(Id)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash f:\mathsf{Nat}\times\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Nat},\,z:\mathsf{Nat}\mathstrut

(Id)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,z:\mathsf{Nat}\vdash z:\mathsf{Nat}\mathstrut

(PairL)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,(\mathsf{pred}(y),z):\mathsf{Nat}\times\mathsf{Nat}\vdash z:\mathsf{Nat}\mathstrut

(AppL)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,f\,(\mathsf{pred}(y),z):\mathsf{Nat}\vdash z:\mathsf{Nat}\mathstrut

(SuccL)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,\texttt{succ}(f\,(\mathsf{pred}(y),z)):\mathsf{Nat}\vdash z:\mathsf{Nat}\mathstrut

The same can be said in the ‘then’ branch of the conditional, since the ‘then’ branch actually returns
z
. So we can use
(IfZL2)
to conclude that
z
is certainly a numeral whenever the conditional expression evaluates to a numeral:

(Id)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,z:\mathsf{Nat}\vdash z:\mathsf{Nat}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\cdots}\mathstrut

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,\texttt{succ}(f\,(\mathsf{pred}(y),z)):\mathsf{Nat}\vdash z:\mathsf{Nat}\mathstrut

(IfZL2)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,\mathsf{ifz}\,y\,\mathsf{then}\,z\,\mathsf{else}\,\texttt{succ}(f\,(\mathsf{pred}(y),z)):\mathsf{Nat}\vdash z:\mathsf{Nat}\mathstrut

Abbreviating the ‘else’ branch for brevity, we can obtain
y:\mathsf{Nat}
from the fact that the conditional branches on
y
, using
(IfZ1)
, and conclude using our derived rule
(PFixnR)
for recursive functions requiring pairs as their input:

(Id)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,y:\mathsf{Nat}\vdash y:\mathsf{Nat}\mathstrut

(IfZL1)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,\mathsf{ifz}\,y\,\mathsf{then}\,z\,\mathsf{else}\,\ldots:\mathsf{Nat}\vdash y:\mathsf{Nat}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\cdots}\mathstrut

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,\mathsf{ifz}\,y\,\mathsf{then}\,z\,\mathsf{else}\,\ldots:\mathsf{Nat}\vdash z:\mathsf{Nat}\mathstrut

(PFixnR)

\displaystyle\vdash\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{add}}}:\mathsf{Nat}\times\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Nat}\mathstrut

Example 2.8.

We can define the higher-order combinator
\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{twice}}}=\lambda fx.\,f\,(f\,x)
. It has the following property. For any type
A
, it guarantees to map all functions of type
A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
to an output function of the same type. We abbreviate
\Gamma=\{f:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A\}
in the proof:

(Id)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash f:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A,\,x:A\mathstrut

(Id)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash f:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A,\,x:A\mathstrut

(Id)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,x:A\vdash x:A\mathstrut

(AppL)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,f\,x:A\vdash x:A\mathstrut

(Id)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,f\,(f\,x):A\vdash x:A\mathstrut

(AbnR)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.\,f\,(f\,x):A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A\mathstrut

(AbsR)

\displaystyle\vdash\lambda fx.\,f\,(f\,x):(A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A)\to A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A\mathstrut

Example 2.9.

We can prove that applying predecessor twice to a pair does
not
evaluate to a natural number. From the foregoing example, we know that
\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{twice}}}\,(\lambda x.\,\mathsf{pred}(x))
is guaranteed to behave like a function that, to produce a numeral, requires a numeral as input:

\displaystyle{\cdots}\mathstrut

\displaystyle\vdash\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{twice}}}:(\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Nat})\to\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Nat}\mathstrut

(Id)

\displaystyle x:\mathsf{Nat}\vdash x:\mathsf{Nat}\mathstrut

(PredL)

\displaystyle\mathsf{pred}(x):\mathsf{Nat}\vdash x:\mathsf{Nat}\mathstrut

(AbnR)

\displaystyle\vdash(\lambda x.\,\mathsf{pred}(x)):\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Nat}\mathstrut

(AppR)

\displaystyle\vdash\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{twice}}}\;(\lambda x.\,\mathsf{pred}(x)):\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Nat}\mathstrut

Note, since our predecessor is a constant of fixed arity, we need to wrap it in an abstraction to provide it as an input.
However, a pair is not a numeral, so we can use disjointness:

\displaystyle{\cdots}\mathstrut

\displaystyle\vdash\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{twice}}}\;(\lambda x.\,\mathsf{pred}(x)):\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Nat}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\cdots}\mathstrut

\displaystyle\vdash(\underline{0},\,\underline{1}):\mathsf{Nat}\times\mathsf{Nat}\mathstrut

(Dis)

\displaystyle(\underline{0},\,\underline{1}):\mathsf{Nat}\vdash\mathstrut

(AppL)

\displaystyle\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{twice}}}\;(\lambda x.\,\mathsf{pred}(x))\,(\underline{0},\,\underline{1}):\mathsf{Nat}\vdash\mathstrut

In fact, not only does
\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{twice}}}\;(\lambda x.\,\mathsf{pred}(x))\,(\underline{0},\,\underline{1})
not evaluate a numeral, it does not evaluate at all – it goes wrong. In the next section we enrich our system with the means to express this

2.2.
Further discussion of the rules

(ZeroL)

A\neq\mathsf{Nat}

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,\texttt{zero}:A\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

(cL2)

\begin{array}[]{l}A\neq\mathsf{Nat}\\
c\in\{\texttt{succ},\mathsf{pred}\}\end{array}

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,c(M):A\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

(PairL2)

A
not a product

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,(M,N):A\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

(FixL)

A
not an arrow

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M:A\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

Figure 3
.
Alternative disjointness rules.

Notice that the
(ZeroR)
rule and the two fixpoint rules
(FixsR)
and
(FixnR)
have no corresponding rule on the left-hand side, the left rules
(predL)
and
(succL)
only concern the case when the type is
\mathsf{Nat}
, and the left rule
(PairL)
only concerns the case when the type is a product
5
5

5

This can be considered unsatisfactory because, for example, unlike when we are affirming
\texttt{succ}(M):A
in the consequent, to refute
\texttt{succ}(M):A
(i.e. on the left), it need not be the case that
A=\mathsf{Nat}
. Similar remarks apply for
\mathsf{pred}
and pair values.
.

To understand the situation better, let us consider each in turn. To refute
\texttt{zero}:A
, it must be that
A\neq\mathsf{Nat}
, so we might expect a rule like
(ZeroL)
in Figure
3
. Similarly, to refute a formula
c(M):A
(for
c\in\{\texttt{succ},\,\mathsf{pred}\}
), either (i)
A=\mathsf{Nat}
and then it must be that we can refute that
M
is a number, as in the existing rule
(cL)
, or (ii) it must be that
A\neq\mathsf{Nat}
. Hence, we appear to be missing a rule such as
(cL2)
in Figure
3
above. A similar analysis leads us to conclude that we are missing a rule for refuting formulas
(M,N):A
when
A
is not a product type, such as
(PairL2)
of Figure
3
.

All the rules from Figure
3
that we have noted as missing express a form of reasoning based on disjointness of types: one can refute
\texttt{zero}:A
,
\texttt{succ}(M):A
and
\mathsf{pred}(M):A
whenever
A
is a type disjoint from
\mathsf{Nat}
, one can refute
(M,N):A
whenever
A
is a type disjoint from any product type. It just happens that, in this system,
A
being disjoint from
\mathsf{Nat}
simply means that
A\neq\mathsf{Nat}
.

However, reasoning based on disjointness of types makes sense for all terms
M
, and not only those that are introduction forms. In principle, it should be possible to refute any typing
M:A
on the basis of affirming a typing
M:B
for some type
B
disjoint from
A
. Indeed, this form of reasoning is closely related to that embodied in the success typing paradigm
(
Lindahl and Sagonas 2006
)
. In success typing, if a function
M
can be assigned the
success type

A\to B
, then it means that the domain of
f
is wholly contained within
A
. Therefore, we can be sure that
f
fails whenever it is applied to a value whose type is disjoint from
A
.

Thus, we formulated our system with a single rule
(Dis)
, expressing a general notion of reasoning based on disjointness, instead of a number of special cases for each introduction form, as in Figure
3
. However, in expressive power, the two are actually incomparable. On the one hand, the rules of Figure
3
are applicable even when the subject
M
of the principal formula would not be well-typed. For example,
\texttt{succ}(\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{id}}}):\mathsf{Nat}\times\mathsf{Nat}
can be refuted using the disjointness rules of Figure
3
, but not using
(Dis)
. On the other hand,
(Dis)
allows for reasoning based on disjointness even when the subject of the principal formula is not an introduction form. For example,
(\lambda xy.\,x)\,2\,3:\mathsf{Nat}\times\mathsf{Nat}
can be refuted using
(Dis)
, but not if we were to take the rules of Figure
3
instead of
(Dis)
. Of course, if one is interested in the most powerful system possible, then one can throw all the rules in together.

Now, let us consider refuting a typing of shape
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M:A
. By a similar analysis, we should expect that
either (i)
A
is not an arrow at all; or (ii)
A
is a sufficiency arrow, say
B\to C
, but then there is a
B
-value
V
such that
M[V/x]
is not a
C
; or (iii)
A
is a necessity arrow, say
B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}C
, but then there is a non-
B
value
V
such that
M[V/x]
is a
C
. For (i), it seems we should expect a rule like
(FixL)
of Figure
3
, but instead we use the
(Dis)
rule, as discussed above. For case (ii), it seems we should have a rule:

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash V:B,\,\Delta}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 17.22217pt{\Gamma,\,f:B\to C,\,M[V/x]:C\vdash\Delta}\mathstrut

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,\mathsf{fix}\,x.\,M:B\to C\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

However, this rule would be unsound. Suppose we have some
B
value
V
such that
M[V/x]
does not evaluate to a
C
, and suppose, for simplicity, that
M
does not contain
f
(so the fixpoint abstraction is just an ordinary
\lambda
-abstraction). This rule would allow us to conclude that
\lambda x.\,M
is not a
B\to C
function. However, it is not enough to know that
M[V/x]
does not evaluate to a
C
, because this includes the possibility that
M[V/x]
diverges, and this is a perfectly acceptable outcome for any
B\to C
function. For example, using this rule we could refute
\lambda x.\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}}:\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{Nat}
, i.e. prove the judgement
\lambda x.\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}}:\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{Nat}\vdash
, but this is absurd because
\lambda x.\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}}

is
a
\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{Nat}
function, according to the usual understanding of this type (and our semantics in Section
4
). The situation for refuting typings of shape
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M:B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}C
, i.e. case (iii) above, is analogous.

Finally, although we formulated our rule
(Id)
rule with principal formula
x:A
on both sides, it would be sound to take the more general
\Gamma,\,M:A\vdash M:A,\,\Delta
as axiom instead. This would be more powerful, e.g.
x\ y:A\vdash x\ y:A
, but it takes us further away from traditional one-sided type systems than we want to go in this paper.

3.
The type of values and Ill Typedness

One may wonder whether there is any use in being able to reason hypothetically about typing. A compelling answer can be given once we introduce a new type
\mathsf{Ok}
, with meaning ‘evaluates’.

Definition 3.1.

We extend the grammar of types with the constant
\mathsf{Ok}
and extend the typing rules to include those given in Figure
4
.

The rule
(OkVarR)
expresses the fact that, in call-by-value, we may assume that all variables denote values. The rules
(OkL)
and
(OkR)
express the fact that
\mathsf{Ok}
is the type of all values. To conclude that
M:\mathsf{Ok}
, it suffices to show
M:A
since every type
A
represents some non-empty set of values. To refute that
M
evaluates to an
A
, it suffices to refute that it evaluates at all. Rules
(OkApL1)
and
(OkApL2)
express the fact that, for an application
M\,N
to evaluate, requires that
M
evaluates to a function (see Remark
3.1
) and that
N
evaluates. Rules
(OkSL)
and
(OkPL)
express the fact that the successor and predecessor are only defined on numerals. In combination with
(OkL)
, these rules subsume
(SuccL)
and
(PredL)
respectively. Finally,
(OkPrL)
allows to refute that
(M_{1},M_{2})
evaluates by refuting that one of the components evaluates.

Remark 3.1
.

Since
\mathsf{Ok}
is the set of all values, the set of all (fixpoint) abstractions can be represented by any type of shape
\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
. Indeed, using
(FixnR)
and
(OkVarR)
we can prove
\vdash\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M:\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
.

For example, if we use
(OkPL)
instead of
(PredL)
in the first derivation of Example
2.9
we can show that
\vdash\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{twice}}}\,(\lambda x.\,\mathsf{pred}(x)):\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok}
. Then, using
(AppL)
and
(Dis)
, we can conclude
\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{twice}}}\,(\lambda x.\,\mathsf{pred}(x))\,(\underline{0},\underline{1}):\mathsf{Ok}\vdash
.

In Example
2.7
, we proved that
add
requires both its arguments to be numerals in order to return a numeral. However, we cannot prove that it requires both its arguments to be numerals in order to evaluate. To see why, suppose the body of the function,
\mathsf{ifz}\,y\,\mathsf{then}\,z\,\mathsf{else}\,\texttt{succ}\,(f\,(\mathsf{pred}(y),z))
, evaluates for some actual parameters
y
and
z
.
Clearly,
y
is required to be a numeral since it appears in the guard. However,
z
is
not
required to be a numeral by the branches of the conditional. In particular, the ‘then’ branch yields
z:\mathsf{Ok}\not\vdash z:\mathsf{Nat}
. However, there is a good reason, it is possible for an application of the function to successfully evaluate even when the second argument is not a numeral! For example,
\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{add}}}(\underline{0},\lambda x.\,x)\Downarrow\lambda x.\,x
.
The best we can do is prove
\vdash\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{add}}}:\mathsf{Nat}\times\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok}
.

Typing Rules for Ok

(OkVarR)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash x:\mathsf{Ok},\,\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,M:\mathsf{Ok}\vdash\Delta}\mathstrut

(OkL)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,M:A\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash M:A,\,\Delta}\mathstrut

(OkR)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash M:\mathsf{Ok},\,\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,M:\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A\vdash\Delta}\mathstrut

(OkApL1)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,M\,N:\mathsf{Ok}\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,N:\mathsf{Ok}\vdash\Delta}\mathstrut

(OkApL2)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,M\,N:\mathsf{Ok}\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,M:\mathsf{Nat}\vdash\Delta}\mathstrut

(OkSL)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,\texttt{succ}(M):\mathsf{Ok}\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,M:\mathsf{Nat}\vdash\Delta}\mathstrut

(OkPL)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,\mathsf{pred}(M):\mathsf{Ok}\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,M_{i}:\mathsf{Ok}\vdash\Delta}\mathstrut

(OkPrL)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,(M_{1},\,M_{2}):\mathsf{Ok}\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

Figure 4
.
Type Assignment with
\mathsf{Ok}

Ill-typed

Some authors use the phrase ‘ill-typed’ as synonymous with untypable, but we will use it to mean that a term is provably not ok (as is suggestive of the suffix
-typed
).

Definition 3.2 (Ill-typed and Well-typed).

Suppose
M
is a closed term.

•

We say that
M
is
ill-typed
just if
M:\mathsf{Ok}\vdash
.

•

We say that
M
is
well-typed
just if
\vdash M:\mathsf{Ok}
.

Of course, due to the
(OkR)
rule, the definition of well-typed subsumes the usual one. Note that
M
being well-typed does not imply that
M
evaluates, only that
M
does not get stuck: having formula
M:A
on the right of the turnstile only means that
M
either diverges or
M
evaluates to an
A
(see Section
4
for details). On the other hand, proving that a closed term
M
is ill-typed can be understood as proving that
M

does not
evaluate:
M:\mathsf{Ok}\vdash
means that
M
does not evaluate to a value in
\mathsf{Ok}
, so
M
either diverges or goes wrong.
The following example is ill-typed only because it will diverge.
We use
\Gamma
to abbreviate
\{\,f:\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok}\,\}
:

(Id)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash f:\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok}\mathstrut

(Id)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,x:\mathsf{Nat}\vdash x:\mathsf{Nat}\mathstrut

(AppL)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,f\,x:\mathsf{Ok}\vdash x:\mathsf{Nat}\mathstrut

(FixnR)

\displaystyle\vdash\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,f\,x:\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok}\mathstrut

(Id)

\displaystyle y:\mathsf{Nat}\vdash y:\mathsf{Nat}\mathstrut

(AbsR)

\displaystyle\vdash\lambda y.\,y:\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{Nat}\mathstrut

(Dis)

\displaystyle\lambda y.\,y:\mathsf{Nat}\vdash\mathstrut

(AppL)

\displaystyle(\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,f\,x)\,(\lambda y.\,y):\mathsf{Ok}\vdash\mathstrut

4.
Semantics of type assignment

In Sections
2
and
3
, we gave some intuitions about how we want to understand the
meaning
of types and the typing judgement. We now make this precise.

Definition 4.1 (Semantics of Types).

Let
\mathsf{Vals}_{0}
be the set of all closed values.
We interpret types as certain sets of closed, well-behaved terms:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}[\![\mathsf{Ok}]\!]&=&\mathsf{Vals}_{0}\\
[\![\mathsf{Nat}]\!]&=&\{\;\underline{0},\,\underline{1},\,\underline{2},\,\ldots\;\}\\
[\![A\times B]\!]&=&\{\;(V,W)\mid V\in[\![A]\!],\,W\in[\![B]\!]\;\}\\
[\![A\to B]\!]&=&\{\;\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M\mid\forall V\in\mathsf{Vals}_{0}.\,V\in[\![A]\!]\Rightarrow M[V/x][\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M/f]\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![B]\!]\;\}\\
[\![A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B]\!]&=&\{\;\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M\mid\forall V\in\mathsf{Vals}_{0}.\,M[V/x][\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M/f]\in\mathcal{T}[\![B]\!]\Rightarrow V\in[\![A]\!]\;\}\\[8.53581pt]
\mathcal{T}[\![A]\!]&=&\{\;M\mid M\Downarrow V,\,V\in[\![A]\!]\;\}\\
\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A]\!]&=&\{\;M\mid M\in\mathcal{T}[\![A]\!]\vee M\Uparrow\;\}\end{array}

The idea is that
[\![A]\!]
is the set of all closed values of type
A
,
\mathcal{T}[\![A]\!]
is the set of all terms that would evaluate to a value of type
A
and
\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A]\!]
is the set of all terms that either evaluate to a value of type
A
or diverge.
It follows that
\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![\mathsf{Ok}]\!]
is the set of closed terms that do not go wrong. As usual, the meaning of the sufficiency arrow
A\to B
is as the set of all functions that guarantee to map each
A
value to a
B
value or diverge in the process. The meaning of the necessity arrow
A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B
is as the set of all functions that require a value from
A
in order to eventually return a value from
B
.

Remark 4.1
.

It seems there is an asymmetry between the semantics of the two arrows, but this is only because of the bias towards values induced by CBV. The two implications are equivalent to:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}V\in\mathcal{T}[\![A]\!]&\Rightarrow&M[V/x][\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M/f]\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![B]\!]\\
M[V/x][\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M/f]\in\mathcal{T}[\![B]\!]&\Rightarrow&V\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A]\!]\end{array}

The relationship between the two function types will become clearer in Section
5
, once we introduce the success semantics. We discuss the choice of CBV semantics in Section
4.2
.

A consequence of the semantics of necessity is that it can be a little subtle to express properties of interest. Given a function
\lambda x.\,M
, its membership in some type
[\![A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B]\!]
becomes trivial whenever there is no value
V
such that
M[V/x]\in\mathcal{T}[\![B]\!]
. This can occur, for example, when
M
simply never produces an
F
. Thus we have:
\lambda x.\,\mathsf{pred}(x)\in[\![\mathsf{Nat}\times\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Nat}\times\mathsf{Nat}]\!]
, because
\lambda x.\,\mathsf{pred}(x)
does not return pairs. This phenomenon can often be exploited in the proof system, using the disjointness rule. For example, using
(Dis)
we can prove
\vdash\lambda x.\,\mathsf{pred}(x):\mathsf{Nat}\times\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Nat}\times\mathsf{Nat}
.

Now, we turn to the semantics of the typing judgement. In CBV, when analysing the body
M
of an abstraction
\lambda x.\,M
, one can assume that any actual parameter that gets bound to
x
must have evaluated. Hence, in the denotational semantics of CBV programming languages, it is standard to interpret a judgement
x:A\vdash M:B
as a function from
values
of type
A
to
computations
of type
B
– where the latter may diverge
(
Winskel 1993
)
. In our two-sided system we can have terms on the left-hand side that are not themselves values, so types cannot be interpreted as sets of values, but we do want to retain this essential asymmetry of CBV. In fact, as we discuss further in Section
4.2
, the soundness of much of the system depends on it. Thus, we will define the semantics so that
M:A
on the left means that
M
is a term that evaluates to an
A
, and
M:A
on the right means that
M
is a term that either evaluates to an
A
or diverges.

Definition 4.2 (Semantics of Judgements).

A
valuation
,
\theta
, is just a closed substitution.

•

Given an atomic formula
M:A
, we say that a valuation
\theta

satisfies the formula on the left
just if
M\theta\in\mathcal{T}[\![A]\!]
. We say that
\theta

satisfies the formula on the right
just if
M\theta\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A]\!]
.

•

We say that a valuation
\theta

satisfies a set of formulas

\Gamma

on the left
, just if
\theta
satisfies each formula in
\Gamma
on the left. A valuation
\theta

satisfies a set of formulas

\Delta

on the right
, just if there is a formula in
\Delta
that is satisfied on the right.

•

We say that a judgement
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
is
true
, written
\Gamma\models\Delta
, just if all valuations
\theta
that satisfy
\Gamma
on the left, satisfy
\Delta
on the right.

To keep the development smooth, whenever we talk about the satisfaction of formulas by some valuation
\theta
, we will assume that
\theta
closes all the terms involved (i.e. its domain is sufficiently large).

Notice from the first of the three clauses that divergence is always allowed on the right, but never on the left. So, for example, valuation
[\underline{1}/x]
satisfies
\mathsf{ifz}\,x\,\mathsf{then}\,\underline{0}\,\mathsf{else}\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}}:\mathsf{Nat}
on the right but not on the left. Then, notice also the asymmetry in the semantics of the judgement:
\Gamma\models\Delta
just if all valuations that satisfy
all
formulas in
\Gamma
on the left, satisfy
some
formula from
\Delta
on the right.

4.1.
Semantic Soundness for Safety Properties

By considering the semantics of types, we can draw a sharp distinction between the necessity arrow and all the other (more usual) types of the system.
To see this, we will need to look in more detail at the behaviour of fixpoints in the system, and so we introduce the following
fixpoint approximants
(see, e.g.
Bradfield and Walukiewicz 2018
for the use of approximants in mu-calculus):

Definition 4.3.

Given a fixpoint term
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M
, the following family of terms, indexed by
n\in\mathbb{N}
, are its
fixpoint approximants
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{fix}^{0}f(x).\,M

\displaystyle=\lambda x.\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{fix}^{n+1}f(x).\,M

\displaystyle=\lambda x.\,M[\mathsf{fix}^{n}f(x).\,M/f]

Note: we are not introducing a new piece of syntax, but merely an abbreviation that will be useful in the sequel. Each approximant is simply a finite unfolding of the possibly infinite fixpoint computation. The idea is to think of a term
C[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]
containing a fixpoint as being finitely approximated by each member of the family
C[\mathsf{fix}^{n}f(x).\,M]
.

As is common, we think of types as defining properties, with a closed term
M
satisfying a property
A
whenever
M\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A]\!]
. In the theory of program verification, it is standard to distinguish between two kinds of properties, safety and liveness. The former is typically characterised by (i) being downwards closed in the termination order – that is, if a program satisfies a safety property, then so does any program that terminates less often, and (ii) the existence of finite counterexamples – whenever a program does not satisfy a safety property, there is a finite trace to act as witness. We make precise this idea for our type system in the following:

Definition 4.4.

For closed terms
N
and
P
, write
P\lesssim N
just if
P
normalises implies
N
normalises and then they have the same normal form. Then we say that a type
A
defines a
safety property
just if the following are both true:

(1)

If
\mathcal{C}[N]\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A]\!]
and
P\lesssim N
, then
\mathcal{C}[P]\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A]\!]
.

(2)

If
C[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]\notin\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A]\!]
, then there is some
k
for which
C[\mathsf{fix}^{k}f(x).\,M]\notin\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A]\!]
.

It is possible to show that, if we exclude the necessity arrow, then all types of the system define safety properties. However, with the necessity arrow, this is no longer the case. In fact, we can find counterexamples to both aspects of safety.

Theorem 4.5.

There are types
A
and
B
such that
A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B
is not a safety property. In particular:

•

The type
\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{Nat}
does not satisfy (S1).

•

The type
\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{Nat}
does not satisfy (S2).

However, by inspection of these proofs, it is evident that allowing function types on the right of the necessity arrow is a key feature. We will now define a fragment of our type system in which types are restricted so as to always define safety properties.

Definition 4.6 (Safety Fragment).

The
safety fragment
consists of restricting the two-sided system to judgements only involving the following types:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}F&\Coloneqq&\mathsf{Nat}\mid\mathsf{Ok}\mid F_{1}\times F_{2}\\
A,B&\Coloneqq&\mathsf{Nat}\mid\mathsf{Ok}\mid A\times B\mid A\to B\mid A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}F\end{array}

We call types
F
the
finitely verifiable
types, because we can show that whenever a term inhabits a finitely verifiable type, there is already a finite approximation of the term that inhabits the type. In the safety fragment, only finitely verifiable types are allowed on the right-hand side of necessity arrows. This is enough to recover safety:

Theorem 4.7 (Safety properties).

Every type in the safety fragment defines a safety property.

The fact that types define safety properties is very helpful for the purpose of analysing the system. The contrapositive of (S2) gives us a principle that we can use in order to show that term satisfies a type: if
\mathsf{fix}^{n}f(x).\,M\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A]\!]
for all
n
, then it follows that
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A]\!]
. We use this principle to show soundness, i.e. that every provable judgement is true.

Theorem 4.8 (Semantic Soundness).

In the safety fragment, if
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
then
\Gamma\models\Delta
.

This formulation of soundness is standard for a sequent calculi
(
Ebbinghaus et al. 1994
)
, but there is another, more syntactical notion of soundness that is standard in the literature for type systems, namely ‘progress and preservation’
(
Wright and Felleisen 1994
)
.
Hence, we call this result
semantic soundness
and the latter kind, which we will obtain in Sections
5
and
6
,
syntactic soundness
. As corollary, we obtain:

Corollary 4.9.

Suppose
M
is a closed term. In the safety fragment:

•

If
M
is well typed, then
M
does not go wrong.

•

If
M
is ill typed, then
M
does not evaluate.

At the time of writing, we do not have a proof of semantic soundness for the full system. However, it seems clear that one could prove a syntactic soundness result for the full system, as we do for its extension with complements in the success semantics (Section
5
) and for the constrained type system (Section
6
). From syntactic soundness (progress and preservation), one also obtains that well-typed programs don’t go wrong and that ill-typed programs don’t evaluate, but we note that these statements only concern closed terms
M
, whereas semantic soundness concerns the truth of judgements in general.

It is perhaps unsurprising to note that the system is incomplete: there are true judgements that are not provable. For example, the term
\mathsf{ifz}\,\underline{0}\,\mathsf{then}\,\underline{1}\,\mathsf{else}\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{id}}}
is neither well-typed nor ill-typed. Semantically, this term evaluates to
\underline{1}
, but we cannot prove
\vdash\mathsf{ifz}\,\underline{0}\,\mathsf{then}\,\underline{1}\,\mathsf{else}\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{id}}}:\mathsf{Ok}
because our rules for conditionals do not allow for a sufficiently precise case analysis. On a related note, one can observe that there are terms that are
both
well-typed and ill-typed: namely certain terms that diverge. The subject of the derivation at the end of Section
3
is one such example.

4.2.
Further discussion of the semantics

One may wonder if the theory would be better developed using a semantics that has more symmetrical judgements, such as call-by-name (CBN). In a CBN semantics, in order for the standard (right) typing rules to be sound, we are forced to interpret
M:A
on the left as ‘
M
either evaluates to an
A

or diverges
’ rather than, as in CBV, ‘
M
evaluates to an
A
’. Thus, the meaning of a formula in the premise and the meaning in the consequent become identical, which may seem desirable.

However,
M:A
as a premise in CBN is a much weaker assumption, and reasoning on the left becomes very weak as a result. Consider a simple judgement of the form:

\mathsf{ifz}\,M\,\mathsf{then}\,\texttt{succ}(x)\,\mathsf{else}\,\texttt{succ}(x):\mathsf{Nat}\vdash x:\mathsf{Nat}

In CBV, this judgement is true independent of the choice of
M
, and is provable using the
(IfZL2)
rule – both branches require
x
to be a
\mathsf{Nat}
, so necessarily the whole term requires
x
to be a
\mathsf{Nat}
. The reason that this is sound depends crucially on knowing that the if-expression terminates. In CBV,
\mathsf{ifz}\,M\,\mathsf{then}\,\texttt{succ}(x)\,\mathsf{else}\,\texttt{succ}(x):\mathsf{Nat}
on the left means that we can assume the if-expression evaluated to a
\mathsf{Nat}
(i.e. terminated), and hence one of the two branches evaluated to a
\mathsf{Nat}
, and whichever branch that was, it used
x
at type
\mathsf{Nat}
, and so the conclusion follows.

By contrast, in CBN,
\mathsf{ifz}\,M\,\mathsf{then}\,\texttt{succ}(x)\,\mathsf{else}\,\texttt{succ}(x):\mathsf{Nat}
on the left means that we can assume only that the if-expression either evaluated to a
\mathsf{Nat}

or diverged
. Therefore, we do not even know that execution of the guard
M
necessarily terminated. In the case that the guard
M
diverged,
neither
of the branches was ever executed. In this situation, it can be that no execution of the program actually uses
x
at type
\mathsf{Nat}
, and so it is not true that
x:\mathsf{Nat}

necessarily
follows. For example, the judgement
\mathsf{ifz}\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}}\,\mathsf{then}\,\texttt{succ}(x)\,\mathsf{else}\,\texttt{succ}(x):\mathsf{Nat}\vdash x:\mathsf{Nat}
is
not
true in CBN, a counterexample is
x:=\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{id}}}
. Therefore, the
(IfZL2)
rule for reasoning about cases on the left is unsound in CBN.

In fact, in CBN, when we assume
\mathsf{ifz}\,M\,\mathsf{then}\,P\,\mathsf{else}\,Q:A
on the left, we can conclude almost nothing about the necessary behaviour of
P
and
Q
! This prevents us from reasoning about almost any interesting program. We do not know that either of
P
and
Q
is ever executed, so we can only use the behaviour of the guard
M
to justify some conclusion. The same is true of an application
MN:A
on the left – we only know that
MN
either yielded an
A

or diverged
and thus we cannot conclude anything about the necessary behaviour of
N
or
M[N/x]
, neither of which may have been executed if the execution of
M
did not terminate. Hence,
(AppL)
is unsound. To restore soundness it seems likely that one would have to equip the system with an ability to reason about termination explicitly. An approach like this was taken by
Vazou et al. 2014
to repair a similar unsoundness when adapting the CBV
Liquid Types
system for Haskell.

5.
Complements and the Success Semantics

The two-sided type systems of this paper provide a form of negation at the meta level: to establish
M\notin\mathcal{T}[\![A]\!]
we can prove
M:A\vdash
. Since there are already a number of one-sided (traditional) type systems that incorporate a negation or complement operator on types, a natural question regards the extent to which two-sided reasoning can be simulated in a one-sided system with complements; in other words if one can internalise the set of judgements of shape
M:A\vdash
by some type
A^{c}
.

Definition 5.1.

We extend the grammar of types in Definition
2.3
by a complement operator,
A\Coloneqq\cdots\mid A^{c}
, and we extend the system of rules with the two below.

Complement Rules

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash M:A,\,\Delta}\mathstrut

(CompL)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,M:A^{c}\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,M:A\vdash\Delta}\mathstrut

(CompR)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash M:A^{c},\,\Delta\mathstrut

The correspondence between these two rules and those for negation in sequent calculi supporting propositional logic should be evident.
Intuitively, the semantics of the new operator is to take the complement of the type with respect to the set of closed values.

Rule
(CompL)
, is closely related to our disjointness rule, and
(Dis)
can be derived from
(CompL)
if the system is augmented with a sufficiently strong subtyping theory, because
A\cap B=\emptyset
iff
A\subseteq B^{c}
.

This rule is sound for our call-by-value semantics, but rule
(CompR)
is
unsound
.
The problem is that when
M:A^{c}
is not satisfied on the right by some
\theta
, it does not imply that
M:A
will necessarily be satisfied by
\theta
on the left.

\displaystyle{\cdots}\mathstrut

\displaystyle\vdash\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{id}}}:\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{Nat}\mathstrut

(Dis)

\displaystyle\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{id}}}:\mathsf{Nat}\vdash\mathstrut

(OkPL)

\displaystyle\mathsf{pred}(\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{id}}}):\mathsf{Ok}\vdash\mathstrut

(CompR)

\displaystyle\vdash\mathsf{pred}(\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{id}}}):\mathsf{Ok}^{c}\mathstrut

For example, consider the derivation to the right.
If this were sound in the call-by-value semantics,
\mathsf{pred}(\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{id}}})
would be guaranteed to
diverge
, since we would have that
\mathsf{pred}(\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{id}}})\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![\mathsf{Ok}^{c}]\!]
, and
[\![\mathsf{Ok}^{c}]\!]=\mathsf{Vals}_{0}\setminus\mathsf{Vals}_{0}=\emptyset
. This is clearly absurd, because
\mathsf{pred}(\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{id}}})
goes wrong. Thus our semantics of Section
4
does not support
(CompR)
.

5.1.
The success semantics

The key issue is that, if we are internalise the judgements of shape
M:A\vdash
by some type
A^{c}
, in the way that we have indicated, then we should expect that
M:A\vdash
and
\vdash M:A^{c}
have the same meaning. However,
M:A\vdash
can hold of some term
M
that gets stuck (goes wrong); but this is impossible for
M
that satisfies
\vdash M:A^{c}
, because the semantics of the judgement ensures that any such term either diverges or evaluates to an
A
value.

Thus, we have only the backward direction of the desired equivalence:
M\notin\mathcal{T}[\![A]\!]

iff

M\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A^{c}]\!]
.

Our approach will be to weaken the semantics of typing on the right of the judgement, so that a formula
M:A
on the right
can (always)
be satisfied by a term
M
that goes wrong.

Definition 5.2.

The
success semantics
is defined as follows. First, we define, for each type
A
, the set of closed terms that may go wrong, diverge, or evaluate to a value in
[\![A]\!]
.

\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot\,\text{\Lightning}}[\![A]\!]=\{\;M\mid\text{$M\in\mathcal{T}[\![A]\!]$ or $M$ diverges or $M$ goes wrong}\;\}

We redefine the meaning of the sufficiency arrow type to allow the body to go wrong when executing on an argument:

[\![A\to B]\!]=\{\;\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M\in\mathsf{Vals}_{0}\mid\forall V\in[\![A]\!].\ M[V/x][\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M/f]\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot\,\text{\Lightning}}[\![B]\!]\;\}

Finally, we redefine satisfaction on the right by saying that a formula
M:A
is
satisfied by
\theta
on the right
just if
M\theta\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot\,\text{\Lightning}}[\![A]\!]
.

The success semantics has the strong point that the judgements are now symmetrical in the sense of the above equivalence.
Thus both
(CompL)
and
(CompR)
are sound for the success semantics.
In fact, we will show that all the typing rules presented in Sections
2
and
3
are (syntactically) sound for the success semantics too.

A considerable disadvantage of the success semantics is that we lose the true negatives theorem
well-typed programs don’t go wrong
. Now, proving
\vdash M:\mathsf{Ok}
means that
M
may either evaluate, diverge or go wrong. In other words, it means nothing at all! We do however retain
ill-typed programs don’t evaluate
, so this system is exclusively for proving that programs behave badly. This puts this system in the same territory as Erlang’s celebrated
success types

(
Lindahl and Sagonas 2006
)
, which similarly provide no guarantees for terms that are well-typed.

However, the symmetry in the system allows for a considerable saving. Under the success semantics,
[\![A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B]\!]=[\![A^{c}\to B^{c}]\!]
– the conditions on membership in these types are the contrapositive of each other. Thus, there is the potential to simply define
A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B
as an abbreviation for
A^{c}\to B^{c}
. Then, the symmetry between
(AppL)
and
(AppR)
, and the symmetry between
(FixsR)
and
(FixnR)
can be exploited to derive necessity from sufficiency + complement:

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash M:B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A,\,\Delta}\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,N:B\vdash\Delta}\mathstrut

(CompR)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash N:B^{c},\,\Delta\mathstrut

(AppR)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash M\,N:A^{c},\,\Delta\mathstrut

(CompL)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,M\,N:A\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,f:B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A,\,M:B\vdash x:A,\,\Delta}\mathstrut

(CompR)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,f:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B\vdash M:B^{c},\,x:A,\,\Delta\mathstrut

(CompL)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,f:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B,\,x:A^{c}\vdash M:B^{c},\,\Delta\mathstrut

(FixsR)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B,\,\Delta\mathstrut

Thus, in the success system, the sufficiency arrow in combination with complements provides a complete treatment of the necessity arrow, and we could dispense with
(FixnR)
and
(AppL)
if desirable.

5.2.
The one-sided system

We can go further by exploiting a key symmetry of the typing rules that we have presented so far.

Definition 5.3 (Variable typings and type environments.).

Let us say that a typing formula
M:A
is a
variable typing
just if
M
is a variable, and otherwise it is a
non-variable typing
. Let us say that an environment
\Gamma
is a
type environment
just in case every formula therein is a variable typing.

In each of the rules we have introduced in Sections
2
and
3
, and so far in
5
, observe the following. If the conclusion has at most one non-variable typing formula, then each of the hypotheses will have at most one non-variable typing formula too.
For example, one can see by inspection of Examples
2.7
and
2.8
of Section
2
that, in each judgement of the respective proof trees, at most one typing formula is non-variable.

When a judgment contains at most one non-variable typing then, in the success semantics, it is equivalent to a judgement in which a non-variable typing is the only typing formula on the right-hand side of the turnstile. Thus, in the success type system, we are able to
normalise
the typing rules by using
(CompL)
and
(CompR)
to exchange the positions of formulas between the two sides, until they form a traditional, one-sided type system. In fact, the use of complements allows us to eliminate further redundancies, and so we obtain a smaller system.

\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 45.29878pt\hbox{}}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(Ok)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=52.5138pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 22.04192pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash M:\mathsf{Ok}\mathstrut$}}}}$}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 68.62395pt\hbox{}}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(OkC1)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=79.80273pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 31.72261pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma,\,x:\mathsf{Ok}^{c}\vdash M:A\mathstrut$}}}}$}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 31.72261pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash M:\mathsf{Ok}^{c}}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-1.58893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(OkC2)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=56.58752pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 34.74564pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash M:A\mathstrut$}}}}$}\\[14.22636pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 84.64818pt\hbox{}}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(Contra)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=103.82333pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 35.73654pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma,\,x:A,\,x:A^{c}\vdash M:A\mathstrut$}}}}$}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 56.5209pt\hbox{}}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(Var)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=71.45801pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 23.79193pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma,\,x:A\vdash x:A\mathstrut$}}}}$}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 27.04376pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash M:B}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-2.58893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 3.0pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(Disj)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 3.8806pt\hbox{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=54.61523pt,height=0.51663pt}}}\thinspace\raise-1.3806pt\hbox{$B\mathrel{||}A$}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-2.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 25.30028pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash M:A^{c}\mathstrut$}}}}$}\\[14.22636pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 54.94469pt\hbox{}}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(Zero)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=60.41656pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 27.73643pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\texttt{zero}:\mathsf{Nat}\mathstrut$}}}}$}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 45.52812pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash M:\mathsf{Nat}}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-1.58893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(Succ1)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=81.76373pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 32.52817pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\texttt{succ}(M):\mathsf{Nat}\mathstrut$}}}}$}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 40.88008pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash M:\mathsf{Nat}^{c}}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-1.58893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(Succ2)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=76.54138pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 32.52817pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\texttt{succ}(M):A\mathstrut$}}}}$}\\[14.22636pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 43.81989pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash M:\mathsf{Nat}}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-1.58893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(Pred1)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=78.6806pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 32.3615pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{pred}(M):\mathsf{Nat}\mathstrut$}}}}$}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 39.17184pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash M:\mathsf{Nat}^{c}}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-1.58893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(Pred2)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=73.45825pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 32.3615pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{pred}(M):A\mathstrut$}}}}$}\\[14.22636pt]
