\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 22.41692pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,f:A\to B,\,x:A\vdash M:B}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-1.58893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(Fix)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=122.0133pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 32.40459pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M:A\to B\mathstrut$}}}}$}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 61.10674pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash N:A}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-1.58893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(Let3)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=116.42093pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 27.33366pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=M\,\mathsf{in}\,N:A\mathstrut$}}}}$}\\[14.22636pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 42.38336pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:(B_{1}\times B_{2})^{c}\\
\Gamma,\,x_{i}:B_{i}^{c}\vdash N:A\;(\forall i)\end{array}}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-1.58893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(Let2)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=125.84686pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 27.33366pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{let}\,(x_{1},x_{2})=M\,\mathsf{in}\,N:A\mathstrut$}}}}$}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 32.64374pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash M:B\times C\\
\Gamma,\,x_{1}:B,\,x_{2}:C\vdash N:A\end{array}}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-1.58893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(Let1)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=125.84686pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 27.33366pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{let}\,(x_{1},x_{2})=M\,\mathsf{in}\,N:A\mathstrut$}}}}$}\\[14.22636pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 30.30591pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash M:B\to A}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\hskip 17.22217pt{\Gamma\vdash N:B}\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-1.58893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(App1)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=129.86743pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 64.57309pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash M\,N:A\mathstrut$}}}}$}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 30.30591pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash M:(\mathsf{Ok}^{c}\to A)^{c}}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-1.58893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(App2)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=86.49454pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 42.88664pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash M\,N:A\mathstrut$}}}}$}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 33.51202pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash N:\mathsf{Ok}^{c}}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-1.58893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(App3)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=61.33307pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 30.30591pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash M\,N:A\mathstrut$}}}}$}\\[14.22636pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 30.0698pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash M:A}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\hskip 17.22217pt{\Gamma\vdash N:B}\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-1.58893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(Pair1)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=111.22519pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 39.86136pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash(M,\,N):A\times B\mathstrut$}}}}$}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 42.76405pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash M_{i}:\mathsf{Ok}^{c}}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-1.58893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(Pair2)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=82.52762pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 30.0698pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash(M_{1},M_{2}):A\mathstrut$}}}}$}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 62.9121pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash M_{i}:A_{i}^{c}}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-1.58893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(Pair3)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=120.85141pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 30.0698pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash(M_{1},M_{2}):(A_{1}\times A_{2})^{c}\mathstrut$}}}}$}\\[14.22636pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 54.25671pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash M:\mathsf{Nat}^{c}}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-1.58893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(IfZ1)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=116.3226pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 26.01419pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{ifz}\,M\,\mathsf{then}\,N\,\mathsf{else}\,P:A\mathstrut$}}}}$}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 30.34761pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash N:A}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\hskip 17.22217pt{\Gamma\vdash P:A}\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-1.58893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(IfZ2)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=116.3226pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 26.01419pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{ifz}\,M\,\mathsf{then}\,N\,\mathsf{else}\,P:A\mathstrut$}}}}$}\end{array}

Figure 5
.
One-Sided Type Assignment

Definition 5.4 (One-sided type system).

A
judgement
of the one-sided system is a pair
\Gamma\vdash M:A
of a type environment
\Gamma
and a typing
M:A
. Provability is defined by the rules of Figure
5
. In rules
(Let1)
,
(Let2)
,
(Let3)
,
(Abs)
we require that the bound variables are not mentioned in
\Gamma
.

In many cases, the rules of Figure
5
derive from their two-sided counterparts simply by normalising the judgements involved, possibly by introducing complements. Rule
(Disj)
arises from
(Dis)
this way. Normalisation often introduces a complemented typing
M:A^{c}
in the conclusion, but in most cases it is sound to generalise to
M:A
. An example is deriving
(IfZ1)
from
(IfZL1)
.

The rule
(IfZ2)
arises in this way, and it subsumes both
(IfZR)
and
(IfZL2)
. However, the soundness of this one-sided rule is not obvious at first glance. It is almost the same as the right side rule
(IfZR)
, except that
(IfZR)
has a third premise. In
(IfZ2)
this premise is absent, and we need only show that each branch has type
A
in order to conclude that the whole conditional has type
A
–
whether or not the guard is of type
\mathsf{Nat}
. However, this
is sound
for the success semantics, because if the guard normalises to something other than a numeral, then the conditional will go wrong, and thus satisfies any success type.

The rules
(Ok)
,
(OkC)
and
(Contra)
represent structural features of the success semantics; namely that every term satisfies
\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot\,\text{\Lightning}}[\![\mathsf{Ok}]\!]
, that no term satisfies
\mathcal{T}[\![\mathsf{Ok}^{c}]\!]
and that
\mathcal{T}[\![A]\!]\cap\mathcal{T}[\![A^{c}]\!]
is empty for every type
A
.
Rule
(Contra)
is necessary in order to account for the possibility that a two-sided judgment is provable due to a contradiction among the variable typings. For example, the two-sided judgment
x:\mathsf{Nat},\,M:A\vdash x:\mathsf{Nat}
is clearly provable using
(Id)
, but when normalised to a one-sided judgement, it becomes
x:\mathsf{Nat},\,x:\mathsf{Nat}^{c}\vdash M:A
and
(Var)
is not applicable.

Theorem 5.5.

Suppose
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are type environments.

•

If
\Gamma,\,M:A\vdash\Delta
in the two-sided system, then
\Gamma\cup\Delta^{c}\vdash M:A^{c}
in the one-sided system.

•

If
\Gamma\vdash M:A,\,\Delta
in the two-sided system, then
\Gamma\cup\Delta^{c}\vdash M:A
in the one-sided system.

Example 5.6.

Consider again the judgement
\vdash\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{twice}}}:(A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A)\to A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
, which we proved as Example
2.8
of Section
2
. As in that example, we use
\Gamma
to abbreviate
\{f:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A\}
:

(Var)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,x:A^{c}\vdash f:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A\mathstrut

(Var)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,x:A^{c}\vdash f:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A\mathstrut

(Var)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,x:A^{c}\vdash x:A^{c}\mathstrut

(App)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,x:A^{c}\vdash f\,x:A^{c}\mathstrut

(Var)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,x:A^{c}\vdash f\,(f\,x):A^{c}\mathstrut

(Abs)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.\,f\,(f\,x):A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A\mathstrut

(Abs)

\displaystyle\vdash\lambda fx.\,f\,(f\,x):(A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A)\to A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A\mathstrut

We prove syntactic soundness for the one-sided system using a progress and preservation argument. In fact, progress as usually understood is not necessary – since we allow going wrong on the right, there is no requirement to show that a subject in normal form is a value, merely that values have sensible types. We get the syntactic soundness of the two-sided system under the success semantics as a corollary via Theorem
5.5
.

Let us say that a closed term
M
is
ill-typed
in the one-sided system just if
\vdash M:\mathsf{Ok}^{c}
.

Theorem 5.7 (One-Sided Syntactic Soundness).

If
M
is ill-typed, then
M
does not evaluate.

6.
A Constrained Type System

We now depart from the study of our PCF-like language, to introduce a two-sided
constrained
type system.
Our aim is to illustrate how one can take the core of an existing one-sided system, which is useful in practice, and extend it to a two-sided system, whilst retaining its good properties (such as its true negatives theorem
6
6

6

For this reason, we will use a standard CBV semantics and not the success semantics of the previous section.
and computable type inference).

Starting from the two-sided rules for pure terms with fixpoints of Figure
1
, we add type constraints, top-level definitions, term constants for algebraic datatypes and pattern-matching, and types corresponding to datatype constructors. This gives us a two-sided system whose features are similar to those of
Aiken et al. 1994
;
Jones and Ramsay 2021
;
Marlow and Wadler 1997
, which are capable of precise reasoning about datatypes and matching.

Definition 6.1.

We assume a denumerable set of
top-level identifiers
, written typically as
\mathsf{f},\mathsf{g}
, and a denumerable set of
local variables
, typically
f,g,x,y,z
. We also fix a finite signature
\mathcal{C}
of ranked datatype
constructors
, typically
c
. We will always assume that the binary pair constructor, written mixfix as
(\,,\,)
is in the signature. We consider various kinds of program expressions:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}p,\,q&\Coloneqq&c(x_{1},\,\ldots,\,x_{m})\\
M,\,N,\,P,\,Q&\Coloneqq&x\mid c(M_{1},\,\ldots,\,M_{m})\mid M\,N\mid\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M\mid\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{|_{i=1}^{k}(p_{i}\mapsto P_{i})\}\\
V,\,W&\Coloneqq&x\mid c(V_{1},\ldots,V_{n})\\
\mathcal{M}&\Coloneqq&\epsilon\mid f=M;\,\mathcal{M}\end{array}

As usual, we identify terms up to renaming of bound variables.
We consider a term to be
closed
just if it contains no free variables (but it may contain top-level function identifiers).
As before, we consider
\lambda x.\,M
as an abbreviation for
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M
when
f
is not free in
M
.

We make the following additional requirements: (i) in a pattern
c(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
the arity of
c
is
n
and the
x_{i}
are pairwise distinct, (ii) in a pattern-match term
\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{|_{i=1}^{k}(p_{i}\mapsto P_{i})\}
, the family
(p_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
is orthogonal (i.e. for every distinct
i
and
j
,
p_{i}
and
p_{j}
are headed by distinct constructors) and
all
bound variables throughout the family have distinct names, (iii) in module
f_{1}=M_{1};\,\ldots;\,f_{n}=M_{n}
, each
f_{i}
is distinct (there are not two definitions for the same top-level identifier).

Thus, we will feel free to index pattern families by the set of constructors, say
I
, that head their alternatives
|_{c\in I}\,c(\vv{x_{c}})
, and sometimes treat a module
\mathcal{M}
as a partial map from identifiers to terms.

The first category are the
patterns
, which are required to be
simple
in the sense of being shallow and constructor headed.
The second category are the
terms
. As in our PCF-like language, we have an applied
\lambda
-calculus with fixpoints, but this time we are interested in datatype constructors and pattern matching and thus we have constants accordingly. Thirdly, we have
modules

\mathcal{M}
, which are just sequences of definitions for top-level identifiers. Finally,
values
are either variables, fixpoint abstractions or datatype constructor terms built entirely from values.

Remark 6.1
.

The requirement for simple matching is to ease the presentation. We could allow arbitrary patterns at the expense of more complex subtype and consistency checking. Since this is not the focus of our work and is anyway well-covered elsewhere, we will be content with the simple case. Note also that the rank (arity) of a constructor is built into the syntax, so we will never consider the possibility of supplying a constructor with an inappropriate number of arguments since it is not considered a well-formed term.

Example 6.2.

Let us assume a constructor signature
\mathcal{C}
, which, in addition to the binary pair constructor
(\,,\,)
, contains the binary list constructor ‘cons’, written infix
::
, and the nullary empty list constructor ‘nil’, written
[]
. We can define the
head
and
map
functions by:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathsf{head}&=&\lambda xs.\,\mathsf{match}\,xs\,\mathsf{with}\,\{y::ys\mapsto y\}\\
\mathsf{map}&=&\mathsf{fix}\,m(f).\,\lambda xs.\,\mathsf{match}\,xs\,\mathsf{with}\,\{[]\mapsto[]\mid y::ys\mapsto f\,y::m\,f\,ys\}\end{array}

As before, we adopt a call-by-value reduction strategy and reuse the notation
7
7

7

Actually the one-step relation is parametrised by a module
\mathcal{M}
, but we leave this implicit.

M\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ N
. The sequence of arguments to a datatype constructor is evaluated from left to right. Due to the requirement for shallow and orthogonal patterns, matching can be executed by simply indexing into the pattern family. A full definition is available in Appendix
E
.

6.1.
Types and type constraints

Our type system is designed for reasoning about the shape of datatype constructions. It is a
constrained type system
(see e.g.
(
Odersky et al. 1999
)
), so each judgement is parametrised by a set of type constraints
C
, which restrict the possible instantiations of type variables.

Our types are a cut-down version of the types defined in the constrained type system of
Aiken et al. 1994
, in that for each datatype constructor
c
of arity
n
, we can form a corresponding constructor type
c(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})
. Intuitively, this type represents the set of all values of shape
c(V_{1},\ldots,V_{n})
, where each
V_{i}
has type
A_{i}
. We allow for universal polymorphism through the construction of
constrained type schemes
,
\forall\vv{a}.\,C\Rightarrow A
. Intuitively, a top-level function that has a such a scheme is guaranteed to behaves like
A[\vv{B}/\vv{a}]
for each instantiation
\vv{B}
of the type variables
\vv{a}
that satisfy the constraints
C
.

Definition 6.3.

We assume a denumerable collection of
type variables

a
,
b
and so on. The types are stratified as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rrcl}\textsf{\footnotesize(Sum Types)}&K&\Coloneqq&\Sigma_{i=1}^{k}c_{i}(\vv{A_{i}})\\
\textsf{\footnotesize(Monotypes)}&A,\,B&\Coloneqq&a\mid K\mid\mathsf{Ok}\mid A\to B\mid A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B\\
\textsf{\footnotesize(Type Schemes)}&S&\Coloneqq&A\mid\forall\vv{a}.\,C\Rightarrow A\end{array}

Here
C
(and sometimes
D
) is a finite set of
type constraints
(also
subtype formulas
), each of shape
A\sqsubseteq B
(i.e. between monotypes). We write
A\equiv B
as an abbreviation for the two constraints
A\sqsubseteq B
and
B\sqsubseteq A
. We identify type schemes up to the renaming of bound type variables and we assume that arrows associate to the right. We require that type schemes are
closed
in the sense that they have no free type variables.

We consider the sum type
\Sigma_{i=1}^{k}c_{i}(\vv{A_{i}})
as a finite set
\{c_{1}(\vv{A_{1}}),\ldots,c_{k}(\vv{A_{k}})\}
, and we require that the elements are orthogonal in the sense that
c_{i}=c_{j}
implies
i=j
. See also Remark
6.1
. In examples, We will often write a particular sum type
\Sigma_{i=1}^{3}\,c_{i}(\vv{A_{i}})
as a list of summands
c_{1}(\vv{A_{1}})+c_{2}(\vv{A_{2}})+c_{3}(\vv{A_{3}})
. In particular, when the sum is a singleton
\Sigma_{i=1}^{1}c_{i}(\vv{A_{i}})
, which is quite typical, we just write
c_{1}(\vv{A_{1}})
.

We don’t have an explicit notion of recursive types with which to type recursively defined data structures. However, as is well known, our constructor types together with (recursive) type constraints can capture the same notion implicitly. For example, our
map
function from Example
6.2
can be assigned the type:

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{map}:\forall a\,b\,\ell_{a}\,\ell_{b}.\,C\Rightarrow(a\to b)\to\ell_{a}\to\ell_{b}\\
\qquad\text{where}\;C=\{\;\ell_{a}\equiv[]+(a::\ell_{a}),\,\ell_{b}\equiv[]+(b::\ell_{b})\;\}\end{array}

This type says that
\mathsf{map}
takes a function from
a
to
b
and a list of
a
and returns a list of
b
. Intuitively, the type ‘list of
a
’ is described by the type variable
\ell_{a}
under the constraint that
\ell_{a}\equiv[]+(a::\ell_{a})
and similarly for ‘list of
b
’ with
\ell_{b}
constrained so that
\ell_{b}\equiv[]+(b::\ell_{b})
.

\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 63.71257pt\hbox{}}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(IdS)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=87.25803pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 23.08359pt\hbox{$\displaystyle C,\,A\sqsubseteq B\vdash A\sqsubseteq B\mathstrut$}}}}$}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 24.33362pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{C\vdash A_{1}\sqsubseteq A_{2}}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\hskip 17.22217pt{C\vdash A_{2}\sqsubseteq A_{3}}\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-1.58893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(TrS)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=131.33588pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 59.97311pt\hbox{$\displaystyle C\vdash A_{1}\sqsubseteq A_{3}\mathstrut$}}}}$}\\[14.22636pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 25.68086pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{C\vdash A^{\prime}\sqsubseteq A}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\hskip 17.22217pt{C\vdash B\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}}\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-1.58893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(ToS)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=122.95377pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 40.16763pt\hbox{$\displaystyle C\vdash A\to B\sqsubseteq A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}\mathstrut$}}}}$}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 23.86139pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{C\vdash A\sqsubseteq A^{\prime}}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\hskip 17.22217pt{C\vdash B^{\prime}\sqsubseteq B}\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-1.58893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(FrS)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=122.95377pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 40.01474pt\hbox{$\displaystyle C\vdash A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B\sqsubseteq A^{\prime}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B^{\prime}\mathstrut$}}}}$}\\[14.22636pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 51.68149pt\hbox{}}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(OkS)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=55.83463pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 26.7642pt\hbox{$\displaystyle C\vdash A\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Ok}\mathstrut$}}}}$}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 55.71649pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{C\vdash A_{i,c}\sqsubseteq B_{i,c}\;(\forall c\in I,\forall i\in[1..n_{c}])}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-1.68544pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 2.09651pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(SmS)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 2.97711pt\hbox{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=222.18878pt,height=0.51663pt}}}\thinspace\raise-1.6174pt\hbox{$I\subseteq J$}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.68544pt\hbox{\hskip 27.80588pt\hbox{$\displaystyle C\vdash\Sigma_{c\in I}\,c(A_{1,c},\ldots,A_{n_{c},c})\sqsubseteq\Sigma_{d\in J}\,d(B_{1,d},\ldots,B_{n_{b},b})\mathstrut$}}}}$}\end{array}

Figure 6
.
Constrained subtyping.

In a constrained type system, it is usual to define subtyping with respect to a context containing subtyping formulas, and so we have the following.

Definition 6.4.

A
subtyping judgement
is a triple
C\vdash A\sqsubseteq B
in which
C
is a set of type constraints and
A\sqsubseteq B
is a type constraint. Provability is defined using the rules of Figure
6
. We extend the notion of provability to sets of constraints, writing
C\vdash C^{\prime}
just if
C\vdash A\sqsubseteq B
for every
A\sqsubseteq B\in C^{\prime}
.

The rule
(IdS)
allows for justification with respect to the context, and the rule
(TrS)
ensures closure under transitivity of subtyping. Rule
(ToS)
gives the familiar relationship between sufficiency arrow types and
(FrS)
describes the dual relationship between necessity arrow types. Rule
(OkS)
puts
\mathsf{Ok}
at the top of the subtype ordering. Finally, the rule
(SmS)
allows for subtyping between sum types. It says that a sum type
K_{1}
is a subtype of
K_{2}
just if whenever
c(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})
is a summand of
K_{1}
, then there is a summand of shape
c(B_{1},\ldots,B_{n})
in
K_{2}
and, moreover, the argument types are covariantly related. For example, using the constructors from Example
6.2
, we have
\vdash[]\sqsubseteq[]+(\mathsf{Ok}::\mathsf{Ok})
and
a\sqsubseteq b\vdash(a::[])\sqsubseteq[]+(b::[])
.

Structural

(VarK)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash x:\mathsf{Ok}\mathstrut

(GVar)

\Gamma\vdash C[\vv{B}/\vv{a}]

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,f:\forall\vv{a}.C\Rightarrow\,A\vdash f:A[\vv{B}/\vv{a}]\mathstrut

(Id)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,x:A\vdash x:A\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,M:B\vdash\Delta}\mathstrut

(SubL)

\Gamma\vdash A\sqsubseteq B

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,M:A\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash M:B}\mathstrut

(SubR)

\Gamma\vdash B\sqsubseteq A

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash M:A\mathstrut

Functions

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,f:A\to B,\,x:A\vdash M:B}\mathstrut

(FixsR)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M:A\to B\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,f:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B,\,M:B\vdash x:A}\mathstrut

(FixnR)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash M:B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 17.22217pt{\Gamma,\,N:B\vdash\Delta}\mathstrut

(AppL)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,M\,N:A\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash M:B\to A}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 17.22217pt{\Gamma\vdash N:B}\mathstrut

(AppR)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash M\,N:A\mathstrut

Constructors

\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 95.0334pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,M_{i}:A_{i}\vdash\Delta}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-1.58893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(CnsL)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=195.86072pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 29.31424pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma,\,c(M_{1},...\,,M_{m}):c(A_{1},...\,,A_{m})+K\vdash\Delta\mathstrut$}}}}$}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 73.1929pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash M_{i}:A_{i}\;(\forall i)}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-1.58893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(CnsR)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=161.6525pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 30.18924pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash c(M_{1},...\,,M_{m}):c(A_{1},...\,,A_{m})\mathstrut$}}}}$}\end{array}

Matching

\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 32.69482pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash M:\Sigma_{i=1}^{k}\,p_{i}[\vv{B_{x}/x}]}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\hskip 17.22217pt{\Gamma\cup\{x:B_{x}\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})\}\vdash P_{i}:A\;(\forall i)}\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-1.58893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(MchR)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=277.2999pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 96.2224pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{|_{i=1}^{k}(p_{i}\mapsto P_{i})\}:A\mathstrut$}}}}$}\\[14.22636pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 31.81982pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,P_{i}:A\vdash x:B_{x}\;(\forall i.\forall x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i}))}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\hskip 17.22217pt{\Gamma,\,(M,P_{i}):(p_{i}[\vv{B_{x}/x}],A)\vdash\Delta\;(\forall i)}\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-1.58893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 2.8806pt\hbox{\raise-0.8806pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(MchL)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=327.03998pt,height=0.51663pt}}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 112.4397pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma,\,\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{|_{i=1}^{k}(p_{i}\mapsto P_{i})\}:A\vdash\Delta\mathstrut$}}}}$}\end{array}

Evaluation

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,M_{i}:\mathsf{Ok}\vdash\Delta}\mathstrut

(CnsK)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,c(M_{1},...\,,M_{n}):\mathsf{Ok}\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,M:\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A\vdash\Delta}\mathstrut

(FunK)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,M\,N:\mathsf{Ok}\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

(CnsDL)

A

with
c\notin I

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,c(M_{1},...\,,M_{n}):A\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

(FixDL)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M:K\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

.
Constrained type assignment.

6.2.
Type assignment

Finally, we have the two-sided type system itself. In the interests of making the syntactical soundness proof and type inference as straightforward as possible (by making the system as close to a traditional type system as possible), we present the type system as an
intuitionistic
sequent calculus, in the sense that there will be allowed
at most one
formula on the right hand side. We choose not to have an explicit component in the judgement for constraints in order to simplify the notation.

Definition 6.5 (Typing Formulas and Type Assignment).

A
typing formula
is a pair of shape either
M:A
or
\mathsf{f}:S
, with
M
a term,
A
a monotype,
\mathsf{f}
a top-level identifier and
S
a type scheme.
A
typing judgment
of the system consists of a pair
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
in which
\Gamma
is a finite set of typing and subtype formulas and
\Delta
is a finite set of typing formulas of shape
M:A
and whose size is at most 1. For brevity, by some abuse, we will write
\Gamma
even for the subset
\{A\sqsubseteq B\mid A\sqsubseteq B\in\Gamma\}
of subtype constraints contained therein.

The rules of the type system are given in Figure
7
. We additionally require that: (i) in the rules
(FixsR)
,
(FixnR)
,
(MchL)
,
(MchR)
and
(FixR)
, the bound variables in the principal subject of the conclusion do not occur in
\Gamma
or
\Delta
; and (ii) in the rule
(GVar)
, the vector of types
\vv{B}
has the same length as the vector of type variables
\vv{a}
; and (iii) the rules
(CnsK)
and
(CnsL)
require that
n>0
and
1\leq i\leq n
.

Many of the typing rules of Figure
7
are recognisable from Sections
2
and
3
, so we will just comment on new aspects. First, we have separate typing rules for local variables (introduced by abstractions and pattern-match cases)
(Id)
, and top-level identifiers
(GVar)
. Top-level identifiers can be assumed to have polymorphic type schemes
\forall\vv{a}.\,C\Rightarrow A
, so the
(GVar)
rule allows for the instantiation of quantified type variables
\vv{a}
by a vector of monotypes
\vv{B}
, subject to the requirement that each of the constraints in
C
is already derivable from the type constraints assumed in
\Gamma
.
The
(CnsL)
,
(CnsR)
and
(CnsK)
rules allow for refuting and affirming the types of constructor-headed terms.

Note that the
(CnsR)
rule allows for concluding the singleton sum
c(A_{1},...,A_{m})
, whereas the
(CnsL)
rule allows a more general sum of shape
c(A_{1},...,A_{m})+K
. This is because the former may yet be generalised to the larger type
c(A_{1},\ldots,A_{m})+K
using
(SubR)
(which would be impossible on the left, since
(SubL)
only allows for concluding smaller types from larger ones).

The rules
(MchL)
and
(MchR)
are used for typing pattern-matching on the left and right respectively. The
(MchR)
rule is relatively standard: one must choose a typing
B_{x}
for each pattern-bound variable
x
(recall from additional requirement (ii) of Definition
6.1
, that we assume all bound variables throughout the pattern-match term to have distinct names), in such a way that the scrutinee
M
is inside the sum of the induced pattern types. These are obtained by taking each pattern case
c(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
and replacing the free variables
x_{i}
by the corresponding type
B_{x_{i}}
, giving
c(B_{x_{1}},\ldots,B_{x_{n}})
. Then one must show that every branch can guarantee an
A
.

In
(MchL)
, one must first derive a necessary type
B_{x}
for each pattern-bound variable
x
, based on how it is used in the corresponding branch of the match. These types then give rise to a type for the scrutinee, as above. Then one must show that, for each branch
i
, either the desired conclusion
\Delta
follows from the fact that the body of the branch
P_{i}
has type
A
, or already from the fact that the scrutinee has type
p_{i}[\vv{B_{x}/x}]
. This disjunction is encoded by asserting that the pair
(M,P_{i})
has type
(p_{i}[\vv{B_{x}/x}],A)
so as to maintain the invariant that at most one non-trivial typing is introduced in each premise. An example of how this is used is at the end of this subsection.

Instead of a single disjointness rule
(Dis)
, in this system we have disjointness rules
(CnsDL)
and
(FixDL)
specific to each introduction form (see Section
2.2
for further discussion of these alternatives). We make this choice here to simplify type inference: we avoid the non-syntax directed rule
(Dis)
, and we avoid the need to infer disjointness constraints in addition to type constraints.

Definition 6.6 (Top-level Function Typing).

Top-level functions are typed according to the rule:

\displaystyle{\Gamma\cup C\vdash\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{f}):A}\mathstrut

\vv{a}=\mathsf{FV}(C)\cup\mathsf{FV}(A)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{f}:\forall\vv{a}.\,C\Rightarrow A\mathstrut

We write
\vdash\mathcal{M}:\Gamma
just if, (i) every top-level function of
\mathcal{M}
appears as a subject of
\Gamma
, and (ii) every top-level function typing
\mathsf{f}:S\in\Gamma
is properly justified
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{f}:S
.

Since each rule of the system allows for an arbitrary context
\Gamma
on the left, it satisfies weakening on the left. Consequently, rules
(AbsR)
and
(AbnR)
of Section
2
are admissible.
Consider the
head
function of Example
6.2
. We show that
\mathsf{head}
requires a cons with an element of type
a
as input in order to produce an
a
, i.e.
\mathsf{head}:\forall a.\,(a::\mathsf{Ok})\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}a
. The derivation starts:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{match}\,xs\,\mathsf{with}\,\{y::ys\mapsto y\}:a\vdash xs:(a::\mathsf{Ok})}\mathstrut

(AbnR)

\displaystyle\vdash\lambda xs.\,\mathsf{match}\,xs\,\mathsf{with}\,\{y::ys\mapsto y\}:(a::\mathsf{Ok})\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}a\mathstrut

Then, according to
(MchL)
we must derive types for the bound variables
y
and
ys
based on how they were necessarily used to obtain type
a
in their branch. In this case, their branch body is just
y
and so we can assign
y
the type
a
and
ys
, which is not used in the branch, must be given
\mathsf{Ok}
.
Thus the scrutinee must have type
a::\mathsf{Ok}
. According to
(MchL)
, we must show that the conclusion,
xs:(a::\mathsf{Ok})
, either follows from this or from the body of the branch. In this case, it is clear that it follows already from the type of the scrutinee. This reasoning is captured as:

(Id)

\displaystyle y:a\vdash y:a\mathstrut

(VarK)

\displaystyle y:a\vdash ys:\mathsf{Ok}\mathstrut

(Id)

\displaystyle xs:(a::\mathsf{Ok})\vdash xs:(a::\mathsf{Ok})\mathstrut

(CnsL)

\displaystyle(xs,y):(a::\mathsf{Ok},a)\vdash xs:(a::\mathsf{Ok})\mathstrut

(MchL)

\displaystyle\mathsf{match}\,xs\,\mathsf{with}\,\{y::ys\mapsto y\}:a\vdash xs:(a::\mathsf{Ok})\mathstrut

Consider the
map
function from Example
6.2
. We will show that, when
map
is given a function that requires an
a
to produce a
b
, then to produce a list of
b
requires it be given a list of
a
:

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{map}:\forall a\,b\,\ell_{a}\,\ell_{b}.\,C\Rightarrow(a\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}b)\to\ell_{a}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\ell_{b}\\
\qquad\text{where}\;\{\;[]+(a::\ell_{a})\sqsubseteq\ell_{a},\,\ell_{b}\sqsubseteq[]+(b::\ell_{b})\;\}\end{array}

The derivation begins as we have seen previously using
(FixsR)
and
(AbnR)
.
The key part is to show that, under
\Gamma=C\cup\{f:a\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}b,\,m:(a\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}b)\to\ell_{a}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\ell_{b}\}
:

\Gamma,\mathsf{match}\,xs\,\mathsf{with}\,\{[]\mapsto[]\mid y::ys\mapsto f\,y::m\,f\,ys\}:\ell_{b}\vdash xs:\ell_{a}

To use the
(MchL)
rule, we first derive types that were necessary for
y
and
ys
to produce a value of type
\ell_{b}
in the cons branch (we omit some standard right-side reasoning in the latter).

(Id)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash f:a\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}b\mathstrut

(Id)

\displaystyle\Gamma,y:a\vdash y:a\mathstrut

(AppL)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,f\,y:b\vdash y:a\mathstrut

(CnsL)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,(f\,y::m\,f\,ys):[]+(b::\ell_{b})\vdash y:a\mathstrut

(SubL)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,(f\,y::m\,f\,ys):\ell_{b}\vdash y:a\mathstrut

\displaystyle{\cdots}\mathstrut

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash m\,f:\ell_{a}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\ell_{b}\mathstrut

(Id)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,ys:\ell_{a}\vdash ys:\ell_{a}\mathstrut

(AppL)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,m\,f\,ys:\ell_{b}\vdash ys:\ell_{a}\mathstrut

(CnsL)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,(f\,y::m\,f\,ys):[]+(b::\ell_{b})\vdash y:\ell_{a}\mathstrut

(SubL)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,(f\,y::m\,f\,ys):\ell_{b}\vdash ys:\ell_{a}\mathstrut

Then, we show that
xs:\ell_{a}
is a necessary consequence of each branch being of type
\ell_{b}
. In fact, it follows directly from the induced type of the scrutinee in each (we omit the
(CnsL)
at the root).

(Id)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,xs:(a::\ell_{a})\vdash xs:(a::\ell_{a})\mathstrut

(SubR)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,xs:(a::\ell_{a})\vdash xs:\ell_{a}\mathstrut

(Id)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,xs:[]\vdash xs:[]\mathstrut

(SubR)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,xs:[]\vdash xs:\ell_{a}\mathstrut

As one final example, we show that
\mathsf{map}
, given a function that requires an
a
to obtain a
b
, returns a cons with element of type
b
only if given a cons with element of type
a
, i.e:

\mathsf{map}:\forall a\,b.(a\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}b)\to(a::\mathsf{Ok})\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}(b::\mathsf{Ok})

The key part of the derivation is again the
(MchL)
rule. Under
\Gamma=\{f:a\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}b,\,m:(a\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}b)\to(a::\mathsf{Ok})\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}(b::\mathsf{Ok})\}
, we find, as above, that the cons case requires
y
to be an
a
(left), but we don’t require anything special of
ys
(right):

\displaystyle{\cdots}\mathstrut

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,(f\,y::m\,f\,ys):\ell_{b}\vdash y:a\mathstrut

(VarK)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,(f\,y::m\,f\,ys):\ell_{b}\vdash ys:\mathsf{Ok}\mathstrut

When showing that the desired conclusion follows from the two cases, we need to use the choice offered by the pair
(M,P_{i}):(p_{i}[\vv{B_{x}/x}],A)
on the left of the second family of premises of
(MchL)
. By using
(CnsL)
to choose one component of the pair with which to continue the proof, we can effectively ignore irrelevant cases.
Here, the nil case is excluded by the type
b::\mathsf{Ok}
of the output (left), and the result follows from the type of the scrutinee in the cons case (abbreviating
M=f\,y::m\,f\,ys
and
\Delta=\{\;xs:(a::\mathsf{Ok})\;\}
):

(CnsDL)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,[]:(b::\mathsf{Ok})\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

(CnsL)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,(xs,[]):([],b::\mathsf{Ok})\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

(Id)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,xs:(a::\mathsf{Ok})\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

(CnsL)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\,(xs,M):(a::\mathsf{Ok},b::\mathsf{Ok})\vdash\Delta\mathstrut

We can now prove the example of ill-typedness in the introduction, the term that takes the head of a list obtained by mapping over the empty list will not evaluate:
\mathsf{head}\,(\mathsf{map}\,(\lambda x.\,x)\,[]):\mathsf{Ok}\vdash
.

6.3.
Constrained Type Inference

For the purpose of inferring types and, in the next part, proving soundness, we are going to restrict ourselves to judgements that are as close to those in a traditional type system as possible: they will have at most one typing whose subject is not a variable. The following definition is a variation of Definition
5.3
suitable for our constrained type system.

Definition 6.7 (Type Environments).

A finite set of formulas
\Gamma
is said to be a
type environment
just if (i) all the typing formulas contained therein are variable typings
x:A
and top-level identifier typings
\mathsf{f}:A
, and (ii) if
M:A\in\Gamma
and
M:B\in\Gamma
then
A=B
. A type environment is said to be a
top-level type environment
just if all the typing formulas are top-level identifier typings.

Inference for the two-sided constrained type system works, broadly, like Hindley-Milner constrained type inference (see e.g.
(
Odersky et al. 1999
)
), but with two notable differences. The first is that most terms will have multiple ‘principal’ types. This is because there are several shapes of proof tree with the same subject in the conclusion, but whose inferred types are not necessarily related. Hence, type inference infers sets of types
8
8

8

One can observe the same phenomenon in, e.g. intersection type systems.
.
The other main difference is that our algorithm requires both the left environment
\Gamma
and the right environment
\Delta
as input when inferring the types of a term on the left.
Hence, inference is split into two procedures,
\mathsf{InferL}
and
\mathsf{InferR}
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{InferL}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{Variable}\times\mathsf{Type})\to\mathsf{Term}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{Variable}\times\mathsf{Type})\to\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{Judgement})

\displaystyle\mathsf{InferR}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{Variable}\times\mathsf{Type})\to\mathsf{Term}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{Judgement})

The call
\mathsf{InferL}(\Gamma)(M)(\Delta)
returns a finite set of judgements
9
9

9

We formalise it this way for convenience, but in practice it need only return
C
and
A
.

C\cup\Gamma,\,M:A\vdash\Delta
that are principal in the sense that all provable judgements with the same left-environment
\Gamma
, subject
M
and right-environment
\Delta
arise as a type-substitution instance of one of those in the set, but with a possibly stronger set of assumed type constraints. The call
\mathsf{InferR}(\Gamma)(M)
acts similarly on the right.

The algorithms terminate because, modulo subtyping, every proof tree has a maximum height that is determined by the shape of the term. As usual, subtyping can be absorbed into the other rules in a so-called algorithmic system, and then inference consists of (implicitly) constructing the finite set of principal proof trees for the given term and environment(s). Inferring on the right for the term
\lambda x.\,\mathsf{f}\,x
in the environment
\{\mathsf{f}:[]\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok}\}
, we will obtain:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathsf{Ok}\sqsubseteq a_{1},\,a_{1}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}a_{2}\sqsubseteq a_{3},\,\mathsf{f}:[]\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok}&\vdash&\lambda x.\,\mathsf{f}\,x:a_{3}\\
\mathsf{Ok}\sqsubseteq a_{1},\,[]\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok}\sqsubseteq a_{3},\,a_{3}\sqsubseteq a_{2}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}a_{1},\,a_{1}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}a_{4}\sqsubseteq a_{5},\,\mathsf{f}:[]\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok}&\vdash&\lambda x.\,\mathsf{f}\,x:a_{5}\\
a_{2}\sqsubseteq a_{1},\,[]\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok}\sqsubseteq a_{3},\,a_{3}\sqsubseteq a_{2}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}a_{4},\,a_{1}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}a_{4}\sqsubseteq a_{5},\,\mathsf{f}:[]\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok}&\vdash&\lambda x.\,\mathsf{f}\,x:a_{5}\\
a_{1}\sqsubseteq a_{2},\,[]\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok}\sqsubseteq a_{3},\,a_{3}\sqsubseteq a_{2}\to a_{4},\,a_{1}\to a_{4}\sqsubseteq a_{5},\,\mathsf{f}:[]\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok}&\vdash&\lambda x.\,\mathsf{f}\,x:a_{5}\end{array}

The first three correspond, modulo subtyping inferences, to proof trees rooted at
(AbnR)
. The first corresponds to the case when the tree is then immediately closed by the
(VarK)
axiom. The second corresponds to proceeding by
(AppL)
before closing with
(GVar)
in the left branch and
(VarK)
in the right branch. The third is similar but corresponds to closing the right branch with
(Id)
instead. The fourth corresponds to a tree rooted at
(AbsR)
, after which the shape is completely determined (modulo subtyping). However, notice that the constraints returned in this last case are inconsistent, we have
[]\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Ok}\sqsubseteq a_{3}
and
a_{3}\sqsubseteq a_{2}\to a_{4}
, but the former function type contains functions that go wrong after being given an input, whereas the latter function type does not. Indeed, one cannot assign a sufficiency arrow type to this term in the given environment under consistent subtyping assumptions.
The full definition is given in Appendix
D
.

Theorem 6.8 (Correctness of the Type Inference Algorithm).

Let
\Gamma,\Delta
be type environments without type constraints,
C
and
C^{\prime}
be sets of constraints,
M
be a term, and
A
,
A^{\prime}
be types. Then:

(Left Soundness)
:

If
(C\cup\Gamma,\,M:A\vdash\Delta)\in\mathsf{InferL}(\Gamma)(M)(\Delta)
, then
C\cup\Gamma,\,M:A\vdash\Delta
is provable.

(Left Completeness)
:

If
C^{\prime}\cup\Gamma,\,M:A\vdash\Delta
is provable, then there exists a type substitution
\sigma
and a judgement
(C\cup\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime}\vdash\Delta)\in\mathsf{InferL}(\Gamma)(M)(\Delta)
such that
A=A^{\prime}\sigma
and
C^{\prime}\vdash C\sigma
.

(Right Soundness)
:

If
(C\cup\Gamma\vdash M:A)\in\mathsf{InferR}(\Gamma)(M)
, then
C\cup\Gamma\vdash M:A
is provable.

(Right Completeness)
:

If
C^{\prime}\cup\Gamma\vdash M:A
is provable, then there exists a type substitution
\sigma
and a judgement
(C\cup\Gamma\vdash M:A^{\prime})\in\mathsf{InferR}(\Gamma)(M)
such that
A=A^{\prime}\sigma
and
C^{\prime}\vdash C\sigma
.

6.4.
Syntactic Soundness

Rather than proving semantic soundness, we take the opportunity to show how one can prove a syntactic soundness result in the sense of
Wright and Felleisen 1994
, but first we need to generalise the definition of well-typed and ill-typed to account for top-level identifiers.

Definition 6.9.

Suppose
M
is closed and
\Gamma
is a consistent top-level type environment with
\vdash\mathcal{M}:\Gamma
.

•

We say that
M
is
well-typed in

\Gamma
just if
\Gamma\vdash M:\mathsf{Ok}
.

•

We say that
M
is
ill-typed in

\Gamma
just if
\Gamma,\,M:\mathsf{Ok}\vdash
.

The force of the qualifier
consistent
is to require that the type constraints in a type environment are not contradictory. Several definitions are possible, and we use an adaptation of the syntactical definition given by
Eifrig et al. 1995
. Since it is orthogonal to the two-sided aspect of the work, the definition has been relegated to Appendix
E
.

An appropriate formulation of
progress
for two-sided systems must take into account a non-trivial subject on the left of the turnstile as well as the right. On the right, as usual, we have that terms can either make a step or are already values. However, in a typing
M:A
on the left, it is possible that
M
can make a step, already be stuck or even be a value. However, in the latter case, the value must not be in
A
. We can state this succinctly as follows:

Theorem 6.10 (Progress).

Suppose
M
is closed and
\Gamma
is a consistent, top-level type environment.

•

If
\Gamma\vdash M:A
then either
M
can make a step, or
M
is a value

•

If
\Gamma,\,M:A\vdash
then either
M
can make a step, or
\Gamma\not\vdash M:A

The formulation uses a fact about typing in our two-sided systems that is peculiar to systems with the necessity arrow: all closed values are typable on the right. We already remarked in Section
3
that every (fixpoint) abstraction is typable on the right with
\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
, and a simple induction shows that every constructor-headed term has a corresponding constructor-headed type. Thus,
\Gamma\not\vdash M:A
above in particular implies that
M
is not a value of type
A
.

For
preservation
, it is typical to prove some preliminary lemmas that show closure under well-typed substitutions. In the two-sided case, there are many more of these substitution lemmas, since one has to take account of the possibilities that the substituted-for variable occurs on the opposite side of the turnstile to the subject (as usual), on the same side, or not at all (which also relies on the fact that closed values are typable). However, preservation then follows directly.

Theorem 6.11 (Preservation).

Suppose
M
is a closed term and
\Gamma
is a consistent, top-level type environment in which all top-level identifiers have justified typings
\vdash\mathcal{M}:\Gamma
.

•

If
\Gamma\vdash M:A
and
M\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ N
then
\Gamma\vdash N:A
.

•

If
\Gamma,\,M:A\vdash
and
M\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ N
then
\Gamma,\,N:A\vdash
.

Finally, syntactic soundness follows immediately.

Theorem 6.12 (Syntactic Soundness).

Suppose
M
is a closed term and
\Gamma
is a consistent, top-level type environment in which all top-level identifiers have justified typings
\vdash\mathcal{M}:\Gamma
.

•

If
M
is well-typed in
\Gamma
, then
M
does not go wrong.

•

If
M
is ill-typed in
\Gamma
, then
M
does not evaluate.

7.
Conclusion and Related Work

We have introduced
two-sided type systems
which are sequent calculi for typing formulas, made possible through a new function type
A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B
. We have shown several ways in which these calculi can be considered sound, and illustrated how left-sided rules can be added to a constrained type system. We also investigated the internalisation of negation, and a one-sided system without a true negatives theorem.
Many of the basic constants of the programming languages we use today have necessary requirements on their inputs. However, traditional type systems largely ignore this basic aspect of their behaviour. Two-sided systems can use this additional dimension, and enable simple reasoning about program incorrectness as well as program correctness.

Typing contexts beyond variables.

Many type systems for higher-order program verification generalise the typing context to allow logical formulae for the purpose of recording path conditions. For example, Liquid Types
(
Rondon et al. 2008
;
Vazou et al. 2013
;
Vazou et al. 2015
)
and the systems of
Terauchi 2010
and
Unno and Kobayashi 2009
place the Boolean valued expression
M
(or an equivalent formula) into the context when proving that the ‘then’-branch of a conditional,
\mathsf{if}\ {M}\ \mathsf{then}\ {N}\ \mathsf{else}\ {P}
, is correctly typed. In a two-sided system, this can be managed very naturally by introducing separate types for
\mathsf{true}
and
\mathsf{false}
(as we have in our system of Section
6
) and assuming
M:\mathsf{true}
.
A rather different use of the typing context was made by
Curien and Herbelin 2000
. Their system is a kind of sequent calculus in which the formulas on the left of the turnstile comprise variable typings and a stack of terms (cf. Krivine machines), which is thought of as an evaluation context.

Constrained type systems.

Constrained type systems and inclusion constraints go back at least to the work of
Mitchell 1984
. A unified treatment is given by
Odersky et al. 1999
. Our system takes inspiration from the one-sided system of
Aiken et al. 1994
(see also
Marlow and Wadler 1997
), though their constraints are far more expressive. Our approach to consistency of subtype constraints is an adaptation of the purely syntactic approach of
Eifrig et al. 1995
. It is not clear how well our algorithm would scale in practice, ideally one would like some guarantees on the size of types or the efficiency of inference, such as features in more recent works by
Dolan and Mycroft 2017
and
Jones and Ramsay 2021
. None of these systems has a true positives theorem.

Success Typing.

Our original motivation was to try to better understand the very effective
success typing
paradigm for Erlang
(
Lindahl and Sagonas 2006
)
. Work by
Jakob and Thiemann 2015
provides a useful view through their falsification type system, in which one constructs a logical formula used to describe inputs that guarantee to crash the function. The formalisation of
López-Fraguas et al. 2018
is also enlightening and they moreover extend the system with polymorphism.

Success Types are perhaps the best known example of a type-based true positives theorem, ‘ill-typed programs always fail’
(
Sagonas 2010
)
. They are so named because they over-approximate the successes of expressions: if
A
is a success type and
M:A
then
M
may succeed in evaluating to an
A
, but may also go wrong or diverge. Hence why we named our alternative semantics after this paradigm. Note, the ‘ill-typed’ of the slogan really means untypable in our sense.

A key feature is a bespoke function type. It is defined in
(
Lindahl and Sagonas 2006
)
as follows:

A success typing of a function
f
is a type signature
(\vv{\alpha})\to\beta
, such that whenever an application
f(\vv{p})
reduces to a value
v
, then
v\in\beta
and
\vv{p}\in\vv{\alpha}
.

Thus, the
success arrow
, let us write
\to_{s}
, can be understood as having the right rule shown to the side. That is, it contains a component of necessity, but restricted to
\mathsf{Ok}
on the right. Hence, with only this

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,M:\mathsf{Ok}\vdash x:\mathsf{Ok}}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 17.22217pt{\Gamma,\,M:\mathsf{Ok}\vdash M:B}\mathstrut

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.\,M:A\to_{s}B,\,\Delta\mathstrut

arrow, one can refute that an application
MN
has a type
A
only when
A=\mathsf{Ok}
.
Yet, despite this, success types appear to be highly effective in practice. One reason could be the following: if an application
MN
fails to evaluate to an
A
, then there are three possibilities, it could go wrong, it could diverge, or it could evaluate to something else. In the first two cases, one can attempt to refute that it has type
\mathsf{Ok}
, and in the third, one can attempt to affirm that it has a type that is disjoint from
A
instead. Thus, it is likely that one can get quite far with only this form of necessity.

Logics for incorrectness.

Incorrectness logic of
O’Hearn 2019
has sparked a new interest in systems for reasoning about program behaviour that enjoy true positives theorems. Subsequent work has extended its scope
(
Raad et al. 2022
)
, and demonstrated its real-world effectiveness
(
Le et al. 2022
)
. A key feature of these logics is
under-approximation
as a means to achieve true positives, and this allows for a more specific notion of ‘positive’, which excludes divergence. In incorrectness logic, states in a postcondition must be
reachable
. This leads to the need to reason about termination, but it also allows for an analysis to dynamically drop disjunctions in order to scale well. A interesting alternative is the Outcome Logic of
Zilberstein et al. 2023
. Here there is a distinction between Boolean disjunction and outcome disjunction. The former has true as annihilator, but the latter does not, and this allows for the dropping of outcomes for efficiency.

Necessity.

The foundation of our system is not under-approximation, but necessity. When
M:A
is provable, type
A
is an
over-approximation
of
M
.

This makes the system closer to the works of, on the one hand,
Cousot et al. 2011
;
Cousot et al. 2013
, and on the other,
Mackay et al. 2022
. The former considers the problem of inferring
necessary preconditions
in order to detect contract violations that will lead to assertion failures. Specifications are given as code assertions and various analyses based on abstract interpretation are showcased for efficient inference. The latter uses necessity as a means to specify the
robustness
of module specifications in object-oriented programming. Three novel necessity operators are added to an assertion language equipped with certain object capabilities, and a logic is developed for proving that a module satisfies its necessity specifications.

Type systems with complement.

A complement operator appears in the system of
Aiken et al. 1994
and a negation in the work of
Parreaux and Chau 2022
, though neither has a true positives theorem.
In the highly expressive dependent type system of
Unno et al. 2017
, none of the programs can go wrong, but the system is nevertheless very well-equipped for incorrectness reasoning, and includes sophisticated means for reasoning about recursion and tracking divergence. Since their system can express the complements of arbitrary types, it should be possible to encode a version of our necessity arrow. One can think of our one-sided system of Section
5
as a kind of sweet spot for partial correctness, in which the theory and automation become particularly straightforward.

Acknowledgements.

The first author is very grateful for an award from Meta Research. This paper was much improved due to the advice of the POPL’24 anonymous reviewers, and we especially thank our shepherd Sophia Drossopoulou. The work has also benefited from many useful discussions with colleagues in the Programming Languages Research Group at the University of Bristol.

Appendix A
Additional Material in Support of Section
2

A.1.
Proof of Lemma
2.6

We prove the following result:

Let
\Gamma
and
\Delta
be sets of formulas,
M
be a term, and
A
be a type. If
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
then both,
\Gamma,\,M:A\vdash\Delta
and
\Gamma\vdash M:A,\,\Delta
.

Proof.

Let
\Gamma,\Delta,M
, and
A
be as above, such that
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
. we then proceed by induction on the derivation of
\vdash
.

(ID)
:

We have
\Gamma,\,x:A\vdash x:A,\,\Delta
. Then, clearly, by
(ID)
we also have,
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime},\,x:A\vdash x:A,\,\Delta
, and by the same reasoning,
\Gamma,\,x:A\vdash x:A,\,M:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
as required.

(ZeroR)
:

We have
\Gamma\vdash\texttt{zero}:\mathsf{Nat},\,\Delta
. Then, clearly, by
(ZeroR)
we also have,
\Gamma,\,M:A\vdash\texttt{zero}:\mathsf{Nat},\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\vdash\texttt{zero}:\mathsf{Nat},\,M:A,\,\Delta
.

(cR)
:

We have
\Gamma\vdash c(N):\mathsf{Nat},\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\vdash N:\mathsf{Nat},\,\Delta
for
c\in\{\texttt{succ},\,\mathsf{pred}\}
. By the inductive hypothesis, we have
\Gamma,\,M:A\vdash N:\mathsf{Nat},\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\vdash N:\mathsf{Nat},\,M:A,\,\Delta
. Then, by
(cR)
, we have
\Gamma,\,M:A\vdash c(N):\mathsf{Nat},\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\vdash c(N):\mathsf{Nat},\,M:A,\,\Delta
as required.

(FixsR)
:

We have
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,N:A\to B,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma,\,f:A\to B,\,x:A\vdash N:B,\,\Delta
. By the inductive hypothesis, we have
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime},\,f:A\to B,\,x:A\vdash N:B,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma,\,f:A\to B,\,x:A\vdash N:B,\,M:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
. Then, by
(FixsR)
, we have
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime}\vdash\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,N:A\to B,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,N:A\to B,\,M:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
as required.

(FixnR)
:

We have
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,N:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma,\,f:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B,\,x:A\vdash N:B,\,\Delta
. By the inductive hypothesis, we have
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime},\,f:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B,\,x:A\vdash N:B,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma,\,f:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B,\,x:A\vdash N:B,\,M:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
. Then, by
(FixsR)
, we have
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime}\vdash\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,N:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,N:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B,\,M:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
as required.

(AppR)
:

We have
\Gamma\vdash P\ Q:B,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\vdash P:A\to B,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\vdash Q:A,\,\Delta
. By the inductive hypothesis, we have
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime}\vdash P:A\to B,\,\Delta
,
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime}\vdash Q:A,\,\Delta
,
\Gamma\vdash P:A\to B,\,M:A^{\prime}\Delta
and
\Gamma\vdash Q:A,\,M:A^{\prime}\Delta
. Then, by
(AppR)
, we have
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime}\vdash P\ Q:B,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\vdash P\ Q:B,\,M:A^{\prime}\Delta
as required.

(PairR)
:

We have
\Gamma\vdash(P,Q):A\times B,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\vdash P:A,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\vdash Q:B,\,\Delta
. By the inductive hypothesis, we have
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime}\vdash P:A,\,\Delta
,
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime}\vdash Q:B,\,\Delta
,
\Gamma\vdash P:A,\,M:A^{\prime}\Delta
and
\Gamma\vdash Q:B,\,M:A^{\prime}\Delta
. Then, by
(PairR)
, we have
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime}\vdash(P,Q):A\times B,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\vdash(P,Q):A\times B,\,M:A^{\prime}\Delta
as required.

(LetR)
:

We have
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=P\,\mathsf{in}\,Q:A,\,\Delta
,
\Gamma\vdash P:B\times C,\,\Delta
, and
\Delta,\,x:B,\,y:C\vdash Q:A,\,\Delta
. By the inductive hypothesis, we have
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime}\vdash P:B\times C,\,\Delta
,
\Delta,\,M:A^{\prime},\,x:B,\,y:C\vdash Q:A,\,\Delta
,
\Gamma\vdash P:B\times C,\,M:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
, and
\Delta,\,x:B,\,y:C\vdash Q:A,\,M:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
. Then by
(LetR)
, we have
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime}\vdash\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=P\,\mathsf{in}\,Q:A,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=P\,\mathsf{in}\,Q:A,\,M:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
as required.

(IfzR)
:

We have
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{ifz}{R}{P}{Q}:A,\,\Delta
,
\Gamma\vdash R:\mathsf{Nat},\,\Delta
,
\Gamma\vdash P:A,\,\Delta
, and
\Gamma\vdash Q:A,\,\Delta
. By the inductive hypothesis, we have
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime}\vdash R:\mathsf{Nat},\,\Delta
,
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime}\vdash P:A,\,\Delta
,
\Gamma,\,M:A\vdash Q:A,\,\Delta
,
\Gamma\vdash R:\mathsf{Nat},\,M:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
,
\Gamma\vdash P:A,\,M:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
, and
\Gamma\vdash Q:A,\,M:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
. Then by
(IfzR)
, we have
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime}\vdash\mathsf{ifz}{R}{P}{Q}:A,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{ifz}{R}{P}{Q}:A,\,M:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
as required.

(Dis)
:

We have
\Gamma,\,N:A\vdash\Delta
,
\Gamma\vdash N:B,\,\Delta
, and
A\mathrel{||}B
. By the inductive hypothesis, we have
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime},\,\vdash N:B,\,\Delta
and
\Gamma\vdash N:B,\,M:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
. By
(Dis)
, we have
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime}\,N:A\vdash\Delta
and
\Gamma,\,N:A\vdash M:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
.

(PairL)
:

We have
\Gamma,\,(M_{1},M_{2}):B_{1}\times B_{2}\vdash\Delta
and
\Gamma,\,M_{i}:B_{i}\vdash\Delta
for some
i\in\{1,2\}
. By the inductive hypothesis, we have
\Gamma,\,M:A,\,M_{i}:B_{i}\vdash\Delta
and
\Gamma,\,M_{i}:B_{i}\vdash M:A,\,\Delta
. Then, by
(PairL)
, we have
\Gamma,\,M:A,\,(M_{1},M_{2}):B_{1}\times B_{2}\vdash\Delta
and
\Gamma,\,(M_{1},M_{2}):B_{1}\times B_{2}\vdash M:A,\,\Delta
as required.

(AppL)
:

We have
\Gamma,\,P\ Q:B\vdash\Delta
,
\Gamma\vdash P:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B,\,\Delta
, and
\Gamma,\,Q:A\vdash\Delta
. By the inductive hypothesis, we have
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime}\vdash P:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B,\,\Delta
,
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime},\,Q:A\vdash\Delta
,
\Gamma\vdash P:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B,\,M:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
, and
\Gamma,\,Q:A\vdash M:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
. Then by
(AppL)
, we have
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime},\,P\ Q:B\vdash\Delta
and
\Gamma,\,P\ Q:B\vdash M:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
as required.

(cL)
:

We have
\Gamma,\,c(N):\mathsf{Nat}\vdash\Delta
and
\Gamma,\,N:\mathsf{Nat}\vdash\Delta
for
c\in\{\texttt{succ},\,\mathsf{pred}\}
. By the inductive hypothesis, we have
\Gamma,\,M:A,\,N:\mathsf{Nat}\vdash\Delta
and
\Gamma,\,N:\mathsf{Nat}\vdash M:A,\,\Delta
. Then, by
(cL)
, we have
\Gamma,\,M:A,\,c(N):\mathsf{Nat}\vdash\Delta
and
\Gamma,\,c(N):\mathsf{Nat}\vdash M:A,\,\Delta
as required.

(IfzL1)
:

We have
\Gamma,\,\mathsf{ifz}{R}{P}{Q}:A\vdash\Delta
and
\Gamma,\,R:\mathsf{Nat}\vdash\Delta
. By the inductive hypothesis, we have
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime},\,R:\mathsf{Nat}\vdash\Delta
and
\Gamma,\,R:\mathsf{Nat}\vdash M:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
. Then by
(IfzL1)
we have
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime},\,\mathsf{ifz}{R}{P}{Q}:A\vdash\Delta
and
\Gamma,\,\mathsf{ifz}{R}{P}{Q}:A\vdash M:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
as required.

(IfzL2)
:

We have
\Gamma,\,\mathsf{ifz}{R}{P}{Q}:A\vdash\Delta
,
\Gamma,\,P:A\vdash\Delta
, and
\Gamma,\,Q:A\vdash\Delta
. By the inductive hypothesis, we have
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime},\,P:A\vdash\Delta
,
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime},\,Q:A\vdash\Delta
,
\Gamma,\,P:A\vdash M:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
, and
\Gamma,\,Q:A\vdash M:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
. Then by
(IfzL2)
, we have
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime},\,\mathsf{ifz}{R}{P}{Q}:A\vdash\Delta
and
\Gamma,\,\mathsf{ifz}{R}{P}{Q}:A\vdash M:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
as required.

(LetL1)
:

We have
\Gamma,\,\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=P\,\mathsf{in}\,Q:A\vdash\Delta
and
\Gamma,\,Q:A\vdash\Delta
. By the inductive hypothesis, we have
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime},\,Q:A\vdash\Delta
and
\Gamma,\,Q:A\vdash M:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
. Then by
(LetL1)
, we have
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime},\,\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=P\,\mathsf{in}\,Q:A\vdash\Delta
and
\Gamma,\,\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=P\,\mathsf{in}\,Q:A\vdash M:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
as required.

(LetL2)
:

We have
\Gamma,\,\mathsf{let}\,(x_{1},x_{2})=P\,\mathsf{in}\,Q:A\vdash\Delta
,
\Gamma,\,P:B_{1}\times B_{2}\vdash\Delta
, and
(\forall i)\,\Gamma,\,Q:A\vdash x_{i}:B_{i},\,\Delta
. By the inductive hypothesis, we have
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime},\,P:B_{1}\times B_{2}\vdash\Delta
,
(\forall i)\,\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime},\,Q:A\vdash x_{i}:B_{i},\,\Delta
,
\Gamma,\,P:B_{1}\times B_{2}\vdash M:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
, and
(\forall i)\,\Gamma,\,Q:A\vdash x_{i}:B_{i},\,M:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
. Then, by
(LetL2)
, we have
\Gamma,\,\mathsf{let}\,(x_{1},x_{2})=P\,\mathsf{in}\,Q:A,\,M:A^{\prime}\vdash\Delta
and
\Gamma,\,\mathsf{let}\,(x_{1},x_{2})=P\,\mathsf{in}\,Q:A\vdash M:A^{\prime},\,\Delta
as required.

∎

Corollary A.1.

Both left and right weakening hold.

Appendix B
Additional Material in Support of Section
4

First, we give an explicit characterisation of getting stuck.

Definition B.1 (Getting Stuck).

We separate the values into different classes:

•

\mathsf{Vals}
– all values

•

\mathsf{FunV}
– those values of shape
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M

•

\mathsf{NatV}
– those values of shape
\underline{n}

•

\mathsf{ProdV}
– those values of shape
(V,\,W)

A term is said to be a
stuckex
if it is of one of the following forms:

•

\texttt{succ}(V)
or
\mathsf{pred}(V)
with
V\notin\mathsf{NatV}

•

V\,M
with
V\notin\mathsf{FunV}

•

\mathsf{ifz}\,V\,\mathsf{then}\,N\,\mathsf{else}\,P
with
V\notin\mathsf{NatV}

•

\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=V\,\mathsf{in}\,M
with
V\notin\mathsf{ProdV}

A term is said to be
stuck
just if it has shape
\mathcal{E}[M]
with
M
a stuckex. A term
M
is said to
get stuck
just if
M\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,N
with
N
stuck. It is easy to see that a term in normal form is either stuck or is a value. Hence, every term either diverges, gets stuck or evaluates.

Next, we have a series of lemmas building up some basic results about the combinatorics of reduction. The overall goal is to show that replacing a subterm by another that either reduces to the same normal form or diverges leads to an essentially similar outcome.

In the following lemma, we use the phrase ‘
M
is not blocked by
z
’ to mean that
M
does not have shape
\mathcal{E}[z]
for any evaluation context
\mathcal{E}
.

Lemma B.2.

If
M[N/z]=\mathcal{E}[P]
and
M
is not blocked by
z
, then there is
\mathcal{F}
and
P^{\prime}
such that
M=\mathcal{F}[P^{\prime}]
and
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{F}[N/z]
and
P=P^{\prime}[N/z]
.

Proof.

By induction on
\mathcal{E}
.

•

Suppose
\mathcal{E}=\Box
and
M
is not blocked on
z
. Then
M[N/z]=P
so let
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}=\Box
and
P^{\prime}=M
.

•

Suppose
\mathcal{E}
is of shape
\texttt{succ}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
and
M
is not blocked by
z
, so
M[N/z]
is of shape
\texttt{succ}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P])
. Since
M
is not blocked by
z
, it must have shape
\texttt{succ}(Q)
for some
Q
again not blocked by
z
, also
Q[N/z]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P]
. Hence, it follows from the induction hypothesis that there is some
\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}
and
P^{\prime}
such that
P^{\prime}[N/z]=P
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}=\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}[N/z]
. Hence, let
\mathcal{F}:=\texttt{succ}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime})
.

•

Suppose
\mathcal{E}
is of shape
\mathsf{pred}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
, we reason analogously to the previous case.

•

Suppose
\mathcal{E}
is of shape
(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},Q_{2})
and
M
is not blocked by
z
, so
M[N/z]
is of shape
(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P],Q_{2})
. Since
M
is not blocked by
z
, it has shape
(Q_{1},Q_{2}^{\prime})
and
Q_{1}
is not blocked by
z
and
Q_{1}[N/z]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P]
and
Q_{2}^{\prime}[N/z]=Q_{2}
. It follows by induction that there is
\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}
and
P^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}[N/z]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime}[N/z]=P
. Hence, let
\mathcal{F}:=(\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime},\,Q_{2}^{\prime})
.

•

Suppose
\mathcal{E}=(V,\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
and
M
is not blocked by
z
. So,
M[N/z]
is of shape
(V,\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P])
. Since
M
is not blocked by
z
,
M
must be of shape
(V^{\prime},Q)
and
Q
is again not blocked by
z
,
V^{\prime}[N/z]=V
and
Q[N/z]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P]
. Then it follows from the induction hypothesis that there are
\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}
and
P^{\prime}
such that
P^{\prime}[N/z]=P
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}[N/z]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
. Hence, let
\mathcal{F}
be
(V^{\prime},\,\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime})
and the result is immediate.

•

Suppose
\mathcal{E}
is of shape
\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=calE^{\prime}\,\mathsf{in}\,Q
and
M
is not blocked by
z
. Then
M[N/z]
is of shape
\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=calE^{\prime}[P]\,\mathsf{in}\,Q
. Since
M
is not blocked by
z
, it must be that
M
has shape
\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=R\,\mathsf{in}\,Q^{\prime}
and
R[N/z]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P]
and
Q^{\prime}[N/z]=Q
and
R
is not blocked by
z
. Hence, it follows from the induction hypothesis that there are
P^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}
such that
R=\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}[P^{\prime}]
and
P^{\prime}[N/z]=P
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}[N/z]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
. Thus, let
\mathcal{F}
be
\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}\,\mathsf{in}\,Q^{\prime}
.

•

Suppose
\mathcal{E}
is of shape
\mathsf{ifz}\,\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\,\mathsf{then}\,Q_{1}\,\mathsf{else}\,Q_{2}
and
M
is not blocked by
z
. Then
M[N/z]=\mathsf{ifz}\,\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P]\,\mathsf{then}\,Q_{1}\,\mathsf{else}\,Q_{2}
. Since
M
is not blocked by
z
, it must be that
M
has shape

\mathsf{ifz}\,Q_{0}\,\mathsf{then}\,Q_{1}^{\prime}\,\mathsf{else}\,Q_{2}^{\prime}
and
Q_{0}[N/z]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P]
,
Q_{1}^{\prime}[N/z]=Q_{1}
,
Q_{2}^{\prime}[N/z]=Q_{2}
. Therefore, it follows from the induction hypothesis that there is
P^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}
such that
P^{\prime}[N/z]=P
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}[N/z]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
. Thus, take
\mathcal{F}
to be
\mathsf{ifz}\,\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}\,\mathsf{then}\,Q_{1}^{\prime}\,\mathsf{else}\,Q_{2}^{\prime}
and the result follows.

•

Suppose
\mathcal{E}
is of shape
(\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,Q)\,\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
and
M
is not blocked by
z
. Then
M[N/z]
is equal to
(\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,Q)\,\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P]
. Since
M
is not blocked by
z
,
M
must have shape
(\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,Q^{\prime})\,R
, with
Q^{\prime}[N/x]=Q
,
R[N/z]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P]
and
R
not blocked by
z
. Hence, it follows from the induction hypothesis that there is
\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}
and
P^{\prime}
such that
R=\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}[P^{\prime}]
,
\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}[N/z]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime}[N/z]=P
. Therefore, take witness
\mathcal{F}=(\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,Q^{\prime})\,\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}
and the result follows.

•

Suppose
\mathcal{E}
is of shape
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\,Q
and
M
is not blocked by
z
. Then
M[N/z]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P]\,Q
. Then, since
M
is not blocked by
z
,
M
must have shape
R\,Q^{\prime}
with
R
not blocked by
z
and
R[N/z]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P]
and
Q^{\prime}[N/z]=Q
. Then it follows from the induction hypothesis that there is
P^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}
with
P^{\prime}[N/z]=P
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}[N/z]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
. Thus, take
\mathcal{F}
to be
\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}\,Q^{\prime}
and the result follows.

∎

Lemma B.3.

If
M[N/z]\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ P
then
M
is blocked by
z
or there is
M^{\prime}
such that
M\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ M^{\prime}
and
P=M^{\prime}[N/z]

Proof.

We prove the following by induction on
\mathcal{E}
.

For all
\mathcal{E}
, for all
M
, if
M[N/z]
is of shape
\mathcal{E}[P_{1}]
and
P_{1}\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ P_{2}
and
M
is not blocked by
z
, then there is some
M^{\prime}
such that
M\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ M^{\prime}
and
M^{\prime}[N/z]=\mathcal{E}[P_{2}]
.

•

Suppose
\mathcal{E}=\Box
. Then assume
M[N/z]
decomposes to
\mathcal{E}[P_{1}]
and
P_{1}\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ P_{2}
and
M
is not blocked by
z
. Then we analyse cases on the axiom
P_{1}\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ P_{2}
.

–

(PZ). Then
M[N/z]
is of shape
\mathsf{pred}(\underline{0})
and
P_{2}=\underline{0}
. Since
M
is not blocked by
z
, it must have shape
\mathsf{pred}(\underline{0})
, so let
M^{\prime}
be
\underline{0}
.

–

(PS). Then
M[N/z]
is of shape
\mathsf{pred}(\underline{k+1})
and
P_{2}=\underline{k}
. Since
M
is not blocked by
z
, it must have shape
\mathsf{pred}(\underline{k+1})
, so let
M^{\prime}
be
\underline{k}
.

–

(IfZ). Then
M[N/z]
is of shape
\mathsf{ifz}\,\underline{0}\,\mathsf{then}\,Q_{1}\,\mathsf{else}\,Q_{2}
and
P_{2}=Q_{1}
. Since
M
is not blocked by
z
, it must have shape
\mathsf{ifz}\,\underline{0}\,\mathsf{then}\,Q_{1}^{\prime}\,\mathsf{else}\,Q_{2}^{\prime}
with
Q_{i}=Q_{i}^{\prime}[N/z]
. So, let
M^{\prime}
be
Q_{1}^{\prime}
and the result follows immediately.

–

(IfS). Then
M[N/z]
is of shape
\mathsf{ifz}\,\underline{n+1}\,\mathsf{then}\,Q_{1}\,\mathsf{else}\,Q_{2}
and
P_{2}=Q_{2}[\underline{n}/x]
. Since
M
is not blocked by
z
,
M
must have shape
\mathsf{ifz}\,\underline{n+1}\,\mathsf{then}\,Q_{1}^{\prime}\,\mathsf{else}\,Q_{2}^{\prime}
and
Q_{i}=Q_{i}^{\prime}[N/z]
. Let
M^{\prime}
be
Q_{2}^{\prime}[\underline{n}/x]
. Then
M^{\prime}[N/z]=(Q_{2}^{\prime}[\underline{n}/x])[N/z]=(Q_{2}^{\prime}[N/z])[\underline{n}/x]
, as required.

–

(Fix
\beta
). Then
M[N/z]
is of shape
(\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,Q)\,V
and
P_{2}=Q[V/x,\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,Q/f]
. Since
M
is not blocked by
z
, it follows that
M
is of shape
(\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,Q^{\prime})\,V^{\prime}
and
Q=Q^{\prime}[N/z]
and
V=V^{\prime}[N/z]
. Let
M^{\prime}
be
Q^{\prime}[V^{\prime}/x,\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,Q^{\prime}/f]
. Then it follows that:

M^{\prime}[N/z]=(Q^{\prime}[N/z])[V^{\prime}[N/z]/x,\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,Q^{\prime}[N/z]/f]

–

(Let). Then
M[N/z]
is of shape
\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=(V_{1},V_{2})\,\mathsf{in}\,Q
and
P_{2}=Q[V_{1}/x,V_{2}/y]
. Since
M
is not blocked by
z
, it follows that
M
has shape
\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=(V_{1}^{\prime},V_{2}^{\prime})\,\mathsf{in}\,Q^{\prime}
with
V_{i}=V_{i}^{\prime}[N/z]
and
Q=Q^{\prime}[N/z]
. Then let
M^{\prime}
be
Q^{\prime}[V_{1}^{\prime}/x,V_{2}^{\prime}/y]
and the result follows immediately.

•

Suppose
\mathcal{E}
is of shape
\texttt{succ}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
and
M[N/z]
is of shape
\texttt{succ}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P_{1}])
and
P_{1}\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ P_{2}
and
M
is not blocked by
z
. Thus
M
must have shape
\texttt{succ}(Q)
and
Q
is again not blocked by
z
, also
Q[N/z]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P_{1}]
. Hence, it follows from the induction hypothesis that there is some
Q^{\prime}
such that
Q\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ Q^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}[N/z]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P_{2}]
. Hence, let
M^{\prime}
be
\texttt{succ}(Q^{\prime})
.

•

Suppose
\mathcal{E}
is of shape
\mathsf{pred}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
, then we reason analogously to the previous case.

•

Suppose
\mathcal{E}
is of shape
(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},Q_{2})
and
M[N/z]
is of shape
(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P_{1}],Q_{2})
and
P_{1}\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ P_{2}
and
M
is not blocked by
z
. Thus
M
has shape
(Q_{1},Q_{2}^{\prime})
and
Q_{1}
is not blocked by
z
and
Q_{1}[N/z]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P_{1}]
and
Q_{2}^{\prime}[N/z]=Q_{2}
. It follows by induction that there is
Q_{1}^{\prime}
and
Q_{1}\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ Q_{1}^{\prime}
and
Q_{1}^{\prime}[N/z]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P_{2}]
. So, let
M^{\prime}
be
(Q_{1}^{\prime},Q_{2}^{\prime})
.

•

Suppose
\mathcal{E}
is of shape
(V,\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
and
M[N/z]
is of shape
(V,\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P_{1}])
and
P_{1}\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ P_{2}
and
M
is not blocked by
z
. So,
M
must be of shape
(V^{\prime},Q)
and
Q
is again not blocked by
z
,
V^{\prime}[N/z]=V
and
Q[N/z]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P_{1}]
. Then it follows from the induction hypothesis that there is
Q^{\prime}
such that
Q\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ Q^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P_{1}]=Q^{\prime}[N/z]
. Hence, let
M^{\prime}
be
(V^{\prime},\,Q^{\prime})
and the result is immediate.

•

Suppose
\mathcal{E}
is of shape
\mathsf{proj}_{i}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
and
M[N/z]
is of shape
\mathsf{proj}_{i}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P_{1}])
and
P_{1}\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ P_{2}
and
M
is not blocked by
z
. So, it must be that
M
has shape
\mathsf{proj}_{i}(Q)
and
Q[N/z]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P_{1}]
and
Q
is not blocked by
z
. Hence, it follows from the induction hypothesis that there is
Q^{\prime}
and
Q\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ Q^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}[N/z]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P_{2}]
. Thus, let
M^{\prime}
be
\mathsf{proj}_{i}(Q^{\prime})
.

•

Suppose
\mathcal{E}
is of shape
\mathsf{ifz}\,\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\,\mathsf{then}\,Q_{1}\,\mathsf{else}\,Q_{2}
,
M[N/z]=\mathsf{ifz}\,\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P_{1}]\,\mathsf{then}\,Q_{1}\,\mathsf{else}\,Q_{2}
and
P_{1}\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ P_{2}
and
M
is not blocked by
z
. So, it must be that
M
has shape
\mathsf{ifz}\,Q_{0}\,\mathsf{then}\,Q_{1}^{\prime}\,\mathsf{else}\,Q_{2}^{\prime}
and
Q_{0}[N/z]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P_{1}]
,
Q_{1}^{\prime}[N/z]=Q_{1}
,
Q_{2}^{\prime}[N/z]=Q_{2}
. Therefore, it follows from the induction hypothesis that there is
Q_{0}^{\prime}
such that
Q_{0}\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ Q_{0}^{\prime}
and
Q_{0}^{\prime}[N/z]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P_{2}]
. Thus, take
M^{\prime}
to be
\mathsf{ifz}\,Q_{0}^{\prime}\,\mathsf{then}\,Q_{1}^{\prime}\,\mathsf{else}\,Q_{2}^{\prime}
and the result follows.

•

Suppose
\mathcal{E}
has shape
(\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,Q)\,\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
and
M[N/z]=(\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,Q)\,\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P_{1}]
and
P_{1}\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ P_{2}
and
M
is not blocked by
z
. So,
M
must have shape
(\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,Q^{\prime})\,R
and
Q^{\prime}[N/z]=Q
and
R[N/z]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P_{1}]
and
R
is not blocked by
z
. Thus, it follows from the induction hypothesis that there is
R^{\prime}
such that
R\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ R^{\prime}
and
R^{\prime}[N/z]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P_{2}]
. Then let
M^{\prime}
be
(\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,Q^{\prime})\,R^{\prime}
and the result follows.

•

Suppose
\mathcal{E}
is of shape
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\,Q
and
M[N/z]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P_{1}]\,Q
and
P_{1}\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ P_{2}
and
M
is not blocked by
z
. Then
M
must have shape
R\,Q^{\prime}
with
R
not blocked by
z
and
R[N/z]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P_{1}]
and
Q^{\prime}[N/z]=Q
. Then it follows from the induction hypothesis that there is
R^{\prime}
with
R\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ R^{\prime}
and
R^{\prime}[N/z]=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}[P_{2}]
. Thus, take
M^{\prime}
to be
R^{\prime}\,Q^{\prime}
and the result follows.

∎

Lemma B.4.

If
M[N/x]\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,P
with
P
a normal form and
Q\lesssim N
, then either
M[Q/x]
diverges or there is some
M^{\prime}
such that
P=M^{\prime}[N/x]
and
M[Q/x]\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,M^{\prime}[Q/x]
.

Proof.

By lexicographic induction on the length of the sequence and the number of free occurrences of
x
in
M
.

•

Suppose
M[N/x]=P
. Then take
M^{\prime}:=M
.

•

Suppose
M[N/x]\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{k}\ \,P
for some
k>0
. If
M
is
\mathcal{E}[x]
, then
M[Q/x]=(\mathcal{E}[Q/x])[Q]
. If
Q
diverges then so does
M[Q/x]
. Otherwise,
N
and
Q
normalise to the same normal form, say
N^{\prime}
. Hence,
M[N/x]\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,(\mathcal{E}[N/x])[N^{\prime}]
and
M[Q/x]\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,(\mathcal{E}[Q/x])[N^{\prime}]
, then
(\mathcal{E}[N/x])[N^{\prime}]\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{m}\ \,P
with
m\leq k
and the number of free occurrences of
x
in
\mathcal{E}[N^{\prime}]
is decreased by 1. Hence, it follows from the induction hypothesis that
(\mathcal{E}[Q/x])[N^{\prime}]
either diverges or there is some
M^{\prime}
such that
P=M^{\prime}[N/x]
and
(\mathcal{E}[Q/x])[N^{\prime}]\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,M^{\prime}[Q/x]
. The result follows immediately. Otherwise, we may assume
M^{\prime\prime}
is not blocked by
z
and we can apply Lemma
B.3
to obtain some
M^{\prime\prime}
such that
M\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ M^{\prime\prime}
. Then
M^{\prime\prime}[N/x]\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{k-1}\ \,P
, and it follows from the induction hypothesis that either
M^{\prime\prime}[P/x]
diverges, or there is some
M^{\prime}
such that
P=M^{\prime}[N/x]
and
M^{\prime\prime}[Q/x]\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,M^{\prime}[Q/x]
. Then the result follows immediately since
M[Q/x]\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ M^{\prime\prime}[Q/x]
.

∎

Lemma B.5.

If
M[N/z]
reduces to a value
V
and
P\lesssim N
, then either
M[P/z]
diverges or
M[P/z]
reduces to a value
W
and all of the following are true:

(1)

if
V
does not contain a fixpoint abstraction, then
W=V
.

(2)

if
V
has shape
(V_{1},V_{2})
, then
W
has shape
(W_{1}[P/z],W_{2}[P/z])
,
W_{1}[N/z]=V_{1}
and
W_{2}[N/z]=V_{2}
.

(3)

if
V
has shape
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,P
, then
W
has shape
