\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,Q[P/z]
with
Q[N/z]=P
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
V
. Spp
M[N/z]\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,V
. Then, by the previous result,
M[P/z]
either diverges or there is some value
U
such that
V=U[N/z]
and
M[P/z]\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,U[P/z]
. If
U=z
, then
N=V
and
M[P/z]\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,P
. If
P
diverges, then
M[P/z]
diverges, as required. Otherwise
P\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,V
and
W=V
, (a), (b) and (c) follow immediately. Otherwise
U\neq z
and we proceed by inspecting the cases.

•

If
V
is a variable
x
, then since
U\neq z
,
x
must be different from
z
and
U=x
.

•

If
V
is
zero
, then since
U\neq z
,
U=\texttt{zero}
.

•

If
V
is of shape
\texttt{succ}(\underline{n})
, then since
U\neq z
,
U
has shape
\texttt{succ}\,W^{\prime}
, and
W^{\prime}[N/z]=\underline{n}
. Therefore, it follows from the induction hypothesis that
W^{\prime}[P/z]
reduces to
\underline{n}
and the conclusion follows.

•

If
V
is of shape
(V_{1},V_{2})
, then since
U\neq z
,
U
is of shape
(M_{1},M_{2})
and
M_{1}[N/z]=V_{1}
and
M_{2}[N/z]=V_{2}
. Therefore,
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are themselves values. Moreover, if
V
did not contain an abstraction, then neither do
V_{1}
or
V_{2}
and the induction hypothesis yields that
M_{1}[P/z]=V_{1}
and
M_{2}[P/z]=V_{2}
. Thus
W=V
.

•

If
V
is of shape
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,P
, then since
U\neq z
,
U
has shape
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,Q
, with
Q[N/z]=P
as required.

∎

Lemma B.6.

If
M[N/z]
gets stuck and
P\lesssim N
, then either
M[P/z]
diverges or
M[P/z]
gets stuck.

Proof.

Suppose
M[N/z]
gets stuck. Then
M[N/z]\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,P
with
P
stuck. It follows that there is some
R
such that
P=R[N/z]
and
M[P/z]
either diverges or reduces to
R[P/z]
. Then
R[N/z]
has shape
\mathcal{E}[Q]
for some stuckex
Q
. By the forgoing lemma,
R
has shape
\mathcal{F}[Q^{\prime}]
with
\mathcal{F}[N/z]=\mathcal{E}
and
Q^{\prime}[N/z]=Q
. If
Q^{\prime}=z
, then
Q=N
and by
P\lesssim N
either
R[P/z]
diverges or
R[P/z]\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,(\mathcal{F}[P/z])[N^{\prime}]
for some stuckex
N^{\prime}
, and thus also gets stuck. Otherwise,
Q^{\prime}\neq z
and we show in each case that
Q^{\prime}[P/z]
is a stuckex.

•

If
Q
is of shape
x
, then since
Q^{\prime}\neq z
, it must be that
x\neq z
and
Q^{\prime}=x
.

•

If
Q
is of shape
V\,R
with
V\notin\mathsf{FunV}
, then since
Q^{\prime}\neq z
, it must be that
Q^{\prime}
has shape
R_{1}\,R_{2}
with
R_{1}[N/z]=V
. Hence, it follows from the previous lemma that either
R_{1}[P/z]
diverges, in which case
M[P/z]
diverges, or
R_{1}[P/z]
is also a value not in
\mathsf{FunV}
.

The remaining cases are similar.
∎

B.1.
Proof of Theorem
4.7

Now we show the first part of Theorem
4.7
.

Theorem B.7.

Let
M[N/z]
be closed,
Q\lesssim N
.

(1)

If
M[N/z]\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A]\!]
, then
M[Q/z]\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A]\!]
.

(2)

If
M[N/z]\notin\mathcal{T}[\![F]\!]
, then
M[Q/z]\notin\mathcal{T}[\![F]\!]
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
A
.

•

When
A=\mathsf{Nat}
, we reason as follows.

(1)

Suppose
M[N/z]\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![\mathsf{Nat}]\!]
, so either
M[N/z]
diverges or
M[N/z]\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,\underline{n}
. Then it follows from Lemma
B.5
that either
M[Q/z]
diverges or
M[Q/z]
evaluates to
\underline{n}
.

(2)

Suppose
M[N/z]\notin\mathcal{T}[\![\mathsf{Nat}]\!]
, so either (i)
M[N/z]\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,V
and
V\notin[\![\mathsf{Nat}]\!]
or (ii)
M[N/z]
gets stuck or (iii)
M[N/z]
diverges. In cases (i) it follows from Lemma
B.5
that
M[Q/z]
either diverges or reduces to the same kind of value. In case (ii), it follows from Lemma
B.6
that
M[Q/z]
either diverges or gets stuck. In case (iii),
M[Q/z]
also diverges. Hence,
M[Q/z]\notin\mathcal{T}[\![\mathsf{Nat}]\!]
.

•

When
A
is of shape
B\times C
:

(1)

Suppose
M[N/z]\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![B\times C]\!]
, so either
M[N/z]
diverges, or
M[N/z]\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,(V_{1},V_{2})
with
V_{1}\in[\![B]\!]
and
V_{2}\in[\![C]\!]
. In the former case, also
M[Q/z]
diverges. Otherwise, it follows from Lemma
B.5
that either
M[Q/z]
diverges or evaluates to
(W_{1}[Q/z],W_{2}[Q/z])
with
W_{1}[N/z]=V_{1}
and
W_{2}[N/z]=V_{2}
. Hence, we have
W_{1}[N/z]\in[\![B]\!]
and thus also
\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![B]\!]
; similarly
W_{2}[N/x]\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![C]\!]
. Consequently, it follows from the induction hypotheses that
W_{1}[Q/z]\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![B]\!]
and
W_{2}[Q/z]\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![C]\!]
. If either diverge, then
M[Q/z]
diverges, as required. Otherwise,
W_{1}[Q/z]
evaluates to a value in
[\![B]\!]
and
W_{2}[Q/z]
evaluates to a value in
[\![C]\!]
, as required.

(2)

Suppose
M[N/z]\notin\mathcal{T}[\![B\times C]\!]
. Here we can further assume that
B
and
C
are finitely verifiable. So either (i)
M[N/z]\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,V
with
V\notin[\![B\times C]\!]
or (ii)
M[N/z]
gets stuck, or (iii)
M[N/z]
diverges. In case (i), it follows from Lemma
B.5
that either
M[Q/z]
diverges or it evaluates to
V
. In case (ii) it follows from Lemma
B.6
that
M[Q/z]
either diverges or gets stuck. In case (iii) it follows that
M[Q/z]
diverges. Hence,
M[Q/z]\notin\mathcal{T}[\![B\times C]\!]
.

•

When
A
is of shape
B\to C
, we need only prove (i). Suppose
M[N/z]\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![B\to C]\!]
, so either:

(1)

M[N/z]
diverges

(2)

or
M[N/z]\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,P
with
P[V/x,\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,P/f]\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![C]\!]
for all
V\in[\![B]\!]
.

In case (i),
M[Q/z]
diverges. In case (ii), it follows from Lemma
B.5
that either
M[Q/z]
diverges or there is some
Q^{\prime}
such that
M[Q/z]\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,Q^{\prime}[Q/z]
and
Q^{\prime}[N/z]=P
.
Let
V\in[\![B]\!]
. We have
(Q^{\prime}[V/x,\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,Q^{\prime}/f])[N/z]=(Q^{\prime}[N/z])[V/x,\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,Q^{\prime}[N/z]]\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![C]\!]
(
V
is closed) and so it follows from the induction hypothesis that we also have
(Q^{\prime}[V/x,\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,Q^{\prime}/f])[Q/z]\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![C]\!]
too.

•

When
A
is of shape
B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}F
, we need only prove (i). Suppose
M[N/z]\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}F]\!]
, so either
M[N/z]
diverges or
M[N/z]\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,P
with
P[V/x,\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,P/f]\notin\mathcal{T}[\![F]\!]
if
V\notin[\![B]\!]
. In the former case,
M[Q/z]
diverges. Otherwise, it follows from Lemma
B.5
that either
M[Q/z]
diverges or there is
Q^{\prime}
such that
M[Q/z]\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,Q^{\prime}[Q/z]
and
Q^{\prime}[N/z]=P
. Let
V
be a closed value and
V\notin[\![B]\!]
. Hence, as in the previous case,
(Q^{\prime}[V/x,\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,Q^{\prime}/f])[N/z]=(Q^{\prime}[N/z])[V/x,\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,Q^{\prime}[N/z]]\notin\mathcal{T}[\![F]\!]
and it follows from the induction hypothesis that therefore
(Q^{\prime}[Q/z])[V/x]\notin\mathcal{T}[\![F]\!]
either.

•

When
A
is of shape
\mathsf{Ok}
we reason as follows. For (i) suppose
M[N/z]\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![\mathsf{Ok}]\!]
, then we reason as above, according to whichever value
M[N/z]
reduces to. For (ii) suppose
M[N/z]\notin\mathcal{T}[\![\mathsf{Ok}]\!]
, then either
M[N/z]
gets stuck or
M[N/z]
diverges. It follows from Lemma
B.6
that, in both cases,
M[N^{\prime}/z]\notin[\![T]\!]
too.

∎

And then the second part of Theorem
4.7
.

Theorem B.8.

For all types
A
and finitely verifiable types
F
:

(1)

If
C[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]\notin\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A]\!]
, then there is some
n_{0}
, such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
:
C[\mathsf{fix}^{n}f(x).\,M]\notin\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A]\!]
.

(2)

If
C[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]\in\mathcal{T}[\![F]\!]
, then there is some
n_{0}
, such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
:
C[\mathsf{fix}^{n}f(x).\,M]\in\mathcal{T}[\![F]\!]
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
A
.

•

When
A
is
\mathsf{Nat}
, we reason as follows.

(1)

Suppose
C[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]\notin\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A]\!]
. Either
C[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]
evaluates to a value that is not a numeral or
C[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]
crashes. In both cases, it requires only finitely many reduction steps and, among them, only finitely many applications of fix
\beta
reduction, say
k
. Then we can take
n_{0}:=k
.

(2)

Suppose
C[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]\in\mathcal{T}[\![A]\!]
. Then
C[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]
evaluates to a numeral, in finitely many steps. Among them, a certain number, say
k
, are applications of the fix
\beta
reduction. Therefore, for any
m\geq k
,
C[\mathsf{fix}^{m}f(x).\,M]\in\mathcal{T}[\![A]\!]
.

•

When
A
is
\mathsf{Ok}
, we reason analogously.

•

When
A
is of shape
B\times C
we reason as follows.

(1)

Suppose
C[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]\notin\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![B\times C]\!]
. Then either (i)
C[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]
evaluates to a value that is not a pair, or (ii)
C[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]
gets stuck, or (iii)
C[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]
evaluates to a pair value
(V_{1},V_{2})
, but either
V_{1}\notin[\![B]\!]
or
V_{2}\notin[\![C]\!]
. In all cases, the reduction sequences involved are finite, and so contain only finitely many applications of the fix
\beta
reduction. Hence, we may obtain the required bound as above. In cases (i) and (ii) the result follows immediately. In case (iii), first observe that this evaluation used only finitely many applications of the fix
\beta
reduction, say
k_{0}
, and so for any
k\geq k_{0}
,
C[\mathsf{fix}^{k}f(x).\,M]
will reduce to a pair value
(W_{1},W_{2})
. Now let us assume that it is
V_{1}\notin[\![B]\!]
because the other case is symmetrical. Note that, since
V_{1}
is a value, we know that
V_{1}\notin\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![B]\!]
. Next, write
V_{1}
as
D[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]
, for some context
D
, in which all descendants of occurrences of
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M
in
C[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]
are replaced by holes in
D
(for example, by introducing labelled subterms). Then it follows from the induction hypothesis that there is
m_{0}
and for all
m\geq m_{0}
,
D[\mathsf{fix}^{m}f(x).\,M]\notin\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![B]\!]
. Since each
D[\mathsf{fix}^{m}f(x).\,M]
is a value, it follows that
D[\mathsf{fix}^{m}x.\,M]\notin[\![B]\!]
. Hence, for all
n\geq m_{0}+k_{0}
,
C[\mathsf{fix}^{n}f(x).\,M]\notin\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![B\times C]\!]
.

(2)

In this case, we may assume that
B
and
C
are finitely verifiable. Suppose
C[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]\in\mathcal{T}[\![B\times C]\!]
. Then
C[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]
evaluates to a pair
(V_{1},V_{2})
(and this pair must not contain any abstraction, since
B
and
C
are finitely verifiable). Then this reduction sequence is finite, and applies the fix
\beta
reduction rule only a certain number, say
n_{0}
of times. We can use this as the bound required by the conclusion.

•

When
A
is
B\to C
, we need only consider (i). So suppose
C[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]\notin\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![B\to C]\!]
. Then either (i)
C[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]
evaluates to a value that is not a fixpoint abstraction, or (ii)
C[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]
gets stuck, or (iii)
C[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]
evaluates to a fixpoint abstraction
\mathsf{fix}\,g(y).\,P
, but there is a value
V\in[\![B]\!]
such that
P[V/y,\mathsf{fix}\,g(y).\,P/g]\notin\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![C]\!]
. In cases (i) and (ii) we can reason as above to obtain the bound. In case (iii), we can first observe that evaluating to an abstraction requires only finitely many applications of the fix
\beta
reduction rule, say
m_{0}
. So, for any
m\geq m_{0}
,
C[\mathsf{fix}^{m}f(x).\,M]
will also evaluate to an abstraction, say
\mathsf{fix}\,g(y).\,Q
. Then we note that we can write
P[V/y,\mathsf{fix}\,g(y).\,P/g]
as
D[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]
, in which all descendants of occurrences of
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M
in
C[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]
are replaced by holes in
D
(for example, by introducing labelled subterms). Then it follows from the induction hypothesis that there is some
p_{0}
such that, for all
p\geq p_{0}
,
D[\mathsf{fix}^{p}f(x).\,M]\notin\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![C]\!]
. Hence, by construction, we have that, for all
n\geq m_{0}+p_{0}
,
C[\mathsf{fix}^{n}f(x).\,M]\notin\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![B\to C]\!]
.

•

When
A
is
B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}F
, we need only consider (i). So suppose
C[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]\notin\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}F]\!]
. Then either (i)
C[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]
evaluates to a value that is not a fixpoint abstraction, or (ii)
C[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]
gets stuck, or (iii)
C[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]
evaluates to a fixpoint abstraction
\mathsf{fix}\,g(y).\,P
, but there is a value
V
such that
V\notin[\![B]\!]
and yet
P[V/y,\mathsf{fix}\,g(y).\,P/g]\in\mathcal{T}[\![F]\!]
. In cases (i) and (ii) we can find the bound as above. In case (iii), we first observe that evaluating to an abstraction requires only finitely many applications of the fixpoint reduction, say
m_{0}
. So, for any
m\geq m_{0}
,
C[\mathsf{fix}^{m}f(x).\,M]
will also evaluate to an fixpoint abstraction. Next, note that we can write
P[V/y,\mathsf{fix}\,g(y).\,P/g]
as
D[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]
by factoring out all descendants of
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M
in the original term. Then, it follows from the induction hypothesis that there is some
k_{0}
such that, for all
k\geq k_{0}
,
D[\mathsf{fix}^{k}f(x).\,M]\in\mathcal{T}[\![F]\!]
. Then, it follows from this construction that, for all
n\geq m_{0}+k_{0}
,
C[\mathsf{fix}^{n}f(x).\,M]\notin\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}F]\!]
.

∎

Note, the contrapositive of part (i) of this result gives us the following principle: if
C[\mathsf{fix}^{n}f(x).\,M]\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A]\!]
for all
n
, then
C[\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M]\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A]\!]
.

Theorem
4.7

follows from Theorems
B.7
and
B.8
by observing that, since
N
and
P
are closed in the statement, we can always find some fresh variable
z
in order to write
C[N]
as
(C[z])[N/z]
.

Theorem B.9 (Fixpoint Closure.).

In the safety fragment:

•

if
\lambda fx.\,M\in[\![(A\to B)\to A\to B]\!]
, then
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A\to B]\!]
.

•

if
\lambda fx.\,M\in[\![(A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B)\to A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B]\!]
, then
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B]\!]
.

Proof.

Suppose
\lambda fx.\,M\in[\![(A\to B)\to A\to B]\!]
. Then, by definition, for all
V\in[\![A\to B]\!]
,
\lambda x.\,M[V/f]\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A\to B]\!]
(*).
We show that
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A\to B]\!]
using the above principle, by induction on
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

•

When
n=0
,
\mathsf{fix}^{n}f(x).\,M=\lambda x.\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}}
and
\lambda x.\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}}\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A\to B]\!]
by definition.

•

When
n
is of shape
k+1
, we assume
\mathsf{fix}^{k}x.\,M\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A\to B]\!]
. Since this is a value, it follows from (*) that
\lambda x.\,M[\mathsf{fix}^{k}x.\,M/x]\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A\to B]\!]
. By definition, therefore
\mathsf{fix}^{k+1}x.\,M\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A\to B]\!]
, as required.

The case when
\lambda fx.\,M\in[\![(A\to B)\to A\to B]\!]
is analogous, since also
\lambda x.\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}}\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B]\!]
.
∎

B.2.
Proof of Theorem
4.8

Finally, we can show semantic soundness, Theorem
4.8
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the derivation.

(Id)
:

Suppose
\theta
satisfies
\Gamma,\,x:A
on the left, then
x\theta\in\mathcal{T}[\![A]\!]
. Hence,
x\theta\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A]\!]
and the conclusion follows.

(Dis)
:

Suppose
A\mathrel{||}B
and that
\theta
satisfies
\Gamma,\,M:A
on the left, so that
M\theta\in\mathcal{T}[\![A]\!]
. It follows that
M\theta\notin\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![B]\!]
(*). Then it follows from the induction hypothesis that
\theta
satisfies
M:B,\,\Delta
on the right, but by (*),
\theta
does not satisfy
M:B
on the right. Therefore,
\theta
must satisfy
\Delta
on the right, as required.

(ZeroR)
:

By definition
\texttt{zero}\theta=\texttt{zero}\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![\mathsf{Nat}]\!]
.

(SuccR)
:

Suppose
\theta
satisfies
\Gamma
on the left, then it follows from the induction hypothesis that
\theta
satisfies
M:\mathsf{Nat}\,\Delta
on the right. Suppose
\theta
does not satisfy
\Delta
on the right. Then
\theta
satisfies
M:\mathsf{Nat}
on the right, so either
M\theta
diverges or
M\theta
evaluates to a number. Hence
\theta
satisfies
\texttt{succ}(M):\mathsf{Nat}
on the right.

(PredR)
:

Analogous to the previous case.

(FixsR)
:

Suppose
\Gamma,\,f:A\to B,\,x:A\models M:A\to B,\,\Delta
and let
\theta
be a valuation satisfying
\Gamma
on the left. To see that
\theta
will satisfy
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M:A\to B,\,\Delta
on the right we suppose
\theta
does not satisfy
\Delta
on the right, and show that
\theta
satisfies
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M:A\to B
on the right, i.e.
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M\theta\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A\to B]\!]
. By this assumption and our original supposition, we have that, for all
V\in[\![A\to B]\!]
, for all
W\in[\![A]\!]
,
(M\theta)[V/f,W/x]\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A\to B]\!]
, so
\lambda fx.\,M\theta\in[\![(A\to B)\to A\to B]\!]
. Then the result follows since typing is closed under taking fixpoints.

(FixnR)
:

Suppose
\Gamma,\,f:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B,\,x:A\models M:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B,\,\Delta
and let
\theta
be a valuation satisfying
\Gamma
on the left. To see that
\theta
will satisfy
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B,\,\Delta
on the right we suppose
\theta
does not satisfy
\Delta
on the right, and show that
\theta
satisfies
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M:A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B
on the right, i.e.
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M\theta\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B]\!]
. By this assumption and our original supposition, we have that, for all
V\in[\![A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B]\!]
, for all
W\in[\![A]\!]
,
(M\theta)[V/f,W/x]\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B]\!]
, so
\lambda fx.\,M\theta\in[\![(A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B)\to A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B]\!]
. Then the result follows since typing is closed under taking fixpoints.

(LetR)
:

Suppose
\theta
satisfies
\Gamma
on the left, then it follows from the induction hypotheses that
\theta
satisfies
M:B\times C,\,\Delta
on the right. Suppose
\theta
does not satisfy
\Delta
. Hence,
\theta
satisfies
M:B\times C
on the right so
M\theta
either diverges or evaluates to a pair
(V,W)
with
V\in[\![B]\!]
and
W\in[\![C]\!]
. In the former case, the let expression diverges under
\theta
and we are done. Otherwise
\theta\cup[V/x,W/y]
satisfies
\Gamma,\,x:B,\,x:C
on the left and it follows from the induction hypothesis that
\theta
satisfies
N:A
on the right, so
(N[V/x,W/y])\theta\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A]\!]
. The conclusion follows since
(\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=M\,\mathsf{in}\,N)\theta\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,(N[V/x,W/y])\theta
.

(AppR)
:

Suppose
\theta
satisfies
\Gamma
on the left. Then it follows from the induction hypotheses that
\theta
satisfies
M:B\to A,\,\Delta
and
N:B,\,\Delta
on the right. Suppose
\theta
does not satisfy
\Delta
. Then
M\theta\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![B\to A]\!]
and
N\theta\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![B]\!]
. If either diverges, then so does
(M\,N)\theta
and we are done. Otherwise,
M\theta
evaluates to some abstraction
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,P\in[\![B\to A]\!]
and
N\theta
evaluates to a value
V
in
[\![B]\!]
and hence it follows from the definition that
(M\,N)\theta\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,(\mathsf{fix}\,x.\,P)\,V\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ P[V/x,\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,P/f]\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A]\!]
, as required.

(PairR)
:

Suppose
\theta
satisfies
\Gamma
on the left, then it follows from the induction hypotheses that
\theta
satisfies
M:A,\,\Delta
and
N:B,\,\Delta
on the right. Suppose
\theta
does not satisfy
\Delta
on the right, then
M\theta\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![A]\!]
and
N\theta\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![B]\!]
. If either diverge, then so does
(M,N)\theta
and the conclusion is immediate. Otherwise,
M\theta
evaluates to some
V\in[\![A]\!]
and
N\theta
evaluates to some
W\in[\![B]\!]
and thus
(M,N)\theta
evaluates to
(V,W)\in[\![A\times B]\!]
.

(IfZR)
:

Suppose
\theta
satisfies
\Gamma
on the left. Then it follows from the induction hypothesis that
\theta
satisfies (i)
M:\mathsf{Nat},\,\Delta
, (ii)
N:A,\,\Delta
and (iii)
P:A,\,\Delta
on the right. Suppose that
\theta
does not satisfy
\Delta
. Then
M\theta
either diverges or evaluates to a numeral, say
\underline{n}
. In the former case, the if expression diverges too and we conclude. Otherwise, if
n=0
, it follows that
\mathsf{ifz}\,M\theta\,\mathsf{then}\,N\theta\,\mathsf{else}\,P\theta\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,N\theta
and the result follows from (ii). If
n>0
, then
\mathsf{ifz}\,M\theta\,\mathsf{then}\,N\theta\,\mathsf{else}\,P\theta\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,P\theta
and the result follows from (iii).

(AppL)
:

Suppose
\theta
satisfies
\Gamma,\,(M\,N):A
on the left. Then
(M\,N)\theta\in\mathcal{T}[\![A]\!]
. It follows from the induction hypothesis that
\theta
satisfies
M:B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A,\,\Delta
on the right. If
\theta
satisfies
\Delta
on the right, then we are done. If
\theta
satisfies
M:B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
on the right, then
M\theta\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A]\!]
. Since
(M\,N)\theta
is guaranteed to terminate, it follows that
M\theta
and
N\theta
are too, and so
M\theta
evaluates to some fixpoint abstraction
\lambda f(x).\,P\in[\![B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A]\!]
and
N\theta
to some value
V
. It follows from the definition that since, therefore,
(M\,N)\theta\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,P[V/x,\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,P/f]\in\mathcal{T}[\![A]\!]
,
V\in[\![B]\!]
and hence
N\theta\in\mathcal{T}[\![B]\!]
. Then
\theta
satisfies
\Gamma,\,N:B
on the left and the result follows from the induction hypothesis.

(LetL1)
:

Suppose
\theta
satisfies
\Gamma,\,\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=P\,\mathsf{in}\,N:A
on the left, so
\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=P\theta\,\mathsf{in}\,N\theta\in\mathcal{T}[\![A]\!]
. Therefore, it must be that
P\theta
evaluates to a pair
(V,W)
and
(N\theta)[V/x,W/y]\in\mathcal{T}[\![A]\!]
. Then
\theta\cup[V/x,W/y]
satisfies
\Gamma,\,N:A
on the left, and it follows from the induction hypothesis that it also satisfies
\Delta
on the right. Since
x
and
y
are bound in the let, we may assume they do not occur free in
\Delta
, so also
\theta
satisfies
\Delta
on the right.

(LetL2)
:

Suppose
\theta
satisfies
\Gamma,\,\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=P\,\mathsf{in}\,N:A
on the left, so
\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=P\theta\,\mathsf{in}\,N\theta\in\mathcal{T}[\![A]\!]
. It must be that
P\theta
evaluates to some pair
(V,W)
and
(N\theta)[V/x,W/y]\in\mathcal{T}[\![A]\!]
. Hence,
\theta\cup[V/x,W/y]
satisfies
\Gamma,\,N:A
on the left and it follows from the induction hypotheses that it also satisfies
x:B,\,\Delta
and
y:C,\,\Delta
on the right. If it satisfies
\Delta
on the right, then
\theta
satisfies
\Delta
on the right since we mat assume the bound variables
x
and
y
do not occur in
\Delta
. Otherwise,
V\in[\![B]\!]
and
W\in[\![C]\!]
and so we have that
P\theta\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,(V,w)\in[\![B\times C]\!]
. Therefore, it follows from the induction hypothesis that
\theta
satisfies
\Delta
on the right.

(SuccL)
:

Suppose
\theta
satisfies
\Gamma,\,\texttt{succ}(P):\mathsf{Nat}
on the left, so
\texttt{succ}(P\theta)\in\mathcal{T}[\![\mathsf{Nat}]\!]
and hence
P\theta
must evaluate to a number. Then the conclusion follows from the induction hypothesis.

(PredL)
:

Analogous to the previous case.

(IfZL1)
:

Suppose
\theta
satisfies
\Gamma,\,\mathsf{ifz}\,Q\,\mathsf{then}\,N\,\mathsf{else}\,P:A
, so that
\mathsf{ifz}\,Q\theta\,\mathsf{then}\,N\theta\,\mathsf{else}\,P\theta
evaluates to an
A
. Then it must be that
Q\theta
evaluates to a numeral, and then the result follows from the induction hypothesis.

(IfZL2)
:

Suppose
\theta
satisfies
\Gamma,\,\mathsf{ifz}\,Q\,\mathsf{then}\,N\,\mathsf{else}\,P:A
, so that
\mathsf{ifz}\,Q\theta\,\mathsf{then}\,N\theta\,\mathsf{else}\,P\theta
evaluates to an
A
. Then it must be that
Q\theta
evaluates to a numeral, say
\underline{n}
. If
n=0
, then the if expression reduces to
N\theta\in\mathcal{T}[\![A]\!]
and the result follows from the first induction hypothesis. Otherwise, the if expression reduces to
P\theta
and the result follows from the second induction hypothesis.

(OkVarR)
:

It is immediate from the definition that
x\theta
is a value.

(OkL)
:

Suppose
\theta
satisfies
\Gamma,\,M:A
on the left. Then
M\theta\in\mathcal{T}[\![A]\!]
. Since
\mathcal{T}[\![A]\!]\subseteq\mathcal{T}[\![\mathsf{Ok}]\!]
, the conclusion follows from the induction hypothesis.

(OkR)
:

Symmetrical to the previous case.

(OkSL)
:

Suppose
\theta
satisfies
\Gamma,\,\texttt{succ}(M):\mathsf{Ok}
on the left. So
\texttt{succ}(M\theta)
evaluates. The only values headed by
succ
are numerals, so it follows that
M\theta
evaluates to a numeral and then the conclusion follows from the induction hypothesis.

(OkPL)
:

Suppose
\theta
satisfies
\Gamma,\,\mathsf{pred}(M):\mathsf{Ok}
on the left. So
\mathsf{pred}(M\theta)
evaluates. Since
\mathsf{pred}(M\theta)
can only reduce to a numeral, and this requires that
M\theta
evaluate to a numeral, the conclusion follows from the induction hypothesis.

(OkPrL)
:

Suppose
\theta
satisfies
\Gamma,\,(M_{1},M_{2}):\mathsf{Ok}
. Then
(M_{1}\theta,M_{2}\theta)
evaluates and it must be that it evaluates to a pair of values
(V_{1},V_{2})
. Then it follows that each
M_{i}
evaluates and so the conclusion follows from the induction hypothesis.

(OkApL1)
:

Suppose
\theta
satisfies
\Gamma,\,M\,N:\mathsf{Ok}
on the left, so
M\theta\,N\theta
evaluates. Then it must be that
M\theta
evaluates to a fixpoint abstraction. Since, for any
A
,
[\![\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A]\!]
is the set of all such, the conclusion follows from the induction hypothesis.

(OkApL2)
:

Suppose
\theta
satisfies
\Gamma,\,M\,N:\mathsf{Ok}
, so
M\theta\,N\theta
evaluates. Then, in particular,
N\theta
evaluates, and the conclusion follows from the induction hypothesis.

∎

B.3.
Proof of Theorem
4.5

Proof.

We prove each separately.

•

The term
\lambda xy.\,(\lambda z.\,\mathsf{pred}(z))\,x\in\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{Nat}]\!]
because, reasoning according to the contrapositive of the definition, if a given value
V
is not a numeral, then it follows that
\lambda y.\,(\lambda z.\,\mathsf{pred}(z))\,V\notin\mathcal{T}[\![\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{Nat}]\!]
, since the body will get stuck on any argument. However, replacing
\lambda z.\,\mathsf{pred}(z)
by
div
, we have that
\lambda xy.\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}}\,x\notin\mathcal{T}_{\!\bot}[\![\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{Nat}]\!]
. Even when a given value
V
is not a numeral, we yet have
\lambda y.\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{div}}}\,V\in\mathcal{T}[\![\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{Nat}]\!]
, since the body diverges on any input.

•

First observe that
\lambda wxyz.\,(\mathsf{fix}\,f(u).\,\mathsf{ifz}\,u\,\mathsf{then}\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{add}}}\,(y,z)\,\mathsf{else}\,f\,(\mathsf{pred}(u)))\,x
is not a value of the type
\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{Nat}
. To see why, let us abbreviate the conditional subterm by
M
and show that when applied to a value
id
that is not a numeral, it may yet behave like a function of type
\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{Nat}
. To see that
\lambda xyz.\,(\mathsf{fix}\,f(u).\,M)\,x
is a value in
[\![\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{Nat}]\!]
, we let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and show that
\lambda yz.\,(\mathsf{fix}\,f(u).\,M)\,\underline{n}
is a value in
[\![\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{Nat}]\!]
. To see this, we use the contrapositive of the definition and show that when applied to any non-numeral value, the function returned does not satisfy
\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{Nat}
. So let
V\notin[\![\mathsf{Nat}]\!]
be a non-numeral value. Then the function
\lambda z.\,(\mathsf{fix}\,f(u).\,\mathsf{ifz}\,u\,\mathsf{then}\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{add}}}(V,z)\,\mathsf{else}\,f\,(\mathsf{pred}(u)))\,\underline{n}
is not in
[\![\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{Nat}]\!]
because, when applied to
\underline{0}
it will reduce to
\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{add}}}(V,\underline{0})
, but
V
is not a numeral.

Next, observe that any term
\lambda wxyz.\,(\mathsf{fix}^{k}f(u).\,\mathsf{ifz}\,u\,\mathsf{then}\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{add}}}\,(y,z)\,\mathsf{else}\,f\,(\mathsf{pred}(u)))\,x
, in which we have replaced the fixpoint by a particular finite approximant,
is
a value of the type
\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{Nat}
. To see this, let us abbreviate the conditional subterm again by
M
and let
W\notin[\![\mathsf{Nat}]\!]
. We show that, therefore,
\lambda xyz.\,(\mathsf{fix}^{k}f(u).\,M)\,x
is not in
[\![\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{Nat}]\!]
. Take
x\coloneqq\underline{k+1}
as witness. Then
\lambda yz.\,(\mathsf{fix}^{k}f(u).\,M)\,\underline{k+1}
is not in
[\![\mathsf{Nat}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{Nat}]\!]
. To see why, take
y\coloneqq\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{id}}}\notin[\![\mathsf{Nat}]\!]
as the witness, and we find that yet
\lambda z.\,(\mathsf{fix}^{k}f(u).\,\mathsf{ifz}\,u\,\mathsf{then}\,\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{add}}}\,(\textsf{\lx@text@underline{\smash{id}}},z)\,\mathsf{else}\,f\,(\mathsf{pred}(u)))\,\underline{k+1}
is actually in the type
\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{Nat}
, because when given any numeral as input, it will diverge.

∎

Appendix C
Additional Material in Support of Section
5

In this appendix, we give the proofs of Theorem
5.5
, showing that two-sided judgements of the specified form are subsumed by one-sided judgements, and Theorem
5.7
, showing syntactic soundness of the one-sided system. The latter requires first establishing a kind of inversion lemma for values, that shows that they can only be given the types one would expect, and then substitution lemmas, and preservation.

C.1.
Proof of Theorem
5.5

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
\Gamma,\,M:A\vdash\Delta
.

(Id)
:

In this case, there are two cases. If
M
is some variable
x
and
x:A\in\Delta
, then we may conclude
\Gamma\cup\Delta^{c}\vdash x:A^{c}
by
(Id)
. Otherwise,
M
is not a variable, but there is a variable typing
x:A
in both
\Gamma
and
\Delta
. In that case,
\Gamma\cup\Delta^{c}
will contain a contradiction, and the result follows by
(Contra)
.

(LetL1)
:

In this case
M
is of shape
\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=N\,\mathsf{in}\,P
and we may assume (i)
\Gamma\cup\Delta^{c}\vdash P:A^{c}
. Then the result follows from
(Let3)
with
A
set to
A^{c}

(LetR)
:

In this case
M
is
\mathsf{let}\,(x_{1},x_{2})=N\,\mathsf{in}\,P
and we can assume (i)
\Gamma\cup\Delta^{c}\vdash N:B\times C
and (ii)
\Gamma\cup\Delta^{c},\,x:B,\,y:C\vdash P:A
. Then the result follows immediately from
(Let1)
.

(LetL2)
:

In this case
M
is of shape
\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=N\,\mathsf{in}\,P
and we can assume (i)
\Gamma,\,\Delta^{c}\,x:B^{c}\vdash P:A^{c}
, (ii)
\Gamma\cup\Delta^{c},\,y:C^{c}\vdash P:A^{c}
and (iii)
\Gamma\cup\Delta^{c}\vdash M:(B\times C)^{c}
. The conclusion follows immediately by instantiating
A
in
(Let2)
by
A^{c}
.

(IfZR)
:

In this case,
M
is of shape
\mathsf{ifz}\,N\,\mathsf{then}\,P_{1}\,\mathsf{else}\,P_{2}
and we may assume (i)
\Gamma\cup\Delta^{c}\vdash N:\mathsf{Nat}
, (ii)
\Gamma\cup\Delta^{c}\vdash P_{1}:A
, (iii)
\Gamma\cup\Delta^{c},\,x:\mathsf{Nat}\vdash P_{2}:A
. Then the desired conclusion follows immediately from (ii) and (iii) by
(IfZ1)
.

(IfZL1)
:

In this case,
M
is of shape
\mathsf{ifz}\,N\,\mathsf{then}\,P_{1}\,\mathsf{else}\,P_{2}
and we may assume (i)
\Gamma\cup\Delta^{c}\vdash N:\mathsf{Nat}^{c}
. Then the result follows immediately by
(IfZ2)
.

(IfZL2)
:

In this case,
M
is of shape
\mathsf{ifz}\,N\,\mathsf{then}\,P_{1}\,\mathsf{else}\,P_{2}
and we may assume (i)
\Gamma\cup\Delta^{c}\vdash P_{1}:A^{c}
and (ii)
\Gamma\cup\Delta^{c},\,x:\mathsf{Nat}\vdash P_{2}:A^{c}
. Then the result follows immediately by
(IfZ1)
.

(CompL)
:

In this case, we may assume
\Gamma\cup\Delta^{c}\vdash M:A
and then the result is immediate.

(CompR)
:

In this case, we may assume
\Gamma\cup\Delta^{c}\vdash M:A^{c}
and again, the result is immediate.

(Disj)
:

In this case, we may assume
\Gamma\cup\Delta^{c}\vdash M:B
and
A\mathrel{||}B
. Then the result follows from
(Dis)
.

(AppL)
:

In this case,
M
is of shape
P\,N
and we may assume (i)
\Gamma\cup\Delta^{c}\vdash P:B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
and (ii)
\Gamma\cup\Delta^{c}\vdash N:B^{c}
. Recall that
B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
is just an abbreviation for
B^{c}\to A^{c}
in the one-sided system. Hence, the result follows immediately from
(App)
.

(OkApL1)
:

In this case,
M
is of shape
P\,N
and we may assume (i)
\Gamma\cup\Delta^{c}\vdash P:(\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A)^{c}=(\mathsf{Ok}^{c}\to A^{c})^{c}
. Therefore, the result follows from
(App2)
.

(OkApL2)
:

In this case,
M
is of shape
P\,N
and we may assume (i)
\Gamma\cup\Delta^{c}\vdash N:\mathsf{Ok}^{c}
. Then the desired conclusion follows immediately by
(App3)
.

(OkL)
:

In this case we may assume
\Gamma\cup\Delta^{c}\vdash M:\mathsf{Ok}
. Hence, the result follows from
(OkC2)
.

(OkR)
:

In this case the result follows immediately from
(Ok)
.

(OkSL)
,
(SuccL)
:

In these cases,
M
is of shape
\texttt{succ}(N)
and we may assume
\Gamma\cup\Delta^{c}\vdash N:\mathsf{Nat}^{c}
. Therefore, the results follow from
(Succ2)
.

(OkPrL)
:

In this case,
M
is of shape
(N_{1},N_{2})
and we may assume
\Gamma\cup\Delta^{c}\vdash M_{i}:\mathsf{Ok}^{c}
. Then the result follows immediately by
(Pair2)
.

(PairL)
:

In this case,
M
is of shape
(N_{1},N_{2})
,
A
is of shape
A_{1}\times A_{2}
. Let us consider the
i=1
case, the other is symmetrical. We may assume that
\Gamma\cup\Delta^{c}\vdash N_{1}:A_{1}^{c}
. Then the result follows from
(Pair3)
.

(FixsR)
:

The result follows from the induction hypothesis and
(Fix)
.

(FixnR)
:

In this case,
M
is of shape
\lambda x.\,P
and
A
is of shape
B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
. We may assume
\Gamma\cup\Delta^{c},\,x:B^{c}\vdash M:A^{c}
. Since, in the one-sided system,
B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
is an abbreviation for
B^{c}\to A^{c}
, the result follows from
(Fix)
.

(AppR)
:

The result follows from the induction hypotheses and
(App1)
.

(SuccR)
:

The result follows from the induction hypotheses and
(Succ1)
.

(ZeroR)
:

The result follows from the induction hypotheses and
(Zero)
.

∎

C.2.
Proof of Soundness

Lemma C.1 (Value Inversion).

Suppose
\vdash V:A
.

•

If
V
is of shape
zero
then
A=\mathsf{Nat}
or
A
is of shape
B^{c}
with
B\mathrel{||}\mathsf{Nat}

•

If
V
is of shape
\texttt{succ}(W)
then
A=\mathsf{Nat}
or
A
is of shape
B^{c}
with
B\mathrel{||}\mathsf{Nat}
.

•

If
V
is of shape
(W_{1},W_{2})
then either:

–

A
is of shape
B\times C

–

or,
A
is of shape
B^{c}
and
B
is not a pair type nor
\mathsf{Ok}
.

–

or,
A
is of shape
(B_{1}\times B_{2})^{c}
and there is
i
with
\vdash W_{i}:B_{i}^{c}

•

If
V
is of shape
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M
then either:

–

there are types
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
such that
A=B_{1}\to B_{2}
and
f:B_{1}\to B_{2},\,x:B_{1}\vdash M:B_{2}

–

or,
A
is of shape
B^{c}
with
B
not an arrow type nor
\mathsf{Ok}
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the derivation. In cases
(OkC)
,
(Contra)
and
(Var)
, the conclusion is immediate because the rules only apply to judgements with a non-empty environment. In cases
(Let1)
,
(Let2)
,
(App1)
,
(App2)
,
(App3)
,
(IfZ1)
and
(IfZ2)
the conclusion is immediate since the subject is not a value.

(OkC2)
:

We suppose the induction hypothesis, but then we obtain a contradiction because, by case analysis on
V
, it follows that
\mathsf{Ok}^{c}
cannot be
\mathsf{Ok}^{c}
. Hence, the result follows vacuously.

(Dis)
:

In this case,
A
has shape
A^{\prime c}
and we assume
B\mathrel{||}A^{\prime}
. We proceed by case analysis on
V
. Note that,
B
cannot be of shape
B^{\prime c}
.

•:

If
V
is a numeral
\underline{n}
, then it follows from the induction hypothesis that
B
must be
\mathsf{Nat}
. Therefore,
A
is of shape
A^{\prime c}
with
A^{\prime}\mathrel{||}\mathsf{Nat}
, as required.

•:

If
V
is a pair
(W_{1},W_{2})
, then it follows from the induction hypothesis that
B
must have shape
B_{1}\times B_{2}
. Therefore,
A^{\prime}
is not a pair type nor
\mathsf{Ok}
.

•:

If
V
is an abstraction
\lambda x.\,P
, then it follows from the induction hypothesis that
B
must have shape
B_{1}\to B_{2}
. Therefore
A^{\prime}
is not an arrow type nor
\mathsf{Ok}
.

(Zero)
,
(Succ1)
:

In these cases,
V
is a numeral and
A
is
\mathsf{Nat}
.

(Succ2)
:

In this case,
V
is a numeral. We assume the induction hypothesis, but then we obtain a contradiction because
\mathsf{Nat}^{c}
is not of shape
B^{c}
with
B\mathrel{||}\mathsf{Nat}
.

(Fix)
:

In this case,
V
is a fixpoint abstraction and
A
has shape
B_{1}\to B_{2}
.

(Pair1)
:

In this case,
V
is of shape
(W_{1},W_{2})
and
A
is of shape
A\times B
and
\Gamma\vdash W_{1}:A
and
\Gamma\vdash W_{2}:B

(Pair2)
:

In this case, we suppose the induction hypothesis, but then we obtain a contradiction because the type of
W_{i}
cannot be
\mathsf{Ok}^{c}
.

(Pair3)
:

In this case,
V
is of shape
(W_{1},W_{2})
and
A
is of shape
(B_{1}\times B_{2})
and there is an
i
such that
\Gamma\vdash W_{i}:B_{i}^{c}
.

∎

Lemma C.2.

Suppose
\Gamma
is a type environment and
V
a closed value and
\Gamma\vdash M:A
.

•

If
x:B\in\Gamma
and
\Gamma\setminus\{x:B\}\vdash V:B
then
\Gamma\setminus\{x:B\}\vdash M[V/x]:A
.

•

If
x\notin\mathsf{Subjects}(\Gamma)
, then
\Gamma\vdash M[V/x]:A
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the derivation.

(Ok)
:

In this case,
\Gamma\vdash M[V/x]:\mathsf{Ok}
by
(Ok)
.

(OkC1)
:

In this case,
\Gamma
contains
y:\mathsf{Ok}^{c}
and we consider two cases. If
x=y
, then we may assume
\Gamma\setminus\{x:B\}\vdash V:\mathsf{Ok}^{c}
and this gives the result since
x[V/x]=V
. Otherwise, the result follows from
(OkC1)
.

(Zero)
:

In this case, the result follows again from
(Zero)
.

(Contra)
:

This rule does not apply when
\Gamma
is a type environment.

(Var)
:

In this case,
\Gamma
contains
y:A^{\prime}
. If
x=y
then
A=A^{\prime}
and we may assume
\Gamma-\{x:B\}\vdash V:A
. Then this gives the result since
x[V/x]=V
. Otherwise, the result follows from
(Var)
since
y[V/x]=y
.

In the remaining cases, the result follows from the induction hypotheses and the fact that bound variables in the conclusion may be assumed distinct from
x
.
∎

Theorem C.3.

If
\vdash M:A
and
M\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ N
then
\vdash N:A
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the derivation. The cases
(OkC1)
,
(Contra)
and
(Var)
do not apply since there are no assumptions.

(Ok)
:

In this case, the result follows immediately from
(Ok)
.

(OkC2)
:

It follows from the induction hypothesis that
\vdash N:\mathsf{Ok}^{c}
and the result follows from
(OkC2)
.

(Dis)
:

In this case,
A
is of shape
A^{\prime c}
. Suppose
A\mathrel{||}B
. It follows from the induction hypothesis that
\vdash N:B
and hence the result follows from
(Dis)
.

(Zero)
:

In this case,
M
is
zero
and we obtain a contradiction from
M\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ N
.

(Succ1)
:

In this case,
M
is of shape
\texttt{succ}(P)
and
A=\mathsf{Nat}
. Thus it must be that
P
makes a step to some
Q
. It follows from the induction hypothesis that
\vdash Q:\mathsf{Nat}
and the result follows from
(Succ1)
.

(Succ2)
:

In this case,
M
is of shape
\texttt{succ}(P)
and thus it must be that
P
makes a step to some
Q
. It follows from the induction hypothesis that
\vdash Q:\mathsf{Nat}^{c}
and thus the result follows from
(Succ2)
.

(Fix)
:

In this case,
M
does not make a step.

(Let3)
:

In this case,
M
has shape
\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=Q\,\mathsf{in}\,P
and there are two cases. If
Q
makes a step to some
Q^{\prime}
then the result follows from the induction hypothesis. Otherwise, it must be that
Q
is a pair
(V,W)
and
N=P[V/x,W/y]
. Since
x
and
y
do not occur in
\Gamma
, the result follows from the substitution lemma.

(Let2)
:

In this case,
M
has shape
\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=Q\,\mathsf{in}\,P
and there are two cases. If
Q
makes a step to some
Q^{\prime}
then the result follows from the induction hypothesis. Otherwise, it must be that
Q
is a pair
(V,W)
and
N=P[V/x,W/y]
. Hence, it follows by value inversion on the first premise that either
\Gamma\vdash V:B_{1}^{c}
or
\Gamma\vdash W:B_{2}^{c}
. In either case, we can use the corresponding second premise and the substitution lemma (both parts) to conclude.

(Let1)
:

In this case,
M
has shape
\mathsf{let}\,(x,y)=Q\,\mathsf{in}\,P
and there are two cases. If
Q
makes a step to some
Q^{\prime}
then the result follows from the induction hypothesis. Otherwise, it must be that
Q
is a pair
(V,W)
and
N=P[V/x,W/y]
. It follows from value inversion that
\Gamma\vdash V:B
and
\Gamma\vdash W:C
and so we can conclude using the substitution lemma.

(App1)
:

In this case,
M
has shape
PQ
. There are two cases. If
P
or
Q
makes a step, then the result follows from the induction hypothesis. Otherwise, it must be that
P
is an abstraction
\lambda x.\,P^{\prime}
and
Q
is a value. By value inversion, it must be that
x:B\vdash P^{\prime}:A
and then the result follows from the substitution lemma.

(App2)
:

In this case,
M
has shape
PQ
. There are three cases. If
P
makes a step, then the result follows from the induction hypothesis. If
Q
makes a step, the result follows immediately from
(App2)
. Otherwise
P
must be an abstraction
\lambda x.\,P^{\prime}
but it follows from value inversion that this is impossible with type
(\mathsf{Ok}^{c}\to A)^{c}
.

(App3)
:

In this case,
M
has shape
PQ
. There are three cases. If
Q
makes a step, then the result follows from the induction hypothesis. If
P
makes a step, the result follows immediately from
(App3)
. Otherwise, it must be that
Q
is a value, but it follows from value inversion that this is impossible with type
\mathsf{Ok}^{c}
.

(Pair1)
:

In this case,
M
is a pair
(P,Q)
and
A
has shape
B_{1}\times B_{2}
. It can only be that either
P
makes a step or
Q
makes a step, and then the result follows from the induction hypothesis in each case.

(Pair2)
:

In this case,
M
is a pair
(P_{1},P_{2})
and
i\in\{1,2\}
. If
P_{i}
makes a step, then the result follows from the induction hypothesis; otherwise the result follows immediately from
(Pair2)
.

(Pair3)
:

In this case,
M
is a pair
(M_{1},M_{2})
and
i\in\{1,2\}
and
A
has shape
(A_{1}\times A_{2})^{c}
. If
M_{i}
makes a step, then the result follows from the induction hypothesis; otherwise the result follows immediately from
(Pair3)
.

(IfZ1)
:

In this case,
M
is of shape
\mathsf{ifz}\,Q\,\mathsf{then}\,P_{1}\,\mathsf{else}\,P_{2}
. There are two cases. If
Q
makes a step, then the result follows from the induction hypothesis. Otherwise,
M
is a numeral. However, it follows from value inversion that this is impossible with type
\mathsf{Nat}^{c}
.

(IfZ2)
:

In this case,
M
is of shape
\mathsf{ifz}\,Q\,\mathsf{then}\,P_{1}\,\mathsf{else}\,P_{2}
. There are two cases. If
Q
makes a step, then the result follows from the induction hypothesis. Otherwise,
M
is a numeral. If
M=\underline{0}
, then
M\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ P_{1}
and the result follows from the first premise. Otherwise,
M\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ P_{2}
and the result follows from the second premise.

∎

C.2.1.
Proof of Theorem
5.7
(One-Sided Syntactic Soundness)

Proof.

Suppose
\vdash M:\mathsf{Ok}^{c}
. For the purpose of obtaining a contradiction, suppose
M
reaches a value
V
. Then it follows from preservation that
\vdash V:\mathsf{Ok}^{c}
, but this contradicts the value inversion lemma.
∎

Appendix D
Additional Material in Support of Section
6

This section contains: a statement (and proof) of (left) weakening for the constrained type system given in Section
6
, a definition for an algorithmic version of the constrained type system rules (where the sub-type rules have been distributed throughout the other rules in the standard way), the proof of its correctness in the form of a soundness and completeness proof (relative to the original constraint type system rules), a type inference algorithm, and its correctness in the form of a soundness and completeness proof (relative to the algorithmic typing rules).

Lemma D.1 (Left Weakening).

If
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
then
\Gamma,\,M:A\vdash\Delta
.

Proof.

Let
\Gamma,\,\Delta
be environments,
N
be a term and
\tau
be a type such that
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
. We now proceed by induction on
\vdash
:

(GVar)
:

This case is trivial by the use of
(GVar)
.

(Id)
:

This case is trivial by the use of
(Id)
.

(VarK)
:

This case is trivial by the use of
(VarK)
.

(SubL)
:

Let
\Gamma,\,M:A\vdash\Delta
,
\Gamma,\,M:B\vdash\Delta
, and
A\sqsubseteq B
.

By the inductive hypothesis,
\Gamma,\,N:\tau,\,M:B\vdash\Delta
.

As
A\sqsubseteq B
, by
(SubL)
, we have
\Gamma,\,N:\tau,\,M:A\vdash\Delta
as required.

(SubR)
:

Let
\Gamma\vdash M:A
,
\Gamma\vdash M:B
, and
B\sqsubseteq A
.

By the inductive hypothesis, we have
\Gamma,\,N:\tau\vdash M:B
.

As
B\sqsubseteq A
, by
(SubR)
, we have
\Gamma,\,N:\tau\vdash M:A
.

(FixsR)
:

Let
\Gamma\vdash(\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M):B\to A
and
\Gamma,\,f:B\to A,\,x:B\vdash M:A
.

By the inductive hypothesis, we have
\Gamma,\,N:\tau,\,f:B\to A,\,x:B\vdash M:A
.

By
(FixsR)
, we have
\Gamma,\,N:\tau\vdash(\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M):B\to A
as required.

(FixnR)
:

Let
\Gamma\vdash(\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M):B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
and
\Gamma,\,f:B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A,\,M:A\vdash x:B
.

By the inductive hypothesis, we have
\Gamma,\,N:\tau,\,f:B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A,\,M:A\vdash x:B
.

By
(FixnR)
, we have
\Gamma,\,N:\tau\vdash(\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M):B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
as required.

(AppL)
:

Let
\Gamma,\,(M\ N^{\prime}):A\vdash\Delta
,
\Gamma\vdash M:B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
, and
\Gamma,\,N^{\prime}:B\vdash\Delta
.

By the inductive hypothesis, we have
\Gamma,\,N:\tau\vdash M:B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
and
\Gamma,\,N:\tau,\,N^{\prime}:B\vdash\Delta
.

By
(AppL)
, we have
\Gamma,\,N:\tau,\,(M\ N^{\prime}):A\vdash\Delta
as required.

(AppL)
:

Let
\Gamma\vdash(M\ N^{\prime}):A
,
\Gamma\vdash M:B\to A
, and
\Gamma\vdash N^{\prime}:B
.

By the inductive hypothesis,
\Gamma,\,N:\tau\vdash M:B\to A
and
\Gamma,\,N:\tau\vdash N^{\prime}:B
.

By
(AppR)
, we have
\Gamma,\,N:\tau\vdash(M\ N^{\prime}):A
as required.

(CnsL)
:

Let
\Gamma,\,c(M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}):c(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})+\Sigma_{d\in\mathcal{C}\backslash\{c\}}(d(\vec{A_{d}}))\vdash\Delta
and
\Gamma,\,M_{i}:A_{i}\vdash\Delta
for some
i
.

By the inductive hypothesis, we have
\Gamma,\,N:\tau,\,M_{i}:A_{i}\vdash\Delta
.

By
rlnm{CnsL}
, we have
\Gamma,\,N:\tau,\,c(M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}):c(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})+\Sigma_{d\in\mathcal{C}\backslash\{c\}}(d(\vec{A_{d}}))\vdash\Delta
as required.

(CnsR)
:

Let
\Gamma\vdash c(M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}):c(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})
and
\Gamma\vdash M_{i}:A_{i}
for all
i
.

By the inductive hypothesis,
\Gamma,\,N:\tau\vdash M_{i}:A_{i}(\forall i)
.

By
(CnsR)
, we have
\Gamma,\,N:\tau\vdash c(M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}):c(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})
as required.

(MchL)
:

Let
\Gamma,\,\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{|_{i=1}^{k}(p_{i}\mapsto P_{i})\}:A\vdash\Delta
,
\Gamma,\,P_{i}:A\vdash x:B_{x}\;(\forall i.\forall x\in\mathsf{FV}{(p_{i})})
, and
\Gamma,\,(M,P_{i}):(p_{i}[B_{x}/x\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}{(p_{i})}],A)\vdash\Delta\;(\forall i)
.

By the inductive hypothesis we have:

(1):

\Gamma,\,N:\tau,\,P_{i}:A\vdash x:B_{x}\;(\forall i.\forall x\in\mathsf{FV}{(p_{i})})

(2):

\Gamma,\,N:\tau,\,(M,P_{i}):(p_{i}[B_{x}/x\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}{(p_{i})}],A)\vdash\Delta\;(\forall i)

By
(MchL)
, we have
\Gamma,\,N:\tau,\,\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{|_{i=1}^{k}(p_{i}\mapsto P_{i})\}:A\vdash\Delta
as required.

(MchR)
:

Let
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{|_{i=1}^{k}(p_{i}\mapsto P_{i})\}:A
,
\Gamma\vdash M:\Sigma_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}[B_{x}/x\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})]
, and
\Gamma\cup\{x:B_{x}\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})\}\vdash P_{i}:A(\forall i)
.

By the inductive hypothesis we have:

(1):

\Gamma,\,N:\tau\vdash M:\Sigma_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}[B_{x}/x\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})]

(2):

\Gamma,\,N:\tau\cup\{x:B_{x}\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})\}\vdash P_{i}:A(\forall i)

By
(MchR)
, we have
\Gamma,\,N:\tau\vdash\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{|_{i=1}^{k}(p_{i}\mapsto P_{i})\}:A
as required.

(CnsK)
:

Let
\Gamma,\,c(M_{1},\cdot,M_{n}):\mathsf{Ok}\vdash\Delta
and
\Gamma,\,M_{i}:\mathsf{Ok}\vdash\Delta
for some
i
.

By the inductive hypothesis, we have
\Gamma,\,N:\tau,\,M_{i}:\mathsf{Ok}\vdash\Delta
for some
i
.

By
(CnsK)
, we have
\Gamma,\,N:\tau,\,c(M_{1},\cdot,M_{n}):\mathsf{Ok}\vdash\Delta
as required.

(FunK)
:

Let
\Gamma,\,M\ N^{\prime}:\mathsf{Ok}\vdash\Delta
and
\Gamma,\,M:\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A\vdash\Delta
.

By the inductive hypothesis, we have
\Gamma,\,N:\tau,\,M:\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A\vdash\Delta
.

By
(FunK)
, we have
\Gamma,\,N:\tau,\,M\ N^{\prime}:\mathsf{Ok}\vdash\Delta
as required.

(CnsDl)
:

This case is trivial by the use of
(CnsDL)
.

(FixDL)
:

This case is trivial by the use of
(FixDL)
.

∎

Definition D.2.

Given a set of typing and subtype formulas
\Gamma
, write
\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)
for the subset of subtype formulas, and
\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma)
for the typing formulas.
Given two sets of typing and subtype formulas
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
write
\Gamma\sqsubseteq\Gamma^{\prime}
just if for all typings
M:A\in\Gamma
, there is a typing
M:B\in\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\Gamma\vdash A\sqsubseteq B
.

Structural

\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 90.81252pt\hbox{}}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 3.00891pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(Inst2)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 3.88951pt\hbox{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=129.74664pt,height=0.51663pt}}}\thinspace\raise-1.38951pt\hbox{$C\vdash C^{\prime}[\vv{B}/\vv{a}]$ and $C\vdash A[\vv{B}/\vv{a}]\sqsubseteq A^{\prime}$}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-2.59784pt\hbox{\hskip 28.93922pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma,\,f:\forall\vv{a}.C^{\prime}\Rightarrow A\mid C\vdash_{2}f:A^{\prime}\mathstrut$}}}}$}\\[14.22636pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 69.18309pt\hbox{}}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 2.15207pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(Var2)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 3.03267pt\hbox{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=88.28226pt,height=0.51663pt}}}\thinspace\raise-1.67296pt\hbox{$C\vdash A\sqsubseteq B$}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.741pt\hbox{\hskip 28.04199pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma,\,x:A\mid C\vdash_{2}x:B\mathstrut$}}}}$}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 61.79611pt\hbox{}}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 2.15207pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(VarK2)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 3.03267pt\hbox{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=61.70258pt,height=0.51663pt}}}\thinspace\raise-1.67296pt\hbox{$C\vdash\mathsf{Ok}\sqsubseteq A$}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.741pt\hbox{\hskip 33.94485pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\mid C\vdash_{2}x:A\mathstrut$}}}}$}\end{array}

Functions

\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 35.42543pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,f:A,\,x:B_{1}\mid C\vdash_{2}M:B_{2}}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-1.91115pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 2.32222pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(FixsR2)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 3.20282pt\hbox{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=126.94543pt,height=0.51663pt}}}\thinspace\raise-1.70282pt\hbox{$C\vdash B_{1}\to B_{2}\sqsubseteq A$}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.91115pt\hbox{\hskip 43.80379pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\mid C\vdash_{2}(\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M):A\mathstrut$}}}}$}\\[14.22636pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 36.556pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,f:A,\,M:B_{1}\mid C\vdash_{2}x:B_{2}}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-2.08893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(FixnR2)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 3.3806pt\hbox{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=126.94543pt,height=0.51663pt}}}\thinspace\raise-1.3806pt\hbox{$C\vdash B_{2}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B_{1}\sqsubseteq A$}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-2.08893pt\hbox{\hskip 44.93436pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\mid C\vdash_{2}(\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M):A\mathstrut$}}}}$}\\[14.22636pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 35.80598pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma\mid C\vdash_{2}M:B_{1}}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\hskip 17.22217pt{\Gamma\mid C\vdash_{2}N:B_{2}}\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-1.91115pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 2.32222pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(AppR2)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 3.20282pt\hbox{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=153.25919pt,height=0.51663pt}}}\thinspace\raise-1.70282pt\hbox{$C\vdash B_{1}\sqsubseteq B_{2}\to A$}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.91115pt\hbox{\hskip 75.35924pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{2}(M\ N):A\mathstrut$}}}}$}\\[14.22636pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 34.93098pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma\mid C\vdash_{2}M:B_{1}}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\hskip 17.22217pt{\Gamma,\,N:B_{2}\vdash_{2}\Delta}\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-2.08893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(AppL2)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 3.3806pt\hbox{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=148.7302pt,height=0.51663pt}}}\thinspace\raise-1.3806pt\hbox{$C\vdash B_{1}\sqsubseteq B_{2}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A$}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-2.08893pt\hbox{\hskip 58.56633pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma,\,(M\ N):A\mid C\vdash_{2}\Delta\mathstrut$}}}}$}\end{array}

Constructors

\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 56.5093pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,M_{i}:A_{i}\mid C\vdash_{2}\Delta}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-4.6167pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 5.02777pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(CnsL2)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 5.90839pt\hbox{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=127.10541pt,height=0.51663pt}}}\thinspace\raise 0.64717pt\hbox{$C\vdash A\sqsubseteq\Sigma_{d\in\mathcal{C}\setminus\{c\}}\,d(\vv{A_{d}})+c(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})$}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-4.6167pt\hbox{\hskip 33.5643pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma,\,c(M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}):A\mid C\vdash_{2}\Delta\mathstrut$}}}}$}\\[14.22636pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 45.66179pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma\mid C\vdash_{2}M_{i}:A_{i}\;(\forall i)}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-2.58893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 3.0pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(CnsR2)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 3.8806pt\hbox{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=114.32764pt,height=0.51663pt}}}\thinspace\raise-1.3806pt\hbox{$C\vdash c(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})\sqsubseteq A$}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-2.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 34.4393pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\mid C\vdash_{2}c(M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}):A\mathstrut$}}}}$}\end{array}

Pattern Matching

\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 44.98228pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma,\,(M,P_{i}):A^{\prime}_{i}\mid C\vdash_{2}\Delta\;(\forall i)\\[2.84526pt]
\Gamma,\,P_{i}:A_{x}\mid C\vdash_{2}x:B_{x}\;(\forall i.\forall x\in\mathsf{FV}{(p_{i})})\end{array}}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-20.58893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 21.0pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(MchL2)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 21.8806pt\hbox{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=182.03772pt,height=0.51663pt}}}\thinspace\raise-1.3806pt\hbox{$\begin{array}[]{l}C\vdash A\sqsubseteq A_{i}\;(\forall i)\\
C\vdash(B_{i},A_{i})\sqsubseteq A_{i}^{\prime}\;(\forall i)\\
C\vdash A\sqsubseteq A_{x}\;(\forall i.\forall x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i}))\\
C\vdash p_{i}[B_{x}/x\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})]\sqsubseteq B_{i}\;(\forall i)\end{array}$}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-20.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 36.06989pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma,\,\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{|_{i=1}^{k}(p_{i}\mapsto P_{i})\}:A\mid C\vdash_{2}\Delta\mathstrut$}}}}$}\\[14.22636pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 37.26553pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\mid C\vdash_{2}M:B\\[2.84526pt]
\Gamma\cup\{x:B_{x}\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})\}\mid C\vdash_{2}P_{i}:A_{i}\;(\forall i)\end{array}}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-9.08394pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 9.49501pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(MchR2)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 10.37561pt\hbox{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=169.25995pt,height=0.51663pt}}}\thinspace\raise-1.3806pt\hbox{$\begin{array}[]{l}C\vdash B\sqsubseteq\Sigma_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}[B_{x}/x\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})]\\
C\vdash A_{i}\sqsubseteq A\;(\forall i)\end{array}$}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-9.08394pt\hbox{\hskip 36.94489pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\mid C\vdash_{2}\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{|_{i=1}^{k}(p_{i}\mapsto P_{i})\}:A\mathstrut$}}}}$}\\[14.22636pt]
\end{array}

Evaluation

\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 58.04724pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,M_{i}:B\mid C\vdash_{2}\Delta}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-1.741pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 2.15207pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(CnsK2)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 3.03267pt\hbox{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=127.10541pt,height=0.51663pt}}}\thinspace\raise-1.67296pt\hbox{$C\vdash\mathsf{Ok}\sqsubseteq B$}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.741pt\hbox{\hskip 34.84209pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma,\,c(M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}):A\mid C\vdash_{2}\Delta\mathstrut$}}}}$}\\[14.22636pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 41.93619pt\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\Gamma,\,M:B\mid C\vdash_{2}\Delta}\mathstrut$}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathstrut$}}}\vskip-2.08893pt}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(FunK2)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 3.3806pt\hbox{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=93.6817pt,height=0.51663pt}}}\thinspace\raise-1.3806pt\hbox{$C\vdash\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A\sqsubseteq B$}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-2.08893pt\hbox{\hskip 35.1671pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma,\,M\ N:A\mid C\vdash_{2}\Delta\mathstrut$}}}}$}\end{array}

Disjointness

\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 103.6518pt\hbox{}}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 2.32222pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(CnsDL21)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 3.20282pt\hbox{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=125.39697pt,height=0.51663pt}}}\thinspace\raise-1.70282pt\hbox{$CA\sqsubseteq B_{1}\to B_{2}$}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-1.91115pt\hbox{\hskip 43.95334pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma,\,c(M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}):A\mid C\vdash\Delta\mathstrut$}}}}$}\\[14.22636pt]

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 103.6518pt\hbox{}}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 5.02777pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(CnsDL23)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 5.90839pt\hbox{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=125.39697pt,height=0.51663pt}}}\thinspace\raise 0.64717pt\hbox{$C\vdash A\sqsubseteq\Sigma_{d\in\mathcal{C}\setminus\{c\}}\,d(\vv{A_{d}})$}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-4.6167pt\hbox{\hskip 43.95334pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma,\,c(M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}):A\mid C\vdash\Delta\mathstrut$}}}}$}\\[14.22636pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 91.17113pt\hbox{}}\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 3.0pt\hbox{{\footnotesize(FixDL2)}}\thinspace\hbox{\raise 3.8806pt\hbox{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\vrule width=113.48026pt,height=0.51663pt}}}\thinspace\raise-1.3806pt\hbox{$C\vdash A\sqsubseteq\Sigma_{c\in\mathcal{C}}c(\vv{A_{c}})$}}}}}\vbox{\vskip-2.58893pt\hbox{\hskip 37.43103pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma,\,\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M:A\mid C\vdash\Delta\mathstrut$}}}}$}\end{array}
