Line 5 generates a tuple of length
k
by enumerating every arrangement of typings for the
k
pairs
(Q,P_{i})
. Hence, the correct sequence of types can be picked according to the inductive hypothesis (point 1).

Line 6 generates a tuple by enumerating every arrangement of typings for each (left) typing of
P_{i}
with delta
x:b_{(i,x)}
(for each
i
and
x
). Hence, the correct sequence of types can be picked according to the inductive hypothesis (point 2).

Hence, the required witness judgment is generated.

As each recursive call produces its own, independent, fresh variables, the pairwise intersection of any two distinct
\sigma
given by the inductive hypotheses is
\tau
, we can define the witness:

\sigma:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}(\sigma_{i}\cup[a_{i}\mapsto A_{i},b_{i}\mapsto B_{i}]\cup\bigcup_{x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})}(\sigma_{(i,x)}\cup[b_{(i,x)}\mapsto B_{x}]))\cup[a\mapsto A]

This satisfies
A=a\sigma
and
C\vdash C^{\prime}\sigma
(where
C^{\prime}
is the generated constraints on line 7/8 of the extract) as required.

∎

Appendix E
Additional Material in Support of SubSection
6.4

In this appendix, we give the definition of reduction for the programming language of the constrained system and we give the definition of consistency of a constraint set. We then establish a number of useful lemmas regarding the type system:

•

left and right inversion lemmas

•

typed substitution lemmas

This enables us to prove progress and preservation, and finally conclude syntactic soundness.

Definition E.1 (Reduction).

The
evaluation contexts
are defined by the following grammar:

\mathcal{E}\Coloneqq\square\mid c(\vv{V},\mathcal{E},\vv{M})\mid\mathcal{E}\,N\mid(\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M)\,\mathcal{E}\mid\mathsf{match}\,\mathcal{E}\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}(p_{i}\mapsto P_{i})\}

Fix a module
\mathcal{M}
, then the one-step reduction relation wrt
\mathcal{M}
, written
M\,\ \mathord{\rhd}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{M}}\ \,N
, is the contextual closure of the following axiom schema:

\begin{array}[]{rrcl}\textsf{\footnotesize(Delta)}&\mathsf{f}&\,\ \mathord{\rhd}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{M}}&\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{f})\\[5.69054pt]
\textsf{\footnotesize(Fix$\beta$)}&(\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M)\,V&\,\ \mathord{\rhd}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{M}}&M[V/x,\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M/f]\\[5.69054pt]
\textsf{\footnotesize(Match)}&\mathsf{match}\,c(\vv{V})\,\mathsf{with}\,\{|_{d\in I}(d(\vv{x_{d}})\mapsto P_{d})\}&\,\ \mathord{\rhd}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{M}}&P_{c}[\vv{V/x_{c}}]\qquad\text{(when $c\in I$)}\end{array}

We will more often simply write
M\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ N
whenever the context is unimportant or otherwise understood, and the reflexive-transitive closure by
M\,\ \mathord{\rhd}^{\!*}\ \,N
.

Definition E.2.

A constraint set
C
is said to be
closed
just if the following conditions are met:

(1)

If
A\sqsubseteq A^{\prime}\in C
and
A^{\prime}\sqsubseteq B\in C
then
A\sqsubseteq B\in C
.

(2)

If
K\sqsubseteq K^{\prime}\in C
,
c(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})\in K
and
c(B_{1},\ldots,B_{n})\in K^{\prime}
, then
A_{i}\sqsubseteq B_{i}\in C
for each
i\in[1..n]
.

(3)

If
A\to B\sqsubseteq A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}\in C
then
A^{\prime}\sqsubseteq A\in C
and
B\sqsubseteq B^{\prime}\in C
.

(4)

If
A\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B\sqsubseteq A^{\prime}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B^{\prime}\in C
then
A\sqsubseteq A^{\prime}\in C
and
B^{\prime}\sqsubseteq B\in C
.

Definition E.3.

We say that a constraint
A\sqsubseteq B
is said to be
syntactically consistent
just if either:

•

At least one of
A
or
B
is a type variable

•

or,
A
and
B
are both sufficiency arrows or are both necessity arrows

•

or,
B
is
\mathsf{Ok}

•

or,
A
is of shape
\Sigma_{c\in I}c(\vv{A})
,
B
is of shape
\Sigma_{d\in J}d(\vv{B})
and
I\subseteq J

We say that a constraint set
C
is
consistent
just if it is closed and contains only syntactically consistent constraints. We say that a type environment
\Gamma
is
consistent
just if
\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)
is consistent.

Definition E.4.

A judgement of the constrained type system is said to be a
single-subject (1S)
just if it either has shape:

(1)

\mathcal{C}\mid\Gamma,\,M:A\vdash\Delta
,
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are consistent variable environments with disjoint subjects

(2)

or
\mathcal{C}\mid\Gamma\vdash M:A
, with
\Gamma
a consistent variable environment

Note, a consequence of the 1S definition is that a 1S judgment has at most non-variable subject – here we will consider top-level identifiers also as (global) variables, which is the
M:A
above. If a 1S judgment has a non-variable subject then it is either of type (I) or type (II), but cannot be both. Otherwise, if a 1S judgement has two formulas on the left-hand side with the same variable subject, then it must be of type (I), with
M
being one of the two. Otherwise, a 1S judgement with all distinct variable subjects on the left-hand side can be seen as either type (I) or type (II). 1S variable judgments are important because well-typing and ill-typing are both 1S judgments, and a proof of a 1S judgment only involves 1S judgments.

Lemma E.5.

A proof tree
\mathcal{T}
of a 1S conclusion contains only 1S judgments at each node.

Proof.

By induction on the height of
\mathcal{T}
. When the tree is of height 0, i.e. it is only a
(GVar)
,
(Id)
,
(CnsDL)
,
(FixDL)
or
(VarK)
and the result follows by assumption. Otherwise, suppose the tree has height
k
and we argue by case analysis on the root:

•

If the root is concluded by
(SubL)
or
(SubR)
, then clearly the judgement in the premise is also 1S and the result follows from the induction hypothesis.

•

If the root is concluded by
(AbsR)
or
(AbnR)
, then the 1S conclusion has shape
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M:A
. Thus
\Gamma
is a consistent variable environment, and we assume by the side condition that the bound variables
f
and
x
do not occur in
\Gamma
. Thus the premises can be seen as 1S type (II) and type (I) respectively, and the result follows from the induction hypothesis.

•

If the root is concluded by
(AppL)
or
(AppR)
, then the 1S conclusion has shape
\Gamma,\,M\,N:A\vdash\Delta
or
\Gamma\vdash M\,N:A
respectively. In the former case,
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are consistent and disjoint variable environments, and therefore
\Gamma\vdash M:B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
is of type (II) and
\Gamma,\,N:B\vdash\Delta
is of type (I). In the latter case,
\Gamma
is a consistent variable environment and hence
\Gamma\vdash M:B\to A
and
\Gamma\vdash N:B
are both of type (II). In both cases, the result follows from the induction hypothesis.

•

If the root is concluded by
(CnsL)
, then the 1S conclusion has shape
\Gamma,\,C(M_{1},\ldots,M_{m}):C(A_{1},\ldots,A_{m})\vdash\Delta
and therefore
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are disjoint, consistent variable environments. Hence,
\Gamma,\,M_{i}:A_{i}\vdash\Delta
is a 1S judgment of type (I) and the result follows from the induction hypothesis.

•

If the root is concluded by
(CnsR)
, then the 1S conclusion has shape
\Gamma\vdash c(M_{1},\ldots,M_{m}):c(A_{1},\ldots,A_{m})
and
\Gamma
is a consistent variable environment. Then
\Gamma\vdash M_{i}:A_{i}
is of type (II) for each
i
and the result follows from the induction hypothesis.

•

If the root is concluded by
(MchL)
, then the 1S conclusion is of shape
\Gamma,\,\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid^{k}_{i=1}(p_{i}\mapsto P_{i})\}:A\vdash\Delta
and so
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are consistent, disjoint variable environments. We may assume by the implicit side condition that the variables bound in the patterns are fresh for the context. Hence, it follows immediately that each
\Gamma,\,P_{i}:A\vdash x:B_{x}
is type (I). Similarly,
\Gamma,\,(M,P_{i}):(p_{i}[B_{x}/x\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})],A)\vdash\Delta
is type (I). Hence, the result follows from the induction hypothesis.

•

If the root is concluded by
(MchR)
, then the 1S conclusion is of shape
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{match}\,M\,\mathsf{with}\,\{|_{i=1}^{k}(p_{i}\mapsto P_{i})\}:A
and
\Gamma
is a consistent variable environment. Moreover, we may assume by the implicit side condition that the variables bound by the patterns are fresh for the context. Therefore, both of the premises are type (II) and the result follows from the induction hypothesis.

•

If the root is concluded by
(CnsK)
, then the 1S conclusion is of shape
\Gamma,\,c(M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}):\mathsf{Ok}\vdash\Delta
and
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are disjoint, consistent variable environments. Therefore, the premise is type (I) and the result follows from the induction hypothesis.

•

If the root is concluded by
(FunK)
, then the 1S conclusion has shape
\Gamma,\,M\,N:\mathsf{Ok}\vdash\Delta
and
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are disjoint, consistent variable environments. Therefore, the premise is type (I) and the result follows from the induction hypothesis.

∎

From now on we will consider only 1S judgements.

Lemma E.6 (Left Inversion).

Suppose
\Gamma,\,M:A\vdash\Delta
has
\Gamma
and
\Delta
consistent, disjoint variable environments. Then either (a)
\Delta
is of shape
x:\mathsf{Ok}
, or (b) one of the following is true:

•

M
is of shape
x
and there is some
B
such that
x:B=\Delta
and
\Gamma\vdash A\sqsubseteq B
.

•

M
is of shape
f
and there is some type
B
such that
x:B=\Delta
and
\Gamma\vdash A\sqsubseteq B

•

M
is of shape
PQ
and either:

(1)

there is a type
B
such that
\Gamma\vdash M:B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
and
\Gamma,\,N:B\vdash\Delta

(2)

or,
\Gamma,\,M:\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A\vdash\Delta
.

•

M
is of shape
c(M_{1},\ldots,M_{m})
and either:

(1)

there are types
B_{1},\ldots,B_{m}
and
i
such that
A\sqsubseteq c(B_{1},\ldots,B_{m})
and
\Gamma,\,M_{i}:B_{i}\vdash\Delta
.

(2)

or,
\Gamma,\,M_{i}:\mathsf{Ok}\vdash\Delta
for some
i

(3)

or, there is some type
A^{\prime}
which is either an arrow or sum type not including a
c
-headed case, such that
\Gamma\vdash A\sqsubseteq A^{\prime}
and
\Gamma,\,c(M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}):A^{\prime}\vdash\Delta

•

M
is of shape
\mathsf{match}\,Q\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}(p_{i}\mapsto P_{i})\}
and there is a family of types
B_{x}
(one for each pattern-bound variable
x
) such that
\Gamma,\,P_{i}:A\vdash x:B_{x}
(for each
i
and each
x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})
), and either
\Gamma,\,Q:p_{i}[B_{x}/x\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})]\vdash\Delta
or
\Gamma,\,P_{i}:A\vdash\Delta
.

•

M
is of shape
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,P
and there is some sum type
A^{\prime}
such that
\Gamma\vdash A\sqsubseteq A^{\prime}
and
\Gamma,\,\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,P:A^{\prime}\vdash\Delta

Proof.

The proof is by cases on the shape of
M
.

•

If
M
is a variable
x
then, according to the assumptions, all the formulas have variables as subjects.
By the completeness of algorithmic type assignment, we have
\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma),\,x:A\mid\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash_{2}\Delta
. Given that
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are disjoint, the only rules that can have concluded such a judgement are
(VarK2)
,
(LVar2)
. The former gives rise to (a). In case
(LVar2)
, since
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are disjoint, it must be that there is some
B
such that
x:B\in\Delta
and
\Gamma\vdash A\sqsubseteq B
, as required by (c).

•

If
M
is a top-level identifier, then the reasoning is as in the former case, since
A
is a monotype.

•

If
M
is an application
PQ
, then we reason as follows. By the completeness of algorithmic type assignment,
\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma),\,P\,Q:A\mid\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash_{2}\Delta
and the only rules that could have concluded this judgement are
(VarK2)
,
(AppL2)
or
(FunK2)
. The former case gives rise to (a). In case
(AppL2)
, it follows that there are types
B_{1}
and
B
such that
\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash B_{1}\sqsubseteq B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
,
\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma)\mid\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash_{2}M:B_{1}
and
\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma),\,N:B\mid\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma)\vdash_{2}\Delta
. It follows from the soundness of algorithmic type assignment that
\Gamma\vdash M:B_{1}
and hence, by
(SubR)
,
\Gamma\vdash M:B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
. Similarly,
\Gamma,\,N:B\vdash\Delta
. This is what is required by (i). In the latter case it follows that there is a type
B
such that
\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash_{2}\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A\sqsubseteq B
and
\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma),\,M:B\mid\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash_{2}\Delta
. It follows from the soundness of algorithmic type assignment that
\Gamma,\,M:B\vdash\Delta
and hence, by
(SubR)
,
\Gamma,\,M:\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A\vdash\Delta
, as required by (ii).

•

If
M
is a constructor term
c(M_{1},\ldots,M_{n})
, then we reason as follows. By the completeness of algorithmic type assignment,
\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma),\,c(M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}):A\mid\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash_{2}\Delta
. Since
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are disjoint, the only rules that can conclude such a judgement are
(VarK2)
,
(ConsL2)
,
(CnsK2)
and
(CnsDL)
. The first possibility give rise to (a). The second implies that there are types
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}
and
\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash A\sqsubseteq c(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})
and
\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma),\,M_{i}:A_{i}\mid\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash_{2}\Delta
for some
i
. By the soundness of algorithmic type assignment,
\Gamma,\,M_{i}:A_{i}\vdash\Delta
, as required by (i). In the third, we have for some
i
,
\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma),\,M_{i}:\mathsf{Ok}\mid\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash_{2}\Delta
. Then (ii) follows by the soundness of algorithmic type assignment. Finally, when the conclusion is by
(CnsDL)
, there is some type
A^{\prime}
which is either an arrow or a sum not including
c
, such that
\Gamma\vdash A\sqsubseteq A^{\prime}
and
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime}\vdash\Delta
.

•

If
M
is a match expression
\mathsf{match}\,Q\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}(p_{i}\mapsto P_{i})\}
then we reason as follows. It follows from the completeness of algorithmic type assignment that
\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma),\,\mathsf{match}\,Q\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}(p_{i}\mapsto P_{i})\}:A\mid\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash_{2}\Delta
. Given the disjointness of the environments, the only rules that could conclude this judgement are
(VarK2)
and
(MchL2)
. In the former case we obtain (a), so let us assume that
\Delta
is not of this shape. In the latter case, we have that there are families of types
B_{i}
,
A_{i}
,
A_{i}^{\prime}
,
A_{x}
and
B_{x}
, such that (1)
\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma),\,(Q,P_{i}):A_{i}^{\prime}\mid\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash_{2}\Delta
for all
i
, (2)
\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma),\,P_{i}:A_{x}\mid\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash_{2}x:B_{x}
for all
i
and all
x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})
,
\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash A\sqsubseteq A_{i}
for all
i
,
\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash A\sqsubseteq A_{x}
for all
i
and all
x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})
,
\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash p_{i}[B_{x}/x\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})]\sqsubseteq B_{i}
for all
i
, and
\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash(B_{i},A)\sqsubseteq A_{i}^{\prime}
for all
i
. It follows from the soundness of algorithmic typing that
\Gamma,\,(Q,P_{i}):A_{i}^{\prime}\vdash\Delta
for all
i
, and
\Gamma,\,P_{i}:A_{x}\vdash x:B_{x}
for all
i
and all
x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})
. Then it follows from the previous case that either (1)
A_{i}^{\prime}=\mathsf{Ok}
and either
\Gamma,\,Q:\mathsf{Ok}\vdash\Delta
or
\Gamma,\,P_{i}:\mathsf{Ok}\vdash\Delta
, or (2)
A_{i}^{\prime}
is an arrow type or (3) there are types
B^{\prime\prime}_{1}
and
B^{\prime\prime}_{2}
such that
\Gamma\vdash A_{i}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq(B^{\prime\prime}_{1},B^{\prime\prime}_{2})
and
\Gamma,\,Q:B^{\prime\prime}_{1}\vdash\Delta
or
\Gamma,\,P_{i}:B^{\prime\prime}_{2}\vdash\Delta
. In case (1), it follows from
(SubL)
that either
\Gamma,\,Q:p_{i}[B_{x}/x\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})]\vdash\Delta
or
\Gamma,\,P_{i}:A\vdash\Delta
, as required. Case (2) is impossible by the consistency of
\Gamma
. In case (3), we have
\Gamma\vdash(p_{i}[B_{x}/x\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})],A)\sqsubseteq(B_{i},A)\sqsubseteq A_{i}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq(B^{\prime\prime}_{1},B^{\prime\prime}_{2})
and thus, by the closedness of
\Gamma
, we have
\Gamma\vdash p_{i}[B_{x}/x\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})]\sqsubseteq B^{\prime\prime}_{1}
and
\Gamma\vdash A\sqsubseteq B^{\prime\prime}_{2}
. Then it follows from
(SubL)
that either
\Gamma,\,Q:p_{i}[B_{x}/x\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})]\vdash\Delta
or
\Gamma,\,P_{i}:A\vdash\Delta

•

When
M
is a fixpoint abstraction
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,P
, it follows from the completeness of algorithmic type assignment that
\Gamma,\,\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,P:A\vdash_{2}\Delta
and then the only applicable rules are
(VarK2)
and
(FixDL2)
. The former case gives rise to (a). In the latter case, it is immediate that there is some sum type
A^{\prime}
such that
\Gamma\vdash A\sqsubseteq A^{\prime}
and
\Gamma,\,M:A^{\prime}\vdash\Delta
.

∎

Lemma E.7 (Right Inversion).

Suppose
\Gamma\vdash M:A
and
\Gamma
a consistent variable environment. Then one of the following is true:

•

M
is of shape
x
and
A=\mathsf{Ok}
or there is some
B
such that
x:B\in\Gamma
and
\Gamma\vdash B\sqsubseteq A
.

•

M
is of shape
f
and there are types
B
,
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
and
B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}
, and constraints
D
such that
f:\forall\vv{a}.\,D\Rightarrow B\in\Gamma
and
\Gamma\vdash B[\vv{B}/\vv{a}]\sqsubseteq A

•

M
is of shape
PQ
and there is a type
B
such that
\Gamma\vdash P:B\to A
and
\Gamma\vdash N:B

•

M
is of shape
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,P
and there are types
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
such that either:

(1)

\Gamma,\,f:B_{1}\to B_{2},\,x:B_{1}\vdash M:B_{2}
and
\Gamma\vdash B_{1}\to B_{2}\sqsubseteq A

(2)

or,
\Gamma,\,f:B_{1}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B_{2},\,M:B_{2}\vdash x:B_{1}
and
\Gamma\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B_{2}\sqsubseteq A

•

M
is of shape
c(M_{1},\ldots,M_{n})
and there are types
B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}
such that
\Gamma\vdash M_{i}:B_{i}
for each
i
, and
\Gamma\vdash c(B_{1},\ldots,B_{n})\sqsubseteq A
.

•

M
is of shape
\mathsf{match}\,Q\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}(p_{i}\mapsto P_{i})\}
and there is a family of types
B_{x}
(for each
i
and each
x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})
) such that
\Gamma\vdash Q:\Sigma_{i=1}^{k}\,p_{i}[B_{x}/x\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})]
and
\Gamma\cup\{x:B_{x}\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})\}\vdash P_{i}:A
(for each
i
).

Proof.

By cases on the shape of
M
.

•

When
M
is a local variable
x
we reason as follows. By the completeness of algorithmic type assignment, we have
\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma)\mid\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash_{2}x:A
. Given that
\Gamma
is consistent, the only rules that can conclude the judgement are
(Var2)
and
(VarK2)
. In the latter case,
A=\mathsf{Ok}
. In the former case, there is some
B
such that
x:B\in\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma)
and
\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash B\sqsubseteq A
.

•

When
M
is an identifier
f
, by the completeness of type assignment we have that
\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma)\mid\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash_{2}f:A
. The only applicable rule was
(Inst2)
and hence there are type variables
\vv{a}
and types
\vv{B}
and
B
, and constraints
D
such that
f:\forall\vv{a}.\,D\Rightarrow B\in\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma)
and
\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash D[\vv{B}/\vv{a}]
and
\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash B[\vv{B}/\vv{a}]\sqsubseteq A
.

•

When
M
is an application
PQ
, by the completeness of type assignment, we have that
\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma)\mid\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash_{2}PQ:A
. The only applicable rule is
(AppR2)
and so there are types
B_{1}
and
B
such that
\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma)\mid\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash_{2}P:B_{1}
,
\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma)\mid\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash_{2}Q:B
and
\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash B_{1}\sqsubseteq B\to A
. It follows from the soundness of algorithmic type assignment that
\Gamma\vdash P:B_{1}
and
\Gamma\vdash Q:B
and by
(()
SubR) also
\Gamma\vdash P:B\to A
, as required.

•

When
M
is a fixpoint abstraction
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,P
, by the completeness of algorithmic type assignment, we have that
\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma)\mid\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash_{2}\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,P:A
. Only the rules
(FixsR2)
and
(FixnR2)
could have concluded. In the former case, we have that there are types
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
such that
\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma),\,f:B_{1}\to B_{2},\,x:B_{1}\mid\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash M:B_{2}
and
\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash B_{1}\to B_{2}\sqsubseteq A
, as required by (i). The latter case is analogous.

•

When
M
is a constructor term
c(M_{1},\ldots,M_{n})
, by the completeness of algorithmic type assignment, we have that
\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma)\mid\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash_{2}c(M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}):A
. The only rule that could conclude this is
(ConsR2)
, and hence there must be types
B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}
such that
\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma)\mid\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash_{2}M_{i}:B_{i}
for each
i
and
\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash c(B_{1},\ldots,B_{n})\sqsubseteq A
. It follows from the soundness of algorithmic type assignment that
\Gamma\vdash M_{i}:B_{i}
for each
i
, as required.

•

When
M
is of shape
\mathsf{match}\,Q\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}(p_{i}\mapsto P_{i})\}
, it follows from the completeness of algorithmic type assignment that
\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma)\mid\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash\mathsf{match}\,Q\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}(p_{i}\mapsto P_{i})\}:A
. The only applicable rule is
(MchR)
and so there is a type
B
and families of types
A_{i}
(for each
i
) and
B_{x}
(for each
i
and each
x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})
) such that
\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma)\mid\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash_{2}Q:B
and
\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma)\cup\{x:B_{x}\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})\}\mid\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash_{2}P_{i}:A_{i}
(for each
i
) and
\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash B\sqsubseteq\Sigma_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}[B_{x}/x\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})]
and
\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash A_{i}\sqsubseteq A
(for all
i
). It follows from the soundness of algorithmic type assignment that
\Gamma\vdash Q:B
and, by
(SubR)
,
\Gamma\vdash\Sigma_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}[B_{x}/x\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})]
. Similarly,
\Gamma\cup\{x:B_{x}\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})\}\vdash P_{i}:A_{i}
and, by
(SubR)
,
\Gamma\cup\{x:B_{x}\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})\}\vdash P_{i}:A
.

•

When
M
is of shape
\mathsf{fix}\,x.\,P
, it follows from the completeness of algorithmic type assignment that
\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma)\mid\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash_{2}\mathsf{fix}\,x.\,P:A
. The only rule that could conclude this judgement is
(FixR2)
and so there is some type
B
such that
\mathsf{Typ}(\Gamma),\,x:A\mid\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash P:B
and
\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)\vdash B\sqsubseteq A
. Hence, it follows from soundness of algorithmic type assignment and
(SubR)
that
\Gamma,\,x:A\vdash M:A
, as required.

∎

Lemma E.8.

If
C\vdash A_{0}\sqsubseteq A_{n}
then there is a sequence of constraints:

A_{1}\sqsubseteq A_{2},A_{3}\sqsubseteq A_{4},\ldots{}A_{n-2}\sqsubseteq A_{n-1}

all of which are in
C
and such that:

(1)

for all
i\in[0..n/2]
either
A_{2i+1}
is
\mathsf{Ok}
or otherwise:

(a)

A_{2i}
is a type variable and
A_{i}=A_{2i+1}

(b)

or,
A_{2i}
is a sufficiency arrow and so is
A_{2i+1}

(c)

or,
A_{2i}
is a necessity arrow and so is
A_{2i+1}

(d)

or,
A_{2i}
is of shape
\Sigma_{c\in I}\,c(\vv{A})
and
A_{2i+1}
is of shape
\Sigma_{d\in J}\,d(\vv{A})
and
I\subseteq J
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the derivation of
C\vdash A\sqsubseteq B
.

(IdS)
:

Clearly this satisfies the conclusion with a sequence of length 1.

(TrS)
:

It follows from the induction hypotheses that there are sequences

A_{1}\sqsubseteq A_{2},A_{3}\sqsubseteq A_{4},\ldots{}A_{n-2}\sqsubseteq A_{n-1}\qquad\text{and}\qquad B_{1}\sqsubseteq B_{2},B_{3}\sqsubseteq B_{4},\ldots{}B_{m-2}\sqsubseteq B_{m-1}

satisfying the appropriate properties. The middle witness is
A_{n}=B_{0}
. If it is a type variable, then
A_{n-1}=A_{n}=B_{0}=B_{1}
. If it is an arrow, then
A_{n-1}
and
B_{1}
are arrows of the same kind too. If it is a sum
\Sigma_{d\in J}\,d(\ldots)
, then
A_{n-1}
is a sum
\Sigma_{c\in I}\,c(\ldots)
with
I\subseteq J
and
B_{1}
is a sum
\Sigma_{k\in K}\,k(\ldots)
with
J\subseteq K
. If it is
\mathsf{Ok}
, then there is no requirement on
A_{n-1}
but
B_{1}
must be
\mathsf{Ok}
. Hence, in each case,
A_{n-1}
and
B_{1}
satisfy the required relationship and thus the witness is just the concatenation of the sequences.

(ToS)
,
(FrS)
,
(SmS)
,
(CnS)
,
(OkS)
:

The conclusion is obtained using a sequence of length 0 because
A_{0}
and
A_{1}
already satisfy the required relationship.

∎

Consistent constraint sets do not derive inconsistencies.

Lemma E.9.

Suppose
C
is syntactically consistent.

•

If
C\vdash A\sqsubseteq B
then
A\sqsubseteq B
is syntactically consistent.

•

If
C\vdash A\mathrel{||}B
then
A\mathrel{||}B
is syntactically consistent.

Proof.

•

If
C\vdash A\sqsubseteq B
, then by the forgoing lemma there is a sequence of inequalities in
C
satisfying the required conditions:

A_{1}\sqsubseteq A_{2},A_{3}\sqsubseteq A_{4},\ldots{}A_{n-2}\sqsubseteq A_{n-1}

Suppose for the purpose of obtaining a contradiction that
A\sqsubseteq B
is not syntactically consistent. Then there is a consecutive subsequence of the above, of shape:

B_{i}\sqsubseteq a_{1},a_{1}\sqsubseteq a_{2},a_{2}\sqsubseteq a_{3}\ldots,a_{k}\sqsubseteq B_{2k+1}

(i.e. intermediate types are all type variables) of constraints in
C
in which
B_{i}\sqsubseteq B_{2k+1}
is already not syntactically consistent. However, it follows that
B_{i}\sqsubseteq B_{2k+1}
is contained in the closure of
C
, which is assumed consistent.

•

The proof is by induction on the derivation of
C\vdash A\mathrel{||}B
. In cases
(ConD)
,
(ToD)
and
(FromD)
the result is immediate. In case
(RfId)
the result follows from the assumption. In case
(SymD)
the result follows from the induction hypothesis since the characterisation of syntactic consistency is symmetrical. Finally, in case
(SubD)
we can extend Lemma
E.8
to show that there are two sequences of subtype constraints in
C
each of which gives rise to one side of the disjointness constraint. Then it follows similarly, that if a disjointness constraint is concluded that is not syntactically consistent, then already there would be a non-syntactically consistent constraint in the closure.

∎

Lemma E.10.

Suppose
\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)
is closed and syntactically consistent. If
\Gamma\vdash V:A
with
V
a closed value and
A
not a type variable, then either:

•

A
is a sum type containing a type of shape
c(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})
and
V
a constructor term of shape
c(W_{1},\ldots,W_{n})
and
\Gamma\vdash W_{i}:A_{i}
for each
i
,

•

or,
A
is an arrow and
V
is a fixpoint abstraction

Proof.

We proceed by cases on the shape of
V
.

•

If
V
is of shape
c(W_{1},\ldots,W_{n})
then, by inversion, there are types
B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}
such that
\Gamma\vdash M_{i}:B_{i}
for each
i
, and
\Gamma\vdash c(B_{1},\ldots,B_{n})\sqsubseteq A
. Therefore, it follows from consistency of subtyping (Lemma
E.9
) that either
A
is a sum type including a
c
-headed type or
A
is a type variable.

•

If
V
is of shape
\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M
then, by inversion, there are types
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
such that either
\Gamma\vdash B_{1}\to B_{2}\sqsubseteq A
or
\Gamma\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B_{2}\sqsubseteq A
.
By consistency of subtyping, in both cases,
A
is either a type variable or an arrow.

∎

E.0.1.
Proof of Theorem
6.10
(Progress)

Proof.

The first is by induction on the derivation. Due to the form of the judgement, every subject in
\Gamma
can make a step, so we focus on the principal formula in each derivation step and exclude cases that cannot occur.

(GVar)
:

in this case
M
is a top-level identifier, so
M
can make a step.

(SubR)
:

It follows from the induction hypothesis that
M
can make a step, or
M
is a value.

(FixsR)
:

It is immediate that
M
is a value.

(FixnR)
:

It is immediate that
M
is a value.

(AppR)
:

In this case,
M
has shape
PQ
and it follows from the induction hypothesis that either
P
can make a step or is a value and
Q
can make a step or is a value. If
P
can make a step, then
M
can make a step, so assume to the contrary. Then
P
is a value. It follows that from Lemma
E.10
that
P
must be an abstraction. Now, if
Q
can make a step, then
M
can make a step and if, not, then it is a value and
M
can make a step by contracting the redex.

(CnsR)
:

In this case,
M
is of shape
c(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n})
and it follows from the induction hypothesis that, for each
i
,
P_{i}
can either make a step or is a value. From any configuration it follows that
M
can either make a step or is a value.

(MchR)
:

In this case,
M
is of shape
\mathsf{match}\,Q\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid^{k}_{i=1}(p_{i}\mapsto P_{i})\}
. It follows from the induction hypothesis that
Q
can either make a step or is a value. If
Q
can make a step, then
M
can make a step. Otherwise,
Q
is a value of a sum type and it follows from Lemma
E.10
that
Q
must be a constructor term
c(W_{1},\ldots,W_{n})
and there is a
c
-headed type in the sum. Hence, there is a pattern
p_{i}
of shape
c(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
and so
M
can make a step.

The second is also by induction on the derivation. Similar remarks apply. Note that, if
M
is stuck, then it follows from the previous result that
\Gamma\not\vdash M:A
for any
A
.

(SubL)
:

In this case we assume
\Gamma\vdash A\sqsubseteq B
. It follows from the induction hypothesis that either
M
makes a step or
\Gamma\not\vdash M:B
. In the former case the result is immediate, in the latter case, we must have
\Gamma\not\vdash M:A
too by the contrapositive of
(SubR)
.

(AppL)
:

In this case,
M
is of shape
PQ
. Since
M
is not a value, either
M
can make a step or
M
is stuck and hence the desired conclusion follows in both cases.

(CnsL)
:

In this case,
M
is of shape
c(M_{1},\ldots,M_{n})
and
A
is of shape
c(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})
. It follows from the induction hypothesis that either
M_{i}
can make a step or
\Gamma\not\vdash M_{i}:A_{i}
. In the former case, either
M
can make a step, or
M
is stuck. In either case, the result follows. If
\Gamma\not\vdash M_{i}:A_{i}
then we have that
\Gamma\not\vdash M:A
. To see this, suppose the contrary. Then it follows from inversion that there are types
B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}
such that
\Gamma\vdash M_{i}:B_{i}
and
\Gamma\vdash c(B_{1},\ldots,B_{n})\sqsubseteq c(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})
. Since
\mathsf{Con}(\Gamma)
is closed, it follows that
\Gamma\vdash B_{i}\sqsubseteq A_{i}
and so
\Gamma\vdash M_{i}:A_{i}
follows by
(SubR)
, which contradicts our supposition.

(MchL)
:

In this case,
M
is of shape
\mathsf{match}\,Q\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid^{k}_{i=1}(p_{i}\mapsto P_{i})\}
. Since
M
is not a value, either
M
can make a step or is stuck. The desired conclusion follows in both cases.

(CnsK)
:

In this case,
M
is of shape
c(M_{1},\ldots,M_{n})
and
A
is
\mathsf{Ok}
. It follows from the induction hypothesis that there is some
M_{i}
that can either make a step or for which
\Gamma\not\vdash M_{i}:\mathsf{Ok}
. In the former case, either
M
can make a step or
M
is stuck and the result follows. In the latter case, since all values are well-typed, it follows that
M_{i}
is stuck and so the result follows.

(FunK)
:

In this case,
M
is of shape
M\,N
and
A
is
\mathsf{Ok}
. Since
M
is not a value, it can either make a step or is stuck and in both cases the desired conclusion follows.

(CnsDL)
,
(FixDL)
:

In these cases, suppose
\Gamma\vdash M:A
, and then we obtain a contradiction from inversion and subtype consistency.

∎

Lemma E.11 (Substitution on the right).

Assume
\Gamma
is a consistent variable environment, disjoint from
\Delta
and that does not contain
x
.

(1)

If
\Gamma,\,x:B\vdash M:A
and
\Gamma\vdash N:B
, then
\Gamma\vdash M[N/x]:A
.

(2)

If
\Gamma,\,x:B,\,M:A\vdash\Delta
and
\Gamma\vdash N:B
, then
\Gamma,\,M[N/x]:A\vdash\Delta
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
M
. Note that, in (ii), it is always possible that
\Delta
is of shape
z:\mathsf{Ok}
for some variable
z
. Then the conclusion follows immediately by
(VarK)
, so we exclude this from the case analysis below.

•

When
M
is
x
, we reason as follows. In (i), it follows by inversion that
\Gamma\vdash B\sqsubseteq A
. Since
M[N/x]=N
, we have
\Gamma\vdash M[N/x]:B
and by
(SubR)
, therefore
\Gamma\vdash M[N/x]:A
. In (ii) it follows from inversion that
\Delta
must contain
y:\mathsf{Ok}
for some variable
y
, which we have dealt with above.

•

When
M
is some variable
y
distinct from
x
, we reason as follows. In (i), it follows from the assumptions and inversion that
y:A^{\prime}\in\Gamma
with
\Gamma\vdash A^{\prime}\sqsubseteq A
. Since
M[N/x]=M=y
, we have by
(LVar)
that
\Gamma\vdash M[N/x]:A^{\prime}
and the conclusion follows from
(SubR)
. In (ii),
\Delta
is
y:A^{\prime}
with
A\sqsubseteq A^{\prime}
. Then the conclusion follows from
(LVar)
and
(SubL)
.

•

When
M
is an application
P\,Q
, we reason as follows. In (i), by inversion we have that there is some
B^{\prime}
such that
\Gamma,\,x:B\vdash P:B^{\prime}\to A
and
\Gamma,\,x:B\vdash Q:B^{\prime}
. It follows from the induction hypotheses that
\Gamma\vdash P[N/x]:B^{\prime}\to A
and
\Gamma\vdash Q[N/x]:B^{\prime}
and so the conclusion follows from
(AppR)
. In (ii), inversion gives us that either: (1) there is a type
B^{\prime}
and
\Gamma,\,x:B\vdash P:B^{\prime}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
and
\Gamma,\,x:B,\,Q:B^{\prime}\vdash\Delta
, or (2)
\Gamma,\,x:B,\,P:\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A\vdash\Delta
. Suppose the former, then it follows from the induction hypothesis part (i) that
\Gamma\vdash P[N/x]:B^{\prime}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
and from part (ii) that
\Gamma,\,Q[N/x]:B^{\prime}\vdash\Delta
. Hence the conclusion follows from
(AppL)
. Suppose the latter, then it follows from the induction hypothesis part (ii) that
\Gamma,\,P[N/x]:\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
, and the conclusion follows from
(FunK)
.

•

When
M
is a fixpoint abstraction
\mathsf{fix}\,f(y).\,P
, we may assume by the variable convention that
y
and
f
do not occur outside of
P
. In (i), by inversion we have that there are types
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
such that either (1)
\Gamma,\,x:B,\,f:B_{1}\to B_{2},\,y:B_{1}\vdash P:B_{2}
and
\Gamma\vdash B_{1}\to B_{2}\sqsubseteq A
or (2)
\Gamma,\,x:B,\,f:B_{1}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B_{2},\,P:B_{2}\vdash y:B_{1}
and
\Gamma\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B_{2}\sqsubseteq A
. In case (1), it follows from the induction hypothesis part (i) that
\Gamma,\,y:B_{1}\vdash P[N/x]:B_{2}
and thus the conclusion follows from
(FixsR)
and
(SubR)
. In case (2) it follows from the induction hypothesis part (ii) that
\Gamma,\,P[N/x]:B_{2}\vdash y:B_{1}
and hence the conclusion follows from
(FixnR)
and
(SubR)
. In (ii), by inversion we have that there is some arrow type or sum type
A^{\prime}
and
\Gamma\vdash A\sqsubseteq A^{\prime}
and
\Gamma,\,x:B,\,\mathsf{fix}\,f(y).\,P:A^{\prime}\vdash\Delta
. In either case, the conclusion follows from
(FixDL)
and
(SubL)
.

•

When
M
is a constructor term
c(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n})
, we reason as follows. In (i) it follows from inversion that there are types
B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}
and
\Gamma,\,x:B\vdash P_{i}:B_{i}
for each
i
and
\Gamma\vdash c(B_{1},\ldots,B_{n})\sqsubseteq A
. It follows from the induction hypothesis that
\Gamma\vdash c(P_{1}[N/x],\ldots,P_{n}[N/x]):c(B_{1},\ldots,B_{n})
and then the conclusion follows from
(SubR)
. In (ii), it follows from inversion that either (1) there are types
B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}
and
i
such that
\Gamma\vdash A\sqsubseteq c(B_{1},\ldots,B_{n})
and
\Gamma,\,x:B,\,P_{i}:B_{i}\vdash\Delta
, or (2) there is an arrow type
A^{\prime}
such that
\Gamma\vdash A\sqsubseteq A^{\prime}
and
\Gamma,\,x:B,\,c(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}):A^{\prime}\vdash\Delta
. In the former case, the conclusion follows from the induction hypothesis and
(SubL)
, in the latter it follows from
(CnsDL)
and
(SubL)
.

•

When
M
is a match expression
\mathsf{match}\,Q\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}(p_{i}\mapsto P_{i})\}
, we reason as follows. In (i), by inversion we have a family of types
B_{x}
(for each
i
and
x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})
) such that
\Gamma,\,x:B\vdash Q:\Sigma_{i=1}^{k}\,p_{i}[B_{x}/x\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})]
and
\Gamma,\,x:B\cup\{x:B_{x}\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})\}\vdash P_{i}:A
for each
i
. The conclusion follows from the induction hypothesis, part (i). In (ii), by inversion we have a family of types
B_{y}
(for each pattern-bound variable
y
) such that
\Gamma,\,x:B,\,P_{i}:A\vdash y:B_{y}
and either
\Gamma,\,Q:p_{i}[B_{y}/y\mid y\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})]\vdash\Delta
or
\Gamma,\,P_{i}:A\vdash\Delta
. In both cases, it follows from the induction hypothesis and
(CnsL)
that
\Gamma,\,(Q[N/x],P_{i}[N/x]):(p_{i}[B_{y}/y\mid y\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})],A)\vdash\Delta
. Then we can obtain the conclusion by applying the induction hypothesis part (ii) to each of the former judgements and concluding by
(MchL)
.

∎

Lemma E.12 (Trivial Substitution).

Assume
\Gamma
is a consistent variable environment disjoint from
\Delta
and neither contain
x
. Assume
\Gamma\vdash V:B
is a closed value. Then:

(1)

if
\Gamma\vdash M:A
then
\Gamma\vdash M[V/x]:A

(2)

if
\Gamma,\,M:A\vdash\Delta
then
\Gamma,\,M[V/x]:A\vdash\Delta

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
M
. Note, in case (ii), there is always the possibility that
\Delta
has shape
z:\mathsf{Ok}
for some variable
z
and then the conclusion follows immediately from
(VarK)
. Moreover, if
M
does not contain
x
, then the conclusion is immediate. Hence, we will exclude these cases from consideration below.

•

If
M
is
x
, we reason as follows. In (i), by inversion, it must be that
A=\mathsf{Ok}
and the conclusion follows because all closed values are well-typed. In (ii), by inversion, it must be that
\Delta
is of shape
z:\mathsf{Ok}
as above.

•

If
M
is of shape
PQ
we reason as follows. In (i) by inversion it must be that there is some type
B
such that
\Gamma\vdash P:B\to A
and
\Gamma\vdash Q:B
. The conclusion follows from the induction hypotheses, part (i). In (ii) by inversion it must be that either (1) there is a type
B
such that
\Gamma\vdash P:B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
and
\Gamma,\,Q:B\vdash\Delta
, or (2)
\Gamma,\,P:\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A\vdash\Delta
. In the former case, the result follows from the induction hypotheses and
(AppL)
. In the latter case, the result follows from the induction hypothesis and
(FunK)
.

•

If
M
is of shape
c(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n})
we reason as follows. In (i), by inversion, it must be that there are types
B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}
and
\Gamma\vdash c(B_{1},\ldots,B_{n})\sqsubseteq A
and
\Gamma\vdash P_{i}:B_{i}
for each
i
. The result follows from the induction hypothesis,
(CnsR)
and
(SubR)
. In (ii), by inversion, it follows that either (1) there are types
B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}
and
i
such that
\Gamma\vdash A\sqsubseteq c(B_{1},\ldots,B_{n})
and
\Gamma,\,P_{i}:B_{i}\vdash\Delta
, or (2) there is an arrow type
A^{\prime}
such that
\Gamma\vdash A\sqsubseteq A^{\prime}
and
\Gamma,\,c(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}):A^{\prime}\vdash\Delta
. In the former case, the result follows from the induction hypotheses,
(CnsL)
and
(SubL)
. In the latter case, the result follows from
(CnsDL)
.

•

If
M
is of shape
\mathsf{fix}\,f(y).\,P
, we may assume that
y
and
f
do not occur outside of
P
. In (i), by inversion, it must be that there are types
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
such that, either: (1)
\Gamma,\,f:B_{1}\to B_{2},\,y:B_{1}\vdash P:B_{2}
and
\Gamma\vdash B_{1}\to B_{2}\sqsubseteq A
or (2)
\Gamma,\,f:B_{1}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B_{2},\,P:B_{2}\vdash y:B_{1}
and
\Gamma\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B_{2}\sqsubseteq A
. In (1), the result follows from the induction hypothesis,
(FixsR)
and
(SubR)
. In (2), the result follows from the induction hypothesis,
(FixnR)
and
(SubR)
. In (ii), by inversion, it must be that there is some sum type
A^{\prime}
such that
\Gamma\vdash A\sqsubseteq A^{\prime}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{fix}\,f(y).\,P:A^{\prime}\vdash\Delta
. Then the result follows from
(FixDL)
.

•

If
M
is of shape
\mathsf{match}\,Q\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}(p_{i}\mapsto P_{i})\}
, we reason as follows. In (i), it follows from inversion that there is a family of types
B_{y}
(for each pattern-bound variable
y
) and
\Gamma\vdash Q:\Sigma_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}[B_{y}/y\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})]
and
\Gamma\cup\{y:B_{y}\mid y\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})\}\vdash P_{i}:A
for each
i
. Then the conclusion follows from the induction hypothesis and
(MchR)
. In (ii), by inversion we have a family of types
B_{y}
(one for each pattern-bound variable
y
) such that
\Gamma,\,P_{i}:A\vdash y:B_{y}
(for each
i
and each
y\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})
) and either
\Gamma,\,Q:p_{i}[B_{y}/y\mid y\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})]\vdash\Delta
or
\Gamma,\,P_{i}:A\vdash\Delta
. In both cases, it follows from the induction hypothesis and
(CnsL)
that
\Gamma,\,(Q[V/x],P_{i}[V/x]):(p_{i}[B_{y}/y\mid y\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})],A)\vdash\Delta
. Then the conclusion follows from the induction hypothesis and
(MchL)
.

∎

Lemma E.13 (Substitution on the Left).

Assume
\Gamma
is a consistent identifier environment disjoint from
\Delta
and neither contains
x
. If
\Gamma,\,M:A\vdash x:B
and
\Gamma,\,V:B\vdash\Delta
then
\Gamma,\,M[V/x]:A\vdash\Delta
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
M
. Note, there is always the possibility that
B=\mathsf{Ok}
, in which case we have
\Gamma,\,V:\mathsf{Ok}\vdash\Delta
for closed value
V
and so it follows from inversion that
\Delta
must be of shape
z:\mathsf{Ok}
for some variable
z
. Hence, the desired conclusion follows in this case immediately from
(VarK)
.

•

If
M
is
x
, then it follows from inversion that
\Gamma\vdash A\sqsubseteq B
. Hence, the result follows from the assumed
\Gamma,\,V:B\vdash\Delta
and
(SubL)
, since
M[V/x]=V
.

•

If
M
is
f
, then it follows from inversion that
B
must be
\mathsf{Ok}
, and we have proven this case above.

•

If
M
is a variable
y
distinct from
x
, the reasoning is as in the previous case.

•

If
M
is an application
PQ
, then it follows from inversion that either (i) there is a type
B^{\prime}
and
\Gamma\vdash P:B^{\prime}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
and
\Gamma,\,Q:B^{\prime}\vdash x:B
, or (ii)
\Gamma,\,P:\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A\vdash x:B
. In (i), it follows from Lemma
E.12
that
\Gamma\vdash P[V/x]:B^{\prime}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
and it follows from the induction hypothesis that
\Gamma,\,Q[V/x]:B^{\prime}\vdash\Delta
. Therefore, the conclusion follows from
(AppL)
. In (ii), the result follows from the induction hypothesis and
(FunK)
.

•

If
M
is a fixpoint abstraction
\mathsf{fix}\,f(y).\,P
we may assume
y
and
f
are fresh for the context. Then it follows from inversion that there is some sum type
A^{\prime}
such that
\Gamma\vdash A\sqsubseteq A^{\prime}
and
\Gamma,\,\mathsf{fix}\,f(y).\,P:A^{\prime}\vdash x:B
. Then the result follows from
(FixDL)
.

•

If
M
is a constructor
c(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n})
then it follows from inversion that either (1) there are types
B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}
and
\Gamma\vdash A\sqsubseteq c(B_{1},\ldots,B_{n})
and
\Gamma,\,P_{i}:B_{i}\vdash x:B
for some
i
, or (2) there is some arrow type
A^{\prime}
such that
\Gamma\vdash A\sqsubseteq A^{\prime}
and
\Gamma,\,c(P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}):A^{\prime}\vdash x:B
. In case (1), the result follows from the induction hypothesis and
(CnsL)
and
(SubL)
. In case (2), the result follows immediately from
(CnsDL)
and
(SubL)
.

•

If
M
is a match expression
\mathsf{match}\,Q\,\mathsf{with}\,\{\mid_{i=1}^{k}(p_{i}\mapsto P_{i})\}
then it follows from inversion that there is some family
B_{y}
(for each pattern-bound variable
y
) and
\Gamma,\,P_{i}:A\vdash y:B_{y}
(for each
i
and
y\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})
) and either
\Gamma,\,Q:p_{i}[B_{y}/y\mid y\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})]\vdash x:B
or
\Gamma,\,P_{i}:A\vdash x:B
. In both cases, it follows from the induction hypothesis and
(CnsL)
that
\Gamma,\,(Q[V/x],P_{i}[V/x]):(p_{i}[B_{y}/y\mid y\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})],A)\vdash x:B
. Then the result follows from the induction hypothesis and
(MchL)
.

∎

Theorem E.14 (Preservation on the Right).

Suppose
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are disjoint, consistent variable environments with
\vdash\mathcal{M}:\Gamma
.
If
\Gamma\vdash M:A
and
M\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ N
then
\Gamma\vdash N:A
.

Proof.

We prove the result for all
\mathcal{E}
, redexes
P
and contractions
Q
such that
M=\mathcal{E}[P]\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ \mathcal{E}[Q]=N
, by induction on
\mathcal{E}
.

•

If the context is just a hole, then we proceed by cases on the redex.

(Fix
\beta
)
:

In this case,
P
has shape
(\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,P^{\prime})\,V
and
Q=P^{\prime}[V/x,\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,P^{\prime}/f]
. It follows from inversion that there is a type
B
such that
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,P^{\prime}:B\to A
and
\Gamma\vdash V:B
. By inversion and the consistency of
\Gamma
, we have some
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
such that
\Gamma,\,f:B_{1}\to B_{2},\,x:B_{1}\vdash P^{\prime}:B_{2}
and
\Gamma\vdash B_{1}\to B_{2}\sqsubseteq B\to A
(the other possibility would involve a type constraint that is not syntactically consistent). By the closedness of the constraints in
\Gamma
, we have
\Gamma\vdash B_{2}\sqsubseteq A
. Then it follows from the substitution lemma (twice) that
\Gamma\vdash P^{\prime}[V/x][\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,P^{\prime}/f]:B_{2}
and hence
P^{\prime}[V/x,\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,P^{\prime}/f]:A
by
(SubR)
.

(Delta)
:

In this case, it follows from inversion that there is some type
B
and family
\vv{B}
such that
f:\forall\vv{a}.\,D\Rightarrow B
and
\Gamma\vdash B[\vv{B/a}]\sqsubseteq A
and
\Gamma\vdash D[\vv{B/a}]
. Then it follows from
\vdash\mathcal{M}:\Gamma
that
\Gamma\cup D\vdash M:B
and therefore
\Gamma[\vv{B/a}]\cup D[\vv{B/a}]\vdash M:B[\vv{B/a}]
. However,
\Gamma
is guaranteed to be closed with respect to type variables, and so we have
\Gamma\cup D[\vv{B/a}]\vdash M:B[\vv{B/a}]
. Since also
\Gamma\vdash D[\vv{B/a}]
, it follows that
\Gamma\vdash M:B[\vv{B/a}]
, as required.

(Match)
:

In this case,
P
has shape
\mathsf{match}\,c(V_{1},\ldots,V_{n})\,\mathsf{with}\,\{|_{i=1}^{n}(p_{i}\mapsto P_{i})\}
and one of the alternatives is of shape
c(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\mapsto P
and
Q=P[V_{i}/x_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq n]
It follows from inversion that there is a family of types
B_{x}
(one for each pattern-bound variable
x
) such that
\Gamma\vdash c(V_{1},\ldots,V_{n}):\Sigma_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}[B_{x}/x\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})]
and
\Gamma\cup\{x:B_{x}\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})\}\vdash P:A
. Therefore, it follows by value inversion that
\Gamma\vdash V_{i}:B_{i}
. Moreover, it follows by induction on
n
and the substitution lemma (since the
x_{i}
are disjoint and the
V_{i}
closed) that
\Gamma\vdash P[V_{i}/x_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq n]:A
, as required.

•

If the context is of shape
\mathcal{E}\,M^{\prime}
then it follows by inversion that there is some
B
such that
\Gamma\vdash\mathcal{E}[P]:B\to A
and
\Gamma\vdash M^{\prime}:B
. Then it follows from the induction hypothesis that
\Gamma\vdash\mathcal{E}[Q]:B\to A
hence the result follows by
(AppR)
.

•

If the context is of shape
(\lambda x.\,M^{\prime})\,\mathcal{E}
then it follows by inversion that there is some
B
such that
\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.\,M^{\prime}:B\to A
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathcal{E}[P]:B
. Then the result follows from the induction hypothesis and
(AppR)
.

•

If the context is of shape
\mathsf{match}\,\mathcal{E}\,\mathsf{with}\,\{|_{i=1}^{k}(p_{i}\mapsto P_{i})\}
then it follows from inversion that there is some family
B_{x}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathcal{E}[P]:\Sigma_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}[B_{x}/x\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})]
for each
i
and
\Gamma\cup\{x:B_{x}\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})\}\vdash P_{i}:A
for all
i
. Then the result follows from the induction hypothesis and
(MchR)
.

∎

Theorem E.15 (Preservation on the Left).

Suppose
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are disjoint, consistent variable environments. If
M\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ N
and
\Gamma,\,M:A\vdash\Delta
then
\Gamma,\,N:A\vdash\Delta
.

Proof.

We prove the result for all
\mathcal{E}
, redexes
P
and contractions
Q
such that
M=\mathcal{E}[P]\,\ \mathord{\rhd}\,\ \mathcal{E}[Q]=N
, by induction on
\mathcal{E}
.

•

When the context is just a hole, we proceed to analyse the redex.

(Fix
\beta
)
:

In this case,
P
has shape
(\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,P^{\prime})\,V
and
Q=P^{\prime}[V/x,\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,P^{\prime}/f]
. It follows from inversion that either (1) there is a type
B
such that
\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.\,P^{\prime}:B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
and
\Gamma,\,V:B\vdash\Delta
, or (2)
\Gamma,\,\lambda x.\,P^{\prime}:\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A\vdash\Delta
. In case (1), it follows by inversion that there are types
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
such that
\Gamma,\,f:B_{1}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B_{2},\,P^{\prime}:B_{2}\vdash x:B_{1}
and
\Gamma\vdash B_{1}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}B_{2}\sqsubseteq B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
(the other possibility is excluded by the consistency of the constraints). Then it follows from the substitution lemma (twice) that
\Gamma,\,P^{\prime}[V/x,\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,P^{\prime}/f]:B_{2}\vdash\Delta
. By the closedness of the constraints,
\Gamma\vdash A\sqsubseteq B_{2}
and so the desired conclusion follows by
(SubL)
. By the Progress theorem, case (2) is impossible unless
\Delta
is of shape
z:\mathsf{Ok}
for some
z
. In this case, the conclusion follows immediately by
(VarK)
.

(Delta)
:

By inversion, these cases are impossible unless
\Delta
has shape
z:\mathsf{Ok}
for some variable
z
and then the conclusion follows immediately by
(VarK)
.

(Match)
:

In this case,
P
has shape
\mathsf{match}\,c(V_{1},\ldots,V_{n})\,\mathsf{with}\,\{|_{i=1}^{k}(p_{i}\mapsto P_{i})\}
and one of the alternatives has shape
c(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\mapsto P
and
Q=P[V_{i}/x_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq k]
. It follows from inversion that either
\Delta
is of shape
z:\mathsf{Ok}
, in which case the conclusion is immediate by
(VarK)
, or there are a family of types
B_{x}
, one for each pattern-bound variable
x
such that
\Gamma,\,P:A\vdash x:B_{x}
(for each
x\in\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
) and, either
\Gamma,\,c(V_{1},\ldots,V_{n}):c(B_{x_{1}},\ldots,B_{x_{n}})\vdash\Delta
or
\Gamma,\,P:A\vdash\Delta
. In the former case, it follows from inversion and the consistency of the constraints in
\Gamma
that there is
j
such that
\Gamma,\,V_{j}:B_{x_{j}}\vdash\Delta
. Then is follows from the substitution lemma that
\Gamma,\,P[V_{j}/x_{j}]\vdash\Delta
. Then it follows from the trivial substitution lemma, by induction on
n
, that
\Gamma,\,P[V_{i}/x_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq n]:A\vdash\Delta
as required.

•

When the context is of shape
\mathcal{E}\,M^{\prime}
, it follows from inversion that either (0)
\Delta
has shape
z:\mathsf{Ok}
for some variable
z
, (1) there is a type
B
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathcal{E}[P]:B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
and
\Gamma,\,M^{\prime}:B\vdash\Delta
, or (2)
\Gamma,\,\mathcal{E}[P]:\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A\vdash\Delta
. In case (0) the result is immediate by
(VarK)
. In case (1), we obtain
\Gamma\vdash\mathcal{E}[Q]:B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
by preservation on the right and the result follows by
(AppL)
. In case (2), we obtain the result from the induction hypothesis and
(FunK)
.

•

When the context has shape
(\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M^{\prime})\,\mathcal{E}
, it follows from inversion that either (0)
\Delta
has shape
z:\mathsf{Ok}
, or (1) there is a type
B
and
\Gamma\vdash\lambda f(x).\,M^{\prime}:B\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A
and
\Gamma,\,\mathcal{E}[P]:B\vdash\Delta
, or (2)
\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{fix}\,f(x).\,M^{\prime}:\mathsf{Ok}\mathrel{\text{\stixarr\char 179\relax}}A\vdash\Delta
. In case (0) the result is immediate by
(VarK)
. In case (2), the result follows by the induction hypothesis and
(AppL)
. In case (3), the result follows from the induction hypothesis and
(FunK)
.

•

When the context has shape
\mathsf{match}\,\mathcal{E}\,\mathsf{with}\,\{|_{i=1}^{k}(p_{i}\mapsto P_{i})\}
it follows from inversion that either
\Delta
has shape
z:\mathsf{Ok}
or there is a family of types
B_{x}
(one for each pattern-bound variable
x
) such that
\Gamma,\,P_{i}:A\vdash x:B_{x}
and, for each
i
, either
\Gamma,\,\mathcal{E}[P]:p_{i}[B_{x}/x\mid x\in\mathsf{FV}(p_{i})]\vdash\Delta
or
\Gamma,\,P_{i}:A\vdash\Delta
. Then the result follows from the induction hypothesis and
(MchL)
.

∎

Finally, we can prove Theorem
6.12
.

Proof.

Suppose
\Gamma\vdash M:\mathsf{Ok}
converges to a normal form
N
. By preservation,
\Gamma\vdash N:\mathsf{Ok}
and, by progress,
N
is a value. Suppose
\Gamma,\,M:\mathsf{Ok}\vdash
converges to a normal form
N
, then, by preservation,
\Gamma,\,N:\mathsf{Ok}\vdash
and, by progress,
\Gamma\not\vdash N:\mathsf{Ok}
. Since closed values are well typed,
N
is not a value.
∎
